The central bay of the main hall, here called the West Room, is both the public space of the inn and residence, and the ritual center of this family: the whole family eats here; guests come to rest and drink tea here; festivals and holidays hold ancestor worship here; relatives who pass away are mourned here; even pilgrims who cannot go up Fangyan for various reasons will set up an incense table here, facing Fangyan Mountain to worship Hu Gong.

Between the rear gold pillars of the West Room is the taishi wall; even in ordinary times, portraits of ancestors hang on the taishi wall. Due to functional needs, the West Room can usually be fully opened; if doors are installed, they are removable panel doors; behind the taishi wall is a storage room, entered through a side door.

The east and west rooms of the West Room are respectively called Upper Great Room and Lower Great Room; east is "upper," slightly higher in status, usually优先分给 the eldest son during family division, and the Lower Great Room to the second son.

The east wing rooms from north to south are respectively called Inner Wing, Outer Wing, Small West Room, Yuan Room and Passage Room. The west wing rooms face the street, serving as shop fronts, also called shop halls.

The two southern shop halls protrude several meters outward along the street; the northernmost one also forms a small overhanging building on the second floor, spanning the old street.

Unfortunately, these two rooms were sold by Cheng Zhaofu; the remaining three from south to north are respectively called Upper shop Hall, Middle shop Hall and Lower shop Hall, among which the Lower shop Hall bay is the entrance to the courtyard and inn.

The three reverse-side rooms from east to west are respectively East Facing Hall, Facing Hall and West Facing Hall. The Facing Hall has smaller depth, so it is the only side in the courtyard, on both first and second floors, without an eaves corridor.

The mingtang is 12 meters wide and about 7.5 meters deep, i.e. the width of three main hall bays and two wing room bays. The mingtang is paved with cobblestones, the ground slightly higher in the middle and lower around, with drainage outlets at the four corners, connected to the drainage ditches on both sides of the street.

In the middle of the courtyard there is also an "in-house well" representing wealth and status. The well water is clear and sweet, and至今 is still the main source of drinking water for Cheng Zhenkang's family.

Passing through the passage room at the southeast corner of the courtyard, there is a three-bay-wide storage room and bathroom; exiting the north door of the storage room is the rear courtyard. In the rear courtyard there is a small pond, about half a room in size, formerly used for washing chamber pots, now used for washing farm tools.

Against the hillside next to the storage room, there is also a small vegetable plot.

The position north of the courtyard facing the street was originally a large kitchen, because the kitchen not only needed to cook but also brew wine, and raise pigs, chickens, ducks, geese and other livestock.

In peak season, Cheng Zhaofu's family plus clerks would also live on the second floor of the kitchen, so as to vacate more rooms for pilgrims. After liberation, part of the kitchen was sold to outsiders; in the 1980s, the remaining kitchen collapsed, so the Small West Room was converted to a kitchen.

Changes

In the early operation of Cheng Chengchang Inn, guest rooms were very simple, mainly receiving pilgrims from Dongyang, Yiwu, Yongkang, Jinhua, Pujiang, Shengxian etc.

Their requirements were not high, and they could be satisfied with two people sharing one "bed." The so-called "bed" was a one-meter-plus-wide wooden board supported by two stools, with a thin layer of straw on top. During the August-September incense season, as long as one could sleep on a floor mat under a roof, guests were content.

In this situation, upstairs and downstairs could accommodate nearly 300 people. But as Hu Gong's fame grew, guests from big cities such as Shanghai and Shaoxing also increased, and they raised higher requirements for the inn's food and facilities.

Cheng Chengchang ultimately could not compete with large inns like Cheng Zhenxing in this regard, so it could only receive "medium" or "lower" grade pilgrims.

In the 1940s, during the years when the Zhejiang Provincial Nationalist Government was stationed at Fangyan, like most other inns, Cheng Chengchang almost ceased operation. Although there were no pilgrims, Cheng Chengchang became busier.

Those coming and going were civil servants working in the Civil Affairs Department and their families; the shop hall was also rented out as a printing factory.

These civil servants had higher requirements for living conditions; for this Cheng Chengchang also carried out a series of renovations, including laying wooden floors on the ground floor for moisture protection; enlarging the windows on the exterior walls for lighting; and for safety reasons, adding a staircase at the passage room position.

After the Anti-Japanese War ended, Cheng Chengchang resumed business, and the inn business prospered for several more years. From 1949 to 1980, the inn opened and closed several times. During 1958-1960, it also served successively as a nursing home, Zhiying People's Commune Umbrella Factory and Yanxia Brigade Umbrella Factory.

Fortunately, because the family had many people, the house was not divided during "Land Reform," which is also an important reason why this courtyard has been well preserved to this day.

In August 1980, the inn resumed business again; with the gradual improvement of business, Cheng Zhenyi moved the inn to a modern small building next to the new street in 1991, renaming it "New Cheng Chengchang."

Cheng Renchang Inn, Cheng Zhenxing Inn, Cheng Keng Villa — Chinese-Western Combined Architecture of Mid-Twentieth Century

In the mid-twentieth century, several Chinese-Western combined buildings were successively completed on the old street. Among them, two分别 belong to parts of Cheng Renchang Inn and Cheng Zhenxing Inn, and the other is called Cheng Keng Villa.

This Chinese-Western combined building belonging to Cheng Renchang Inn is located at the west foot of Lion Mountain, west of the old street, outside the north wall of Cheng Renchang Old shop.

This was originally a four-story wooden structure standalone building; its building materials, construction methods and decorative motifs all follow the characteristics of local traditional architecture, and the influence of Western architecture is mainly reflected in spatial layout.

According to the recollection of elderly Cheng Zhenlong, a descendant of Cheng Renchang Inn, when building the house, Cheng Zhaoye originally wanted to build a traditional four-sided courtyard, but on the suggestion of Engineer Pan changed it to a standalone mansion.

Unlike other courtyard buildings on the old street, this building of Cheng Renchang Inn has its courtyard紧挨着 the old street; the nearly square-plan three-bay building sits on the west side of the courtyard, with a front porch transitioning between rooms and courtyard.

The courtyard ground level is the same as the valley; the entrance on the old street is equivalent to second-floor height; after entering, one reaches the first floor through steps on both sides. Another difference in spatial layout is reflected in section.

The second floor of courtyard houses on the old street originally did not accommodate people, only for storage, so the second-floor railing practice was just arranging several wooden boards vertically in the frame.

Compared with Western architecture, high-rise spaces have always been the main living space, so railings naturally become the focus of decoration. After the rise of the inn industry, second-floor spaces became important living spaces during incense seasons; buildings built after the mid-twentieth century more or less decorated the railings.

Each floor of this building has twenty-nine exquisitely carved railing panels. In the mid-1930s, Cheng Zhaoye's eldest son Cheng Xuanhu inherited this building and a small courtyard behind it, renaming the shop Xin Renchang.

Cheng Xuanhu had three sons; in early liberation due to poor economic conditions, he was forced to sell the third and fourth floors of the building.

Cheng Keng Villa is located at the entrance of Fangyan Mountain, south of the old street. Cheng Keng (1895-1968), according to genealogy records named Cheng Xuankeng, was the eldest son of Cheng Zhaochun.

According to the recollection of elderly Cheng Zhenfeng (born 1939, Chong generation, 21st generation of Cheng), grandson of Cheng Zhaochun, Cheng Zhaochun (born 1864, death year unknown) was the actual builder of this villa; after his death, the villa was jointly owned by the eldest and second sons, and the third son, i.e.

his father, inherited the old house. Cheng Keng, as a locally somewhat famous painter in the early twentieth century, left home young to develop in Shanghai, and settled in Yongkang after the "August 13" Incident.

Although he may never have lived here and did not own the entire building, people still call this building Cheng Keng Villa because of his fame. Cheng Keng Villa is also a standalone wooden structure building; compared with Cheng Renchang Inn, the influence of Western architecture on spatial layout is more significant here.

The entire building sits on a one-meter-plus-high stone platform, facing east, surrounded by courtyard space on all sides. The plan is three bays, composed of six nearly square spaces, with corridors on all four sides.

The front and rear central bays are open or semi-open halls; the four north-south rooms are for living, with doors opening to the south and north corridors.

One of the greatest advantages of this layout is that each bedroom has windows in two directions, conducive to ventilation; no wonder Cheng Keng's descendants living here emphasize "this house is very cool." Like Cheng Renchang Inn, the second-floor railing of Cheng Keng Villa also has exquisite openwork wood carvings.

Among the many buildings owned by Cheng Zhenxing Inn, there are two connected Western-style buildings. The northern one was built by Cheng Zhaofu in the 1930s; it is a two-story six-bay brick-stone structure building. Its north side connects to the south wall of Cheng Zhaofu ancestral hall, and the east wall is紧挨着 the old street.

From the outside, almost no trace of local architecture can be seen; replaced by common Western architectural features such as brick pilasters, round small windows and wide bright glass windows. The southern row was built in 1942, commissioned by Cheng Zhaofu's sons Cheng Xuanmei and Cheng Xuanji from a Hangzhou construction factory.

This three-story seven-bay Western building is actually still a wooden structure; all rooms face south and are ventilated north-south, with rooms on each floor connected by corridors and open to the south-facing inner courtyard.

Western architectural elements are mainly reflected in the detailed treatment of the envelope brick walls, such as brick-arched windows and Western-style gatehouses. In the mid-1940s, Cheng Zhenxing was divided into two; Cheng Xuanmei's son Cheng Chongxue inherited these two buildings, renaming the shop Xin Zhenxing.

In early liberation, due to economic reasons, one floor of the second Western building was sold, now only two floors remain.

本篇术语 Key Terms

住宅 / Residence (zhuzhai) 得耕居 / Degeng Residence 程立重宅院 / Cheng Lizhong Mansion 花厅 / Flower Hall (huating) 太师壁 / Taishi Wall (screen wall) 戏台 / Stage (xitai) 日月井 / Sun-Moon Well (pavilion over pond) 后堂 / Rear Hall (houtang) 重檐楼牌式门楼 / Double-eave gatehouse 玉带环腰 / Jade Belt Encircling the Waist (fengshui) 背山面水 / Backing onto mountains facing water 靠山/祖山/案山 / Backing mountain/ancestral mountain/table mountain 屋里塘/屋里井 / In-house pond/in-house well 八尺路 / Eight-Foot Road (alley) 月梁 / Crescent beam (yue liang) ...等46个
3
宗祠
中文

三、宗祠

在农耕社会里，人们无论是盖房、修路，或是开沟渠、整田亩，还是抵抗各种外来的天灾人祸，都不是一个人或是一个家庭可以独立完成的，而是需要许多人的相互扶持和共同努力。在中国几千年的父系社会里，建立在血缘和亲情基础之上的父系家族就很自然地承担起了这个责任。于是，聚族而居和血缘村落就自然而然地成为一种普遍的现象。这种客观现象的存在，以及历代中国社会都尊崇"孝道"的伦理准则，便为宗祠的产生提供了前提条件。纵观中国历史，北方长年战乱并且人口流失严重，相比之下南方相对安定，更易于血缘村落的形成和发展。

所以在江浙地区，特别是像岩下老街这样，地处丘陵地带、交通不发达的村落，一般都保留有较强的宗族意识和相对完整的世族体系。

宗祠的基本功能在于，它是一个供奉和祭拜祖先的仪式性场所。这里的祠堂里都供奉着历代先人的牌位；逢年过节和祭祀时，还要挂上本房祖宗的画像。有的宗祠里还设有谱房以便管理宗族人口的派系、行辈、婚姻等问题。祠堂同时也是商议族内大事、调解族人矛盾、旌表功名、办学堂、存放公共器物的场所。在大一点儿的祠堂里还有戏台，在特殊的日子里，宗祠便暂时放下它的神圣地位，而成为全村的文化娱乐中心，甚至还允许族人在这里赌博娱乐。

以上这些都体现了宗祠存在的意义和重要性，即通过一系列祖先崇拜的仪式及活动来加强程氏家族的内部凝聚力。"加强宗族的内聚力，就是加强人际合作的社会单元的能量，这是人们生存并发展的最重要依靠，从而促成了祖先崇拜，也便是血缘认同。"

虽然橙麓村和岩下村供奉的是同一始祖程锴，但是老街旁曾先后建造了大大小小至少八座程氏各房派的祠堂。它们在村里都是最重要，通常也是建造最精美的建筑。在江浙地区的多数村落中，祠堂通常会位于村落相对中心的地带，其位置和朝向多少都会影响到村落的布局。但是老街的发展由于受到地形的限制，所以这里的祠堂大多都沿街坐落，并没能对村落的空间序列起到决定性的影响。即便如此，在用地紧张的情况下，祠堂前通常都有水塘或空地，这个空间依旧是村落生活的舞台。

程氏宗祠

程氏宗祠始建于明正德己亥年（1515年），位于橙麓村象鼻岗山脚。它是迁居这里的程氏所建的第一个祠堂，也是岩下老街众多祠堂中唯一被收入县志的，"三十五都程宗祠：始祖闰二闰三"。祠堂坐西朝东，东侧是螺蛳塘山，山上建有三世祖程权之墓。在嘉靖年间，祠堂曾改建为前后两进；在道光丙午年重修成五开间，前后三进院落，并且用石柱代替已经糟朽的木柱。据当地人回忆：整个祠堂宽二十多米，进深近五十米；有牌门一座，享堂五间，寝室九间，还有厢房位于南北两侧。

在抗日战争时期，中共浙江省委书记刘英被关在程氏宗祠内，并于1942年5月13日在祠堂内遇害。1992年1月21日，程氏宗祠随着刘英烈士陵园的开工而被拆毁。在当地程氏后人的努力下，部分石柱、柱础和门楣等建筑构件被送到文楼村保存，以便日后重建。居住在橙麓村的程家麟老人还花钱买下了一些柱础，这些柱础现在还依然摆放在他家的院墙外。还有一些柱础上面架上了石条，成了村里人小憩和唠家常的地方。

在橙麓村的水塘边和小路旁，还能看到许多似乎曾被用作为台明石的大块石料；水塘里的一块洗衣石上还有两个刚劲的楷体大字"之墓"。老人们已经不记得这块石头的来历，更无法寻找另一半墓碑的下落。这些点点滴滴的痕迹，都记录着那幢曾经矗立在村口长达五百年之久的建筑的辉煌。

世远公祠及作勳公祠

背景

世远公祠，俗称小祠堂，是为纪念程坦（1411—1477年）而建造的。程坦字世远，为永康程氏六世祖。作勳公指的是十三世祖程际烈（1646—1725年）。作勳公祠又叫友恭常祠，友常和恭常分别代表作勳公的两个儿子程伊臣（1691—1765年）和程经臣（1697—1771年）的房派。

村里的老人们已经不记得这两座祠堂具体的建造年代了，但是他们大多都很肯定一点，那就是虽然世远公比作勳公早出生二百多年，但是作勳公祠建在世远公祠之前，并且它们都比不远处的绍常祠堂要早。从两个祠堂的相互关系上看，世远公祠位于作勳公祠前，并挡住了其正门，也从侧面支持了世远公祠建造较晚的说法。现住在作勳公祠里的程茂发（1934年生，崇字辈，程氏21代）老人讲，作勳公祠是程伊臣和程经臣为其父亲共同建造的，所以又叫友恭常祠；而世远公祠是作勳公的孙子建造的。

这种说法听起来也不无道理，在时间上也是成立的，即作勳公祠建于十八世纪中叶，世远公祠可能在随后的一二十年后。而曾参加过抗美援朝的程龙兴（1930年生，圣字辈，程氏22代）则振振有词地说，世远公祠是在明嘉靖年间（1522—1566年），由世远公的孙子建造的。家谱中记载，世远公的长孙生活的年代为1494—1568年，所以这在时间上也是可能的。

这两座祠堂的先后建成，标志着岩下村已经从一个杂姓聚居的村落，转变成了一个程氏的血缘村落。之所以人们为作勳公建造祠堂，不仅是因为程际烈家境富足，更是因为他"资雄里闬，每遇岁歉必出粟赈饥，凡有求者缓靡不相济。人皆德之"。不仅如此，程际烈的两个儿子也广受乡亲的尊敬。家谱记载其长子伊臣"文艺精通"，并且独修宗谱；次子经臣"善承家业"。为世远公建造祠堂则意义更为重大，岩下村的程氏一直奉世远公为始祖，村里新修好的家谱就叫《世远公宗谱》。究其原因为，虽然三世祖程权是岩下村的始迁祖，但是他的子孙除程坦外，即世远公，均外迁他乡。自六世祖程坦以后，这支程氏才世代留居在了岩下村。

环境

这两座祠堂位于上坑口外，老街以西。方岩溪在它们的西边，紧贴着世远公祠的后墙绕了一个半圆后，继续向北。这两幢建筑的轴线几乎相互垂直，世远公祠朝东南，作勳公祠朝西南。外墙之间只有一条一米多宽的小道，叫世远巷。紧贴着世远公祠的东墙和部分南墙，有一个长方形的水池，叫源流塘。水塘本身与世远公祠或作勳公祠之间不存在诸如轴线、对称或呼应等空间关系，却像是世远公祠"吃"掉了水塘的一个角。所以世远公祠也不能像其他祠堂一样，把入口冠冕堂皇地设在正中，而是设在了东北角上。

即便如此，还是有一部分入口，落在了水塘边界以内。世远公祠的另外一个入口，也是紧贴着水塘，位于侧立面的东南角上。次入口门外不远处，方岩溪缓缓流过，祠堂入口几乎正对着溪上的一座小桥。这是一座两侧有斜撑的平板石桥，叫望屏桥。过桥不远，便是绍常祠堂。

这组祠堂建筑群，没有一般宗祠建筑所具有的那种威严和霸气，反倒是十分生动而亲切。其灵活与不对称的空间构成，不禁让人想起著名的圣马可广场。祠堂建筑和老街之间有一个院落的距离，这样极好地回避了香客们的喧嚣。走在老街上，通过两座院落之间的"世远巷"，也可以隐约看到祠堂和水塘的一角。这两个祠堂，以及源流塘所围合的空间，虽然不大，却是村里重要的公共空间之一。经常可以看到附近的村民来这里浣洗衣物；到了吃饭的时间就会有人坐在水塘边的石板上吃饭；也会有人在水塘和作勳公祠之间不大的空地上晒晒干柴。

世远公祠

按村里的说法，世远公祠是全村人都有份的祠堂。它的平面布局及构造，代表这个地区的一种普遍形式。祠堂长约26米，宽约17米，在横向上有五个开间。中间三个开间纵向全部开敞，直接暴露抬梁式屋架，从前到后分别是前厅，天井，正堂，戏台及左右两个小天井，最后是享堂。侧面是穿斗结构的厢房，并有夹层。除了前厅里面的十根木柱外，其余的柱子均为石柱。三种不同尺寸的柱子与五种规格各异的柱础之间的不同搭配，间接界定了空间的主次。

最重要的空间当然是正堂，正堂位于三个天井之间，有八根圆柱并辅以有覆盆的巨型柱础。正堂的堂号依旧取三世祖程权"终身得耕陇亩足矣"中的"得耕"二字，为"得耕堂"。厢房和享堂内的柱子均为方柱，但是只有正堂两侧的柱子和享堂中间的四根，才配以一种规格较高的方形柱础；余下的只是采用很简单的，大小和柱子截面相当的四面倒锥形柱础。前厅中间面向正堂的两根檐柱采用的是石圆柱，辅以鼓形柱础。前厅内余下的柱子皆为较细的木柱，柱础也采用规格更小的鼓形柱础。

正堂两个前金柱内侧分别写有"六世衍宗祧有典有则，千秋隆孝享是训是行"，后金柱上的对联则在前面，为"后嗣相承祖德与方山并寿，前徽弗替孙谋偕丽水俱长"。"得耕堂"的匾也挂在后金柱之间，略向下倾斜。很显然，这两副对联和横匾共同"指向"的地方，就是程氏子孙在祠堂里双膝碰地的地方。

享堂宽五开间，两侧没有厢房，它的地面比正堂高四十公分。享堂后面原本是按照左昭右穆摆放牌位的地方，虽然这些木构的台面、神龛现已全部缺失，但是从柱子侧面的榫口可以推断出它们的位置和高度。以前老街上如果有程氏男丁过世，家人就要做一个牌位送到世远公祠里来；如果是友恭常下的后人，还要做一个送到作勳公祠；如果又是恭下毅常的，则要做第三个送到绍常祠堂；对于已经结婚的男丁，则要做夫妻牌位。如果当时妻子还在世，则用一块红布把妻子的牌位暂时罩起来；如果妻子先走一步，则要等到丈夫去世后一起进祠堂。

正堂两侧的厢房主要用于储藏一些祭祀用品。二层的夹层则是堆放一些大型的器物，例如每年舞龙灯时用的龙头和龙尾。祠堂南墙外还有一个不规则的小屋，平时是教书先生办公及居住的地方，在过年和清明的时候用做灶堂。

作勳公祠

作勳公祠的建筑规格则小许多，简单地说就是一个三合院。正厅为三开间，抬梁式结构，尺度和世远公祠正堂的尺寸十分接近。东西厢房各三间，为穿斗式结构，靠南的两间有夹层。厢房的首间和正厅围合形成了一个进深约三米的内院。

自从1958年"生产大跃进"后，作勳公的房产由四家人共有。据住在西厢房的程茂发（1934年生，崇字辈，程氏21代）老人讲，他的继父程宣占由于很穷，在1946年便分到了作勳公祠的一部分房产，他和继父，以及生父一家人就住了进来。在这以前由于家里穷没有房，也会到祠堂里租房子住。之后，又有三家人买房。由于居住的需要，作勳公祠如今被划分出了许多小的隔间。但是据程茂发老人讲，以前祠堂里的正房都是开敞的，现在各家用来当作门窗的隔板就是原来正堂里排位前面的门板拆下来的；祠堂里以前有一块写着"贡元"的匾，不幸在"文革"期间被损坏。

绍常祠堂

据说绍常祠堂是程立重（1736—1793年）为报答丈人在"岩脚竖花厅"的赠银之恩，于乾隆五十三年（1788年）建造的。程立重字逢时，号毅庵，为作勳公之孙，程经臣之子。程经臣的四个儿子各以"发"、"强"、"刚"、"毅"为房派，所以程立重的后代又被称为"恭下毅常"。由于程立重排行最小，所以"毅常"又被俗称为"小常"。永康话"小"与"绍"同音，所以得名为绍常祠堂。

环境

绍常祠堂坐北朝南，位于方岩溪以西，背后斜对着方岩山。在建造绍常祠堂以前，这可能只是一片田地。在绍常祠堂建成以后的很长一段时间里，都是孤零零地独自坐落在方岩溪的这一侧。

祠堂前面有一块较大的空场，这里既是秋收后村里人晒谷子的草场，又是正月里迎龙灯的高潮所在。空场的南面还有一幢几十年前建的电影院，如今家家都有了电视，电影院便冷清了。空场的东缘由方岩溪界定，溪水东岸就是程立重丈人出资修建的，有屋里塘、屋里井，有花园、菜地的大宅院。在空场的南、北两端各有一个小桥横跨方岩溪通往宅院。从绍常祠堂向北不远，还可以通过望屏桥走到世远公祠和作勳公祠。

建筑

绍常祠堂的平面布局和世远公祠基本相似，正堂和享堂的尺寸也相当，但是总面积却是世远公祠的两倍。与世远公祠相比，绍常祠堂在平面上的主要区别体现在如下几点：前厅和厢房进深较深，厢房和轴线上的主要空间之间局部有走廊，三个主要天井面积都比较大，在正堂两侧的厢房之间还各有一个有楼梯的小天井。

祠堂的正立面左右两端为高低错落的马头墙，主入口在中间，两边各有一个次入口连接厢房和前厅的走廊。除此之外，在这青砖墙上还高低错落地安插了几个方形和圆形的小窗，主要用来给尽端厢房和前厅次间通风。

前厅明间通高，次间二层的空间与两侧厢房相连。明间用月梁，前后单步梁，中间双步梁上又立童柱，上层采用穿斗结构。穿过前厅是和前厅同宽的明堂，明堂四面有廊，有十二根檐柱环绕。每一根檐柱上方均有一个雕着卷龙、鸟兽或花木的牛腿，牛腿上面还有一组精美的叠斗，用以支撑上面的挑檐枋。叠斗外侧均有镂雕插叶，内侧为盘龙形蟺梁。

正堂三间梁架全部露明，为九檩进深。明间有四柱，采用前后双步梁加中间五架梁；次间用五柱，穿斗与抬梁相结合，柱间有四组双步梁。正堂整个屋顶的重量依次通过檩枋、叠斗、大斗、大斗下的花墩、月梁、梁托，最后传到柱子上。在支撑檩枋的叠斗和柱子之间，还用蟺梁做连接，蟺梁不但有极强的装饰效果，还能起到在纵向上稳定结构的作用。除了蟺梁以外，叠斗、雀替、花墩、梁头和梁托都是绍常祠堂大木结构雕饰的重点。此外，在两根上金檩、前下金檩、檐檩和挑檐枋底面还有精美的镂空浮雕花鸟鱼纹。

正堂后面就是一个平面为方形的戏台，戏台两侧各有一个近方形的天井。从这里开始，厢房的进深变浅，从而使轴线上的主要空间可以拓宽成五开间。

享堂五开间，位于一个两步高的台基上。享堂的梁架结构和正堂大体类似。不同的是前后的双步梁变成了单步梁；后面的单步梁外面又增加了穿枋结构的一个廊，作为摆放神橱的空间；明间前面由于需要和戏台衔接，所以对梁架做了一些变化。享堂中间三开间在金柱间设门，但是可以全部开敞。据老人们讲，门上曾雕有精美的二十四孝，用以教育逢年过节来这里祭拜的程氏子孙。享堂的大木雕饰比正堂的稍简洁，在檩下叠斗之间没有用蟺梁，只是在叠斗两侧饰卷草形插叶。

东西厢房自南向北分别被分隔成大小不同的五个空间。中间三间各自朝向不同的天井开窗采光，尽端的两间则利用外墙开窗通风。厢房与前厅、一进天井、正堂之间均有走廊，在正堂侧壁后面的厢房之间还各有一个小天井，天井一侧各有一个楼梯通往二层。

周氏宗祠

周氏宗祠位于狮子山西麓的一个小山坳里，坐东朝西，紧邻老街。祠堂的位置是这段老街地势最高的部分，它和方岩山之间没有任何建筑物遮挡，有与方岩对视、俯瞰山谷的感觉。

周氏宗祠是老街上唯一一座非程姓的祠堂。据《游川周氏宗谱》记载，这支周氏被称为"草墅周氏"。其始祖时文公（1254—1315年）在元至元十七年至二十八年（1280—1291年）期间任两浙提举，因"奉敕兼管督理台温分司盐课，秩满道经永康，爱其风俗之美"，所以从金华泮塘迁居永康升平乡草墅，由此得名为"草墅周氏"。自此，时文公的子孙便从草墅迁往永康各地。

周氏宗祠建在这里是因为始祖周时文公墓位于它后面不远处，一个叫公道山"狮子撰屙"的洞穴里。据说周姓人家在老街上只居住了很短的时间，并在程氏迁到这里之前就离开了；也有人说周氏从未在这里居住过，只是在清明的前十天会有上百人来这里祭奠周氏始祖。《游川周氏宗谱》中记载这里原来只有"平厅三楹，厅前又有庵屋数间"，用以在清明时给远道来祭祖的子孙提供休息场所。后来由游川、岘川、璋川三宗扩建成现在的规模，现存建筑为光绪三十四年（1908年）重修。在国民党浙江省政府迁驻方岩的时候，这里曾作为浙江省警察大队的办公场所，其中还有一个小型的监狱。

周氏宗祠是一个接近方形的四合院。临街一面两侧是马头墙，白色的墙头上曾绘有彩画，但是如今只能依稀看见斑驳的色彩。正门居中，两旁各有一个偏门通向院内走廊。一进为三开间、七檩进深的拜厅，这是整幢建筑中雕饰最为精美的地方；天井后面，高台之上为五开间、进深略浅的享堂；两侧各有五间厢房供前来祭祀者暂住。不仅如此，"祠外两旁小厅，并楼屋八间，为看守者安身之宅也"。这"小厅"指的是周氏宗祠两边，跨过老街架设的路亭，这两个路亭是老街上为数不多的既能避风雨，又能小憩的地方。

"楼屋八间"现在只有三间归周氏所有，守墓人周岩好夫妇及其大嫂现居住在这里。家谱中记载，祠墓应由游川、岘川、璋川三宗轮流派可靠能干的子孙驻守，而"岩好公为游川小四房子孙，自其曾祖起住守始祖公祠墓至今已逾百年"。

其他祠堂

闰二宗祠位于橙麓村东侧，始建于明代。从建筑布局上看，应该和世远公祠类似，即三进正堂、品字形天井布局。基于"土改"时期的政策，闰二宗祠属于五代以上的祠堂，被归为国有。于是它先是作为小学校，又在1988年改作橙麓村老年协会的活动场所。2006年，祠堂内部被修葺一新，地面和部分墙面用混凝土重做，中部的大跨度屋顶也改用钢桁架结构。

程昭夫祠堂位于狮子山西麓，老街西侧。由程昭夫建于1924年，为程振兴旅店的一部分。祠堂形制和世远公祠基本相同，平面尺寸略大。祠堂在香汛时期也做客房使用，中间开敞空间不仅作为旅店的厅堂来招待香客、设摆晚宴，晚上还会请艺人在这里唱戏。浙江省国民政府临时迁驻方岩那段时间，这里还是承办政府会议、各式筵席甚至举办婚礼的场所。

启文公祠位于回龙桥西侧，其建造者为程振兴旅店老板的弟弟程昭纯。程昭纯（1864生，卒年不详），字启文，为程乐涞五子，并于1900年在老街南端开办了程成兴雨伞。据程昭纯孙程振锋（1939年生，崇字辈，程氏21代）老人讲，祠堂布局原本和世远公祠类似。现在可惜只剩下了中间的三开间。

老街上还有一个几乎已经被人们忘记的祠堂，这就是位于下胡坑坑口靠西的禄房祠堂。据程振锋老人回忆，禄房祠堂是其祖父程昭纯主持修建的，并作为祠堂管事。祠堂的规模比世远公祠略小，只有前后两进院落。这样算来，祠堂大约建于二十世纪初。不幸的是在"文革"期间，由于疏于维护，这座祠堂最终毁于白蚁和火灾。目前仅存一些残垣断壁，连基本的平面布局也无法辨认。

English

3. ancestral halls

In an agrarian society, whether building houses, repairing roads, digging irrigation channels, tending fields, or resisting various natural and man-made disasters, none of these could be accomplished independently by one person or one family; they required the mutual support and joint efforts of many people.

In China's thousands of years of patrilineal society, the patrilineal clan built on blood ties and kinship naturally assumed this responsibility. Thus, living together as a clan and blood-line villages naturally became a widespread phenomenon.

The existence of this objective phenomenon, plus the ethical principle of "filial piety" revered throughout Chinese history, provided the preconditions for the emergence of ancestral halls.

Looking at Chinese history, the north experienced long years of warfare and severe population loss; in comparison, the south was relatively stable, more conducive to the formation and development of blood-line villages.

Therefore in the Jiangsu-Zhejiang region, especially in villages like Yanxia Old Street located in hilly terrain with poor transportation, strong clan consciousness and relatively complete clan systems are generally preserved.

The basic function of an ancestral hall is that it is a ritual place for enshrining and worshipping ancestors. The halls here all enshrine the ancestral tablets of successive generations; during festivals and sacrifices, portraits of the branch ancestors are also hung.

Some ancestral halls also have genealogy rooms to manage clan population lineage, generation ranks, marriages and other issues. The hall is simultaneously a place for discussing major clan affairs, mediating clan disputes, commending academic achievements, running schools, and storing public artifacts.

In larger halls there is also a stage; on special days, the ancestral hall temporarily sets aside its sacred status and becomes the village's cultural and entertainment center, even allowing clan members to gamble here.

All these reflect the significance and importance of the ancestral hall's existence, namely strengthening the internal cohesion of the Cheng clan through a series of ancestor-worship rituals and activities.

"Strengthening clan cohesion is strengthening the energy of the social unit of interpersonal cooperation; this is the most important reliance for people's survival and development, thereby促成 ancestor worship, which is blood-line identity."

Although Chengli village and Yanxia village worship the same first ancestor Cheng Kai, at least eight ancestral halls of various Cheng branch lineages were successively built beside the old street, large and small. They are the most important, and usually the most exquisitely built, buildings in the village.

In most villages in the Jiangsu-Zhejiang region, ancestral halls are usually located in relatively central areas of the village, and their position and orientation more or less influence the village layout.

But because the old street's development was constrained by terrain, the halls here are mostly located along the street, and did not play a decisive role in the village's spatial sequence. Even so, under conditions of tight land use, there is usually a pond or open space in front of the hall, and this space remains a stage for village life.

Cheng clan ancestral hall

The Cheng clan ancestral hall was first built in the Jihai year of Ming Zhengde (1515), located at the foot of Xiangbi Hill in Chengli village.

It was the first hall built by the Cheng clan after moving here, and is the only one among the many halls of Yanxia Old Street recorded in the county gazetteer: "Cheng ancestral hall of 35th District: first ancestors Run-er and Run-san." The hall faces east, with Luositang Mountain to its east, on which stands the tomb of the third-generation ancestor Cheng Quan.

During the Jiajing period, the hall was rebuilt into two front-rear courtyards; in the Bingwu year of Daoguang it was rebuilt into five bays, three front-rear courtyards, with stone pillars replacing the already decayed wooden pillars.

According to local recollection: the entire hall was over twenty meters wide and nearly fifty meters deep; with one memorial gate, five-bay worship hall, nine-bay dormitory, and wing rooms on the north and south sides.

During the Anti-Japanese War, Liu Ying, Secretary of the Zhejiang Provincial Committee of the Communist Party of China, was imprisoned in the Cheng clan ancestral hall and killed inside the hall on May 13, 1942. On January 21, 1992, the Cheng clan ancestral hall was demolished with the commencement of the Liu Ying Martyrs Cemetery.

Through the efforts of local Cheng descendants, some architectural components such as stone pillars, pillar bases and door lintels were sent to Wenlou village for preservation for future reconstruction. Elder Cheng Jialin living in Chengli village also spent money buying some pillar bases, which are still placed outside his courtyard wall.

Some pillar bases have stone slabs placed on top, becoming places for villagers to rest and chat. By the pond and along paths in Chengli village, one can still see many large stone blocks that seem to have been used as platform stones; on a laundry stone in the pond there are two vigorous regular-script characters "Zhi Mu" (Tomb of).

Elders no longer remember the origin of this stone, nor can they find the other half of the tombstone. These little traces all record the glory of that building that once stood at the village entrance for five hundred years.

Shiyuan Gong Hall and Zuoxun Gong Hall

Background

Shiyuan Gong Hall, commonly called the Small Hall, was built to commemorate Cheng Tan (1411-1477). Cheng Tan, courtesy name Shiyuan, was the sixth-generation ancestor of the Yongkang Cheng clan. Zuoxun Gong refers to the thirteenth-generation ancestor Cheng Jilie (1646-1725).

Zuoxun Gong Hall is also called Yougongchang Hall; Youchang and Gongchang respectively represent the branch lineages of Zuoxun Gong's two sons Cheng Yichen (1691-1765) and Cheng Jingchen (1697-1771).

village elders no longer remember the exact construction dates of these two halls, but they mostly affirm one point: although Shiyuan Gong was born over two hundred years earlier than Zuoxun Gong, Zuoxun Gong Hall was built before Shiyuan Gong Hall, and both are earlier than the nearby Shaochang ancestral hall.

From the relationship between the two halls, Shiyuan Gong Hall is located in front of Zuoxun Gong Hall and blocks its main entrance, which also indirectly supports the claim that Shiyuan Gong Hall was built later.

Elder Cheng Maofa (born 1934, Chong generation, 21st generation of Cheng), who now lives in Zuoxun Gong Hall, says Zuoxun Gong Hall was jointly built by Cheng Yichen and Cheng Jingchen for their father, hence also called Yougongchang Hall; while Shiyuan Gong Hall was built by Zuoxun Gong's grandson.

This说法 sounds reasonable and is chronologically plausible, i.e. Zuoxun Gong Hall built in the mid-eighteenth century, Shiyuan Gong Hall possibly ten to twenty years later.

Cheng Longxing (born 1930, Sheng generation, 22nd generation of Cheng), who participated in the Korean War, assertively says Shiyuan Gong Hall was built during the Ming Jiajing period (1522-1566) by Shiyuan Gong's grandson. Genealogy records that Shiyuan Gong's eldest grandson lived 1494-1568, so this is also chronologically possible.

The successive construction of these two halls marks that Yanxia village had transformed from a multi-surname settlement into a Cheng clan blood-line village.

The reason people built a hall for Zuoxun Gong is not only because Cheng Jilie's family was wealthy, but更是 because he "was prominent in the neighborhood, every year of famine he必定 gave grain to relieve hunger, anyone who sought help was never refused.

All people praised his virtue." Moreover, Cheng Jilie's two sons were also widely respected by villagers.

Genealogy records his eldest son Yichen "excelled in literature and arts," and alone revised the clan genealogy; second son Jingchen "was good at carrying on the family business." Building a hall for Shiyuan Gong has even greater significance: the Cheng clan of Yanxia village has always revered Shiyuan Gong as their first ancestor, and the newly revised genealogy in the village is called "Shiyuan Gong Genealogy." The reason is that although the third-generation ancestor Cheng Quan was the founding ancestor who moved to Yanxia village, all his descendants except Cheng Tan, i.e.

Shiyuan Gong, moved elsewhere. Only from the sixth-generation ancestor Cheng Tan onward did this Cheng branch世代 remain in Yanxia village.

Environment

These two halls are located outside Shang Ravine mouth, west of the old street. Fangyan Stream is to their west, tightly hugging the rear wall of Shiyuan Gong Hall in a semicircle before continuing north. The axes of these two buildings are almost perpendicular to each other: Shiyuan Gong Hall faces southeast, Zuoxun Gong Hall faces southwest.

Between the exterior walls there is only an over-one-meter-wide alley called Shiyuan Alley. Tight against the east wall and part of the south wall of Shiyuan Gong Hall, there is a rectangular pond called Yuanliu pond.

The pond itself has no spatial relationship such as axis, symmetry or echo with either Shiyuan Gong Hall or Zuoxun Gong Hall; rather it seems as if Shiyuan Gong Hall "ate" a corner of the pond. Therefore Shiyuan Gong Hall, unlike other halls, could not place its entrance grandly in the center, but set it at the northeast corner.

Even so, part of the entrance still falls within the pond boundary. Another entrance of Shiyuan Gong Hall is also tight against the pond, located at the southeast corner of the side facade. Not far outside the secondary entrance, Fangyan Stream flows slowly, and the hall entrance almost directly faces a small bridge over the stream.

This is a flat stone slab bridge with diagonal supports on both sides, called Wangping bridge. Not far across the bridge is Shaochang ancestral hall.

This group of ancestral hall buildings does not have the majesty and domineering quality of typical ancestral hall architecture; instead it is quite lively and亲切. Its flexible and asymmetrical spatial composition inevitably reminds one of the famous Piazza San Marco.

There is a courtyard's distance between the hall buildings and the old street, which excellently avoids the noise of pilgrims. Walking on the old street, through the "Shiyuan Alley" between the two courtyards, one can also faintly see a corner of the halls and pond.

The space enclosed by these two halls and Yuanliu pond, although not large, is one of the village's important public spaces. One can often see nearby villagers coming here to wash clothes; at mealtime someone will sit on the stone slabs by the pond to eat; and someone will dry firewood in the small open space between the pond and Zuoxun Gong Hall.

Shiyuan Gong Hall

According to village说法, Shiyuan Gong Hall is a hall shared by everyone in the village. Its planar layout and structure represent a common form in this region. The hall is about 26 meters long and 17 meters wide, with five bays horizontally.

The middle three bays are fully open longitudinally, directly exposing the post-and-lintel roof frame; from front to back they are respectively the front hall, skylight, main hall, stage with two small skylights on left and right, and finally the worship hall. The sides are wing rooms of column-and-tie structure, with mezzanines.

Except for the ten wooden pillars inside the front hall, all other pillars are stone pillars. Different combinations of three sizes of pillars and five specifications of pillar bases indirectly define the hierarchy of spaces.

The most important space is of course the main hall, located between three skylights, with eight cylindrical pillars supplemented by giant basin-shaped pillar bases.

The hall name still takes the two characters "Degeng" (Obtaining Plowing) from the third-generation ancestor Cheng Quan's words "content to plow fields for life," called "Degeng Hall." Pillars in the wing rooms and worship hall are all square pillars, but only the pillars on both sides of the main hall and the four middle pillars of the worship hall are paired with a higher-specification square pillar base; the rest use very simple four-sided inverted-cone pillar bases of similar size to the pillar cross-section.

The two eave pillars in the front hall facing the main hall use stone cylindrical pillars with drum-shaped pillar bases. The remaining pillars in the front hall are thinner wooden pillars with smaller drum-shaped pillar bases.

Inside the two front gold pillars of the main hall are respectively written "Six generations extend the ancestral temple with rules and principles; a thousand years prosper filial worship with training and conduct"; the couplet on the rear gold pillars is in front, reading "Descendants inherit ancestral virtue coeval with Fang Mountain; former glory continues grandsons' plans as enduring as Lishui River." The "Degeng Hall" plaque also hangs between the rear gold pillars, slightly tilted downward.

Obviously, the place these two couplets and horizontal plaque jointly "point to" is where Cheng descendants kneel with both knees touching the ground in the hall.

The worship hall is five bays wide, with no wing rooms on both sides, and its floor is forty centimeters higher than the main hall.

Behind the worship hall was originally the place for placing ancestral tablets according to the left-zhao right-mu system; although these wooden platforms and shrines are now all missing, their positions and heights can be inferred from mortises on the pillar sides.

Previously, if a Cheng male on the old street passed away, the family would make a tablet and send it to Shiyuan Gong Hall; if a descendant of Yougongchang, they would also make one to send to Zuoxun Gong Hall; if also of Gongxia Yichang, they would make a third to send to Shaochang ancestral hall; for married males, a husband-and-wife tablet would be made.

If the wife was still alive at the time, a red cloth would temporarily cover the wife's tablet; if the wife passed away first, it would wait until the husband's death to enter the hall together.

The wing rooms on both sides of the main hall are mainly used for storing sacrificial supplies. The second-floor mezzanine stores large artifacts, such as the dragon head and tail used for the annual dragon lantern dance.

Outside the south wall of the hall there is also an irregular small room, usually the office and residence of the schoolteacher, used as a kitchen during New Year and Qingming festivals.

Zuoxun Gong Hall

Zuoxun Gong Hall's architectural规格 is much smaller; simply put it is a three-sided courtyard. The main hall is three bays, post-and-lintel structure, with dimensions very close to the main hall of Shiyuan Gong Hall. The east and west wing rooms are three bays each, column-and-tie structure, with the two southern rooms having mezzanines.

The first bay of the wing rooms and the main hall enclose an inner courtyard about three meters deep.

Since the "Great Leap Forward" in 1958, Zuoxun Gong's property has been jointly owned by four families.

According to elderly Cheng Maofa (born 1934, Chong generation, 21st generation of Cheng) living in the west wing room, his stepfather Cheng Xuanzhan, being very poor, was allocated part of Zuoxun Gong Hall's property in 1946, and he, his stepfather and biological father's family moved in.

Before that, because the family was poor and had no house, they would also rent rooms in the hall. Later, three more families bought houses. Due to residential needs, Zuoxun Gong Hall is now divided into many small compartments.

But according to elderly Cheng Maofa, the main rooms in the hall were originally all open; the partitions now used by each family as doors and windows are the door panels originally in front of the tablets in the main hall, taken down; there was once a plaque reading "Gong Yuan" (Tribute Graduate) in the hall, unfortunately damaged during the Cultural Revolution.

Shaochang ancestral hall

It is said that Shaochang ancestral hall was built by Cheng Lizhong (1736-1793) in the 53rd year of Qianlong (1788), to repay his father-in-law's gift of silver for "building a flower hall at the rock foot." Cheng Lizhong, courtesy name Fengshi, hao Yi'an, was grandson of Zuoxun Gong and son of Cheng Jingchen.

Cheng Jingchen's four sons each took "Fa (Prosperity)," "Qiang (Strength)," "Gang (Firmness)," "Yi (Resolution)" as branch names, so Cheng Lizhong's descendants are called "Gongxia Yichang." Because Cheng Lizhong was the youngest, "Yichang" was commonly called "Xiaochang" (Small Chang).

In Yongkang dialect "small" and "shao" are homophones, hence the name Shaochang ancestral hall.

Environment

Shaochang ancestral hall faces south, located west of Fangyan Stream, with its back diagonally facing Fangyan Mountain. Before Shaochang ancestral hall was built, this may have been just farmland. For a long time after its completion, it stood alone on this side of Fangyan Stream.

In front of the hall there is a relatively large open field, which serves both as a threshing ground for villagers after autumn harvest and as the climax location for welcoming dragon lanterns in the first lunar month. South of the open field there is also a cinema built decades ago; now every family has a television, so the cinema has become quiet.

The east edge of the open field is bounded by Fangyan Stream; on the east bank of the stream is the large mansion funded by Cheng Lizhong's father-in-law, with in-house pond, in-house well, garden and vegetable plot. At the south and north ends of the open field there is each a small bridge across Fangyan Stream leading to the mansion.

Not far north from Shaochang ancestral hall, one can also reach Shiyuan Gong Hall and Zuoxun Gong Hall via Wangping bridge.

Architecture

Shaochang ancestral hall's planar layout is basically similar to Shiyuan Gong Hall, with main hall and worship hall of comparable dimensions, but its total area is twice that of Shiyuan Gong Hall.

Compared with Shiyuan Gong Hall, the main differences in Shaochang ancestral hall's plan are reflected in the following points: the front hall and wing rooms have greater depth; there are local corridors between the wing rooms and the main spaces on the axis; the three main skylights are all relatively large; between the wing rooms on both sides of the main hall there is also a small skylight with a staircase each.

The left and right ends of the hall's front facade are高低错落 horse-head walls; the main entrance is in the center, with a secondary entrance on each side connecting to the corridors of the wing rooms and front hall. In addition, several square and round small windows are高低错落 inserted in this gray brick wall, mainly for ventilating the end wing rooms and front hall side bays.

The front hall central bay is full-height; the second-floor space of the side bays connects with the wing rooms on both sides. The central bay uses crescent beams, front and rear single-step beams, with a child pillar erected on the middle double-step beam; the upper floor uses column-and-tie structure.

Passing through the front hall is a mingtang of the same width as the front hall, with corridors on all four sides and twelve eave pillars surrounding it.

Above each eave pillar there is an ox-leg (corbel) carved with coiled dragons, birds and beasts, or flowers and trees; above the ox-leg there is also a set of exquisite stacked brackets supporting the overhanging eave tie beam above.

The outer sides of the stacked brackets all have openwork carved leaf inserts, and the inner sides are coiled-dragon shaped curved beams.

The three-bay main hall's beam frame is all exposed, with nine-purlin depth. The central bay has four pillars, using front and rear double-step beams plus a middle five-purlin beam; the side bays use five pillars, combining column-and-tie and post-and-lintel, with four sets of double-step beams between pillars.

The entire roof weight of the main hall is transmitted successively through purlin ties, stacked brackets, large brackets, flower piers under large brackets, crescent beams, beam brackets, and finally to the pillars.

Between the stacked brackets supporting the purlin ties and the pillars, curved beams are also used for connection; curved beams not only have a strong decorative effect but also serve to stabilize the structure longitudinally.

Besides curved beams, stacked brackets, sparrow brackets, flower piers, beam heads and beam brackets are all the focus of large wooden structure carvings in Shaochang ancestral hall.

In addition, the undersides of the two upper gold purlins, front lower gold purlin, eave purlin and overhanging eave tie beam have exquisite openwork relief carvings of flowers, birds, fish patterns.

Behind the main hall is a square-plan stage, with a nearly square skylight on each side of the stage. From here onward, the wing room depth becomes shallower, allowing the main spaces on the axis to widen to five bays.

The five-bay worship hall sits on a two-step-high platform. Its beam frame structure is大体 similar to the main hall.

The difference is that the front and rear double-step beams become single-step beams; outside the rear single-step beam another column-and-tie corridor is added, serving as space for placing shrines; the front of the central bay, due to the need to connect with the stage, has some modifications to the beam frame.

The middle three bays of the worship hall have doors between the gold pillars, but can be fully opened. According to elders, the doors were once carved with exquisite Twenty-Four Paragons of Filial Piety, to educate Cheng descendants who come to worship during festivals.

The large wooden carvings of the worship hall are slightly simpler than the main hall; curved beams are not used between the stacked brackets under the purlins, only scrolling-grass leaf inserts on both sides of the stacked brackets.

The east and west wing rooms are divided from south to north into five spaces of different sizes. The middle three rooms each have windows facing different skylights for lighting; the two end rooms use exterior wall windows for ventilation.

There are corridors between the wing rooms and the front hall, first skylight, and main hall; between the wing rooms behind the main hall side walls there is also a small skylight each, with a staircase on one side of each skylight leading to the second floor.

Zhou clan ancestral hall

Zhou clan ancestral hall is located in a small col at the west foot of Lion Mountain, facing west,紧挨着 the old street. The hall's position is the highest part of this section of old street; there are no buildings between it and Fangyan Mountain, giving a feeling of facing Fangyan and overlooking the valley.

Zhou clan ancestral hall is the only non-Cheng surname hall on the old street.

According to the "Youchuan Zhou clan Genealogy," this Zhou branch is called "Caoshu Zhou." Its first ancestor Shiwen Gong (1254-1315) served as Liangzhe Tiju (regional supervisor) from the 17th to 28th year of Yuan Zhiyuan (1280-1291), and because "ordered to also supervise the salt administration of Taiwen branch, after completing his term he passed through Yongkang and loved its beautiful customs," he moved from Jinhua Pantang to Caoshu in Shengping Township, Yongkang, hence named "Caoshu Zhou." From then on, Shiwen Gong's descendants moved from Caoshu to various places in Yongkang.

The Zhou clan ancestral hall was built here because the tomb of first ancestor Zhou Shiwen is located not far behind it, in a cave called "Lion Excreting" on Gongdao Mountain.

It is said that the Zhou family only lived on the old street for a very short time and left before the Cheng clan moved here; some say the Zhou never lived here, only that over a hundred people come here to worship the Zhou first ancestor in the ten days before Qingming.

The "Youchuan Zhou clan Genealogy" records that here originally there were only "three bays of flat hall, with several nunnery rooms in front," used to provide resting places for descendants coming from afar to worship ancestors during Qingming.

Later it was expanded by the three clans Youchuan, Xianchuan and Zhangchuan to its current scale; the existing building was rebuilt in the 34th year of Guangxu (1908). When the Kuomintang Zhejiang Provincial Government was stationed at Fangyan, this place served as the office of the Zhejiang Provincial Police Brigade, including a small prison.

Zhou clan ancestral hall is a nearly square four-sided courtyard. The street-facing side has horse-head walls on both sides; the white wall tops were once painted with colored pictures, but now only mottled colors can be faintly seen. The main door is centered, with a side door on each side leading to the inner corridor.

The first entry is a three-bay, seven-purlin-depth worship hall, the most exquisitely carved part of the entire building; behind the skylight, on a high platform, is a five-bay, slightly shallower worship hall; on both sides there are five wing rooms each for visiting worshippers to stay temporarily.

Moreover, "outside the hall on both sides are small halls, plus eight rooms of buildings, as residences for caretakers." These "small halls" refer to the road pavilions on both sides of Zhou clan ancestral hall, spanning across the old street; these two road pavilions are among the few places on the old street that can both shelter from wind and rain and provide rest.

Of the "eight rooms of buildings," only three now belong to the Zhou clan; the tomb keeper Zhou Yanhao and his wife and sister-in-law now live here.

Genealogy records that the hall and tomb should be guarded by reliable and capable descendants sent in rotation by the three clans Youchuan, Xianchuan and Zhangchuan; and "Yanhao Gong is a descendant of the fourth son of Youchuan, and his family has lived guarding the first ancestor's hall and tomb for over a hundred years since his great-grandfather."

Other Halls

Run-er ancestral hall is located east of Chengli village, first built in the Ming dynasty. From its architectural layout, it should be similar to Shiyuan Gong Hall, i.e. three-entry main hall with Pin-shaped skylight layout. based on policies during the Land Reform period, Run-er ancestral hall, belonging to over five generations, was nationalized.

Thus it first served as a primary school, then in 1988 was converted to the activity venue of Chengli village Elderly Association. In 2006, the interior of the hall was newly renovated, with floor and part of walls redone in concrete, and the central large-span roof also changed to steel truss structure.

Cheng Zhaofu Hall is located at the west foot of Lion Mountain, west of the old street. Built by Cheng Zhaofu in 1924, it is part of Cheng Zhenxing Inn. The hall's form is basically the same as Shiyuan Gong Hall, with slightly larger plan dimensions.

The hall also served as guest rooms during incense seasons; the central open space not only served as the inn's hall to entertain pilgrims and set up banquets, but also invited performers to sing opera here at night.

During the period when the Zhejiang Provincial Nationalist Government was temporarily stationed at Fangyan, this was also the venue for government meetings, various banquets and even weddings.

Qiwen Gong Hall is located west of Huilong bridge; its builder was Cheng Zhaochun, younger brother of Cheng Zhenxing Inn boss. Cheng Zhaochun (born 1864, death year unknown), courtesy name Qiwen, was the fifth son of Cheng Lelai, and in 1900 opened Cheng Chengxing Umbrella shop at the south end of the old street.

According to elderly Cheng Zhenfeng (born 1939, Chong generation, 21st generation of Cheng), grandson of Cheng Zhaochun, the hall layout was originally similar to Shiyuan Gong Hall. Unfortunately now only the middle three bays remain.

There is also a hall on the old street that has almost been forgotten: the Lu Branch Hall, located west of Xiahu Ravine mouth. According to elderly Cheng Zhenfeng's recollection, the Lu Branch Hall was built under the supervision of his grandfather Cheng Zhaochun, who served as hall manager.

The hall's scale is slightly smaller than Shiyuan Gong Hall, with only front and rear two courtyards. By this calculation, the hall was built around the early twentieth century. Unfortunately during the Cultural Revolution, due to lack of maintenance, this hall was ultimately destroyed by termites and fire.

Currently only some ruined walls remain, and even the basic planar layout cannot be identified.
