广慈寺历史
中文

二、广慈寺历史

创建初期

广慈寺原名大悲寺，是一座有上千年历史的天台宗名刹。它始建于唐宣宗大中四年（850年），也就是"会昌法难"结束后，恢复佛教的第四年。佛教自西汉传入中国以来，到隋唐时期已发展到鼎盛。在唐朝建立之初的二百年里，历任皇帝都大力提倡佛教并给予极高的礼遇。到唐朝后期，随着佛教影响力的不断扩大和经济势力的日益膨胀，作为一种宗教信仰，它便逐渐威胁到了唐朝的中央统治。不仅如此，唐武宗（840—846年）本人信仰道教，因此在他即位后，便推行了一系列的"灭佛"政策，包括下令拆毁寺宇，勒令僧尼还俗等。

广慈寺的肇基祖师正德禅师在那个时候也被迫离开了天台山国清寺。正德禅师，原为寺里的一个火头和尚，即负责烧饭的和尚。离开国清寺之前，他曾在国清寺第十二世祖师物外禅师门下修习止观（即是禅定、智慧二学，是修学一切佛法的根本）。

公元847年，崇信佛教的唐宣宗即位，改国号为大中元年，并立即下令恢复所有在会昌年间被废的佛寺。由于天台山国清寺没有能被马上恢复，正德禅师可能因此便来到不远的方岩山来修头陀行，即佛教苦行之一，并居住在现方岩山腰罗汉洞内。他见方岩山"仍属混沌绝境，遂访山农指点，缘藤而上，匍匐跻攀，透洞始见山巅古树参天，槁木横卧，荆棘遍地，万般寂寥。探至凹处见上中下三潭水清澈见底，涧水淙淙不绝"。于是，正德禅师在上、中潭畔先建茅棚以弘扬佛法，并于大中四年（850年）在原地创建了大悲寺。在这之后的一百多年间，方岩山清清静静，只听得到寺院里回荡的晨钟暮鼓和木鱼罄声。

胡则借读

在北宋端拱元年（988年）的春天，永康胡库人胡则来到寺院里借住，专心读书以准备赴考。作为回报，他在读书堂里教授寺院里的小和尚们读书写字，正因为如此，他广受寺院里和尚们的尊敬。据广慈寺前任住持允明禅师讲，胡则走的时候，当时的住持资助了胡则上京赶考的路费，并叮嘱他，如果考不上，就一定要回来；如果考上了，也一定要回来报喜。胡则就这样带着老和尚的嘱托和小和尚们的希望走了，他在这里读书的故事也从此被老和尚传小和尚这样一代一代地流传了下来。胡则在离别之前的阴历八月初一，"率为五言十二韵，书于屋壁下"，题名为《别方岩》，以抒发他临行前的依依不舍之情。

寓居峰顶寺，不觉度炎天。山叟频为约，林僧每出禅。

虚怀思往事，宴坐息诸缘。照像龛镫暗，通宵磬韵传。

宴心资寂寞，琢句极幽玄。拾菌寒云外，烹茶翠竹前。

远阴临岳树，清响落岩泉。僻道无来客，深秋足乱蝉。

松风生井浪，溪雨长苔钱。自省浮尘世，终难驻永年。

遍游曾宛转，欲别重留连。明日东西路，依依独黯然。

胡则于北宋端拱二年（989年）进士及第，并随即开始了他的为官生涯。他辗转睦州、温州、信州、福州、杭州、池州等地，但是却再也没有回到方岩山大悲寺来看看。胡则于北宋宝元二年（1039年）病死他乡，葬于杭州钱塘县南山履泰乡龙井源。当消息传到大悲寺，当年受胡则教诲的小和尚都已经成了老和尚，出于对老师的怀念，他们便给胡则立了一个牌位，供奉在读书堂里。关于胡则读书堂的位置，在明嘉靖《永康县志》里，还有这样一段描写，在广慈寺东边的千人坑"之侧有小径，缘崖而登，行二百余步，有石穴，曰读书堂。俗传胡侯弦诵之地"。

在清光绪《永康县志》里则把读书堂列入了地理志，古迹篇，"读书堂：去听泉楼百许步，岩腰有小石洞即胡侍郎读书处。好事者架飞甍为游人临眺之所"。但是广慈寺前任住持允明禅师却说，读书堂位于长生塘的西侧，又由于"宋高宗敕赐赫灵庙额诰"中提到"遂就旧读书处永康方岩寺池边立祠"，可以认为在山腰上的小石穴可能只是胡则的"弦诵之地"，而在长生塘西侧的读书堂则是他读书及教书的地方，也是后来被改称为佑顺侯祠和赫灵庙的建筑。

胡则从"人"到"神"的转变

在胡则去世后的八十多年里，他可能只有一个排位被寺里的和尚供奉在当年的读书堂，并享用一些来自里人的香火；读书堂也可能依然被用作小和尚的读书处。在这期间，大悲寺于北宋治平二年（1065年）失火，同年重建后易名为广慈寺。据广慈寺前任住持允明禅师讲，从那时起胡则的牌位和一尊小的塑像就被放在了如来佛的前面。在近六十年后，方岩山广慈寺在北宋宣和二年（1120年）爆发的方腊起义中，曾作为永康知县的赵琪退守并最终击败起义军的地方。这件貌似偶然且毫无关系的事件却成为胡则从"人"变成"神"的关键第一步，其实这两件事之间暗含着深刻的历史原因。

自汉朝以来，中国历代的皇帝都独尊儒术，不但封其为官教，而且把它作为维护统治阶级的一种精神工具。但是儒家学说中教化的理念占到主要成分，它对生命的体会却十分肤浅，甚至不能回答一些有关生、死、祸、福等从皇帝到老百姓都关心的人生根本问题。所以历代皇帝在独尊儒术的同时，又利用佛教或道教来满足这些世俗的需求，同时进一步巩固其统治地位。换句话来说，"原始宗教的天命观、鬼神观、巫术等，在皇权的暗中保护下，大量地保存下来，并在人民中找到适宜的生存空间。

封建皇帝对民间信仰中的神灵也从不一概排斥，甚至对许多地方涌现出的神予以册封。"与胡则生活在同一时代，家喻户晓的"妈祖"（960—987年）就是一个有力的证明。"妈祖"真名为林默，由于她生前和死后的众多传说，宋、元、明、清的历代帝王都对她有多次的册封，封号也从"夫人"、"天妃"、"天后"到"天上圣母"，并列入国家祀典。历代帝王们不但通过对神祇的册封来确立神的地位，从而巩固自己的统治，更重要的是借此来证明一种远远比"君权神授"更高的权力地位，即"神权君授"。

宋代政权建立之后的前几代皇帝都推崇佛教，但是宋徽宗（在位1100—1125年），却尊信道教，他曾一度命令佛教和道教合流，改寺院为道观，并使佛号、僧尼的名称都道教化。在历史上，每当新任皇帝弃佛从道的时期，起源于异国的佛教为了生存，都不得不对土生土长在中国大地上的道教进行妥协，把一些属于道教的，或者是民间的神祇请入佛堂共享香火。在这一点上，广慈寺也不例外，这也是它能历经千年依然香火旺盛的原因之一。在宋徽宗当政时期发生的一件事，恰好让广慈寺顺理成章地把一位民间神祇纳入了自己的领地。

宣和二年（1120年）十月初九，歙州农民方腊由于不满北宋腐朽统治，借摩尼教的旗号在睦州青溪爆发起义。摩尼教又称明教，在唐会昌年间被禁，随后转入民间秘密流行，历经五代至宋朝仍在闽浙一带有广泛的信徒，因此这次起义迅速得到了广大农民的热烈拥护和响应。北宋王朝的统治者在镇压这次带有宗教色彩的农民起义的时候，也充分利用了地方神祇的力量，宣传神人相助的神话。由于这次起义在短短一年半的时间就被全部镇压，所以在这其中的所有战斗都能最终以正派神祇的胜利作为结束，从而一箭双雕地打击"邪教"和"寇匪"。

在此期间，北宋王朝大力褒奖地方神祇，因此"在方腊起义中显灵庇护官军而报至朝廷请求嘉奖的祠庙不在少数"，如"上虞遗德庙、嵊县显应庙等"。有些即使是未受"寇匪"侵扰的地方，地方统治者也会宣称是因为有神人暗中相助的结果。

在方腊起义中，永康方岩陈十四也揭竿而起。他们攻破永康县城，迫使知县赵琪退据方岩山。虽然赵琪最终是仓皇逃离了方岩山，但是因为陈十四的起义军不久后就被镇压了，可以推测知县赵琪也顺势迎合皇帝的意愿，杜撰了在方岩山有神人助王师灭寇的神话。而曾经"逮事三朝，十握州符，六持使节"，并且已经被当地人所熟悉和爱戴的永康人胡则，也顺理成章地成了神人助王师神话的主角。况且当时胡则在广慈寺读书堂里已立有牌位，把他神化成方岩山神便再合适不过了。

这些神话故事有许多版本，它们的真实性和逻辑性并不是本文所讨论的重点，所以不妨就引用"宋高宗敕赐赫灵庙额诰"中所记述的官方版本，"民以重罹兵革为患，神独感梦于主将，显灵而阴助之。又贼魁梦一巨人，衣白衣跨白马饮池，黎明视水涸，其徒惊骇，遂至大败"。在宋徽宗宣和四年（1122年），也就是方腊起义被全面镇压后的这年，胡则被封为"佑顺侯"，这也是他去世后得到的第一次封赏。或许因为在同时有众多的地方神祇都因为类似的原因而得到了封赏，以至于"王廉访当时奏请失于勘斟，遂致所封敕中不言姓名"，而"止用方岩神奏"。

或许是这次册封的确不重要，以至于没能引起胡氏后人的重视，因为这次册封的诰书并没有收录进《库川胡氏宗谱》。广慈寺在当时推崇道教的大背景下，倒是可能利用了这次册封，把胡则称为"方岩神佑顺侯"而纳入佛家圣地，并"香火供养甚谨"。而同时，方岩附近的一些地区也开始供奉胡则。作为一方神祇，胡则是"若水若旱若疫若疠，有求无不应，有祷无不答"。随着他在当地的名气越来越大，胡氏后人可能不满足于这次未言姓名的封赏，于是"阖邑之士状于有司"。

在绍兴二十一年（1151年），作为建安县丞，也是胡则重孙的胡廷直向当时的宋高宗"详述始末，力求正名"。在核实了衢州委司户参军赵善和绍兴府诸暨县主簿赵彦伦之后，礼部依建炎三年（1129年）颁布的"挥神祠遇有灵应即先赐额"的圣旨，向皇帝建议赐"赫灵庙"为胡则祠的匾额。绍兴二十二年（1152年）二月，宋高宗降旨"敕赐赫灵庙"。

胡则的这次被册封，只是他日后一系列册封的开始。在宋代，册封制度已经逐步完善，规定对于有灵应的神祠要"无爵号者赐以额，已赐额者加封爵。初封侯，再封公，次封王"。而对于同一级别内封号的字数也有限制，开始为两个字，可逐步增加最多到八个字。关于册封制度的这些具体要求，从胡则的历次册封就可以看出来。在宋绍熙三年（1192年），胡则被宋光宗赐为"佑顺嘉应侯"；随后在嘉泰三年（1203年）和嘉定三年（1210年），被宋宁宗分两次册封至"佑顺嘉应福泽显灵侯"。

从这些封号用词的选择中，可以看出在表彰胡则时间中态度的变化。"佑顺"是从皇帝角度上一种客观的认可；"嘉应"代表了一种赞许和肯定；"福泽"则是站在人民的立场上一种不遗余力的褒扬；最终到"显灵"，即一种超客观的神化。与此同时，胡则被"庙祀于衢、婺之间，无虑数十"。到淳祐六年（1246年），宋理宗封胡则"显应公"，随后"加正惠，宝祐初再加忠祐"。

在胡则被一次一次加封的同时，方岩山上供奉胡则的读书堂却"岁久事殊，址存堂废"。宋景定三年（1262年），里人王氏"不忍其坏，举而新之夫"。在这篇吴邃写的《方岩读书堂记》中还提到了作者本人在读书堂重修之后，"谨即斯堂拜公遗像"。由此可见，位于读书堂内的胡则塑像，最迟在重修后就已经存在了。据广慈寺前任住持允明禅师讲，胡公庙以前就位于读书堂的位置。如此看来，有了胡则塑像的读书堂，日后就逐渐演化成了明嘉靖《永康县志》中提到的"佑顺侯祠"和清道光《永康县志》里所指的"赫灵祠"。

近一百年后，写于元至正九年（1349年）的《胡侍郎庙碑阴记》中再次提到地方的胡则庙"布满于郡境，不啻数十百区"，胡则更是被演化成了一位"能出云为风雨"的万能神。

明清两代的继续发展

在经历了崇奉喇嘛教的元代，明代是佛教各个宗派逐步恢复和发展的时期。在这个时期里，广慈寺的香火也逐步趋于鼎盛，值得一提的是在明万历年间（1589—1619年）任住持的聿孤禅师，以及在他任职期间发生的两件事。据说聿孤禅师买了八个小孩当徒弟，改革寺之始就实行的"子孙派"，将其分为乾、天、承、地、人、才、宝、智八房，轮流管理寺院香火和常务工作。"子孙派"规定只接收不超过五岁的幼儿皈依，小沙弥自幼与师父同起居、同饮食，形同父子，并死后合葬。

聿孤禅师的改革既保持了"子孙派"极具亲和力的特点，又使寺院管理可以互相监督，并在此基础上发展了众多"下属寺院"。这也是广慈寺能历经多种磨难而成为千年古刹矗立在此的主要原因。

另一件事对广慈寺则是一个"重创"。据允明禅师讲，在明隆庆五年（1571年），附近的文楼村出了一个榜眼叫程正谊，后任四川左布政。程正谊的父亲叫程方峰（1498—1585年），他看上了方岩山这块宝地，便让儿子向当时的住持聿孤禅师借地做坟。聿孤禅师开始并不愿意，但念在自己是程方峰的孙子辈，便写了"卖地十丈"的地契。若干年后，聿孤禅师圆寂了，程方峰也过世了，程氏子孙把"卖地十丈"改成了"卖地千丈"，并在前街路边上的母子樟旁开始建造程方峰祠堂，一场官司便由此而生。

寺里的和尚以整个方岩山也没有千丈为据来证明"卖地千丈"不实。审判结果是"卖地是真，千丈是假"，并判以千人坑和长生塘为界，南边归程氏，北侧归广慈寺。而这个审判结果在清光绪《永康县志》中也有明确记载，"程方峰先生梓墓：方岩上自千人坑流水为界，至峰门悬崖峭壁俱属墓地。万历（1573—1620年）年间奉勘合给。"这件事不知过了多久以后，文楼村的程氏看出了方岩山上的商机，开始在天街上做起了香烛、供品等生意。

一半的方岩山归了别人，广慈寺的香火和收入多少都会受到影响。清顺治十一年（1654年），赫灵庙就在胡氏后人的帮助下历经了一次修葺。可是几十年后，清康熙《永康县志》中就已经记载赫灵祠因年久失修，几近坍塌，寺里的僧人便把胡则的塑像搬到了广慈寺内。同时搬过去的应该还有胡则的牌位和那块敕赐的"赫灵庙"匾，它们一起被供奉在当时广慈寺的前殿内，从而真正意义上做到了寺庙合一。

在这以后，从雍正七年（1729年）到道光二十七年（1847年）的一百多年里，奉胡则为伯祖的库川胡氏后人，先后多次出资帮助广慈寺修理或重建前厅和大殿，仅有记载的就有六次。其中嘉庆二十四年（1819年）对前厅的重建和扩建，基本上奠定了广慈寺中轴线最终的平面格局。

据允明禅师回忆，广慈寺中轴线上有四进殿堂，各殿堂之间有廊相连。第一进为广慈寺前厅，挂"广慈寺"匾；第二进为前殿，挂"赫灵庙"匾，中间供吃荤的"行公"胡则，背面为韦驮，两旁供胡则十弟兄；第三进为大雄宝殿，中间供如来佛，前面是乾隆三年（1738年）重塑的胡则像，旁边是文殊和普贤菩萨，殿堂两侧为十八罗汉；第四进是屏风阁，主供玉皇大帝；在大雄宝殿的东侧还建有观音阁。八房僧人的僧房则位于这些殿堂的外围，在平面上形成一个"八卦"图案。

在宣统二年（1910年），出于对"人神共庙"批判的回应，寺里的僧人把玉皇大帝请到了位于西侧的地藏殿，把如来佛前的胡则像单独供奉于屏风阁，为吃素的"坐公"胡则。胡公虽然从大雄宝殿里被请了出来，但是据允明禅师说，即使现在胡公像后面依旧有如来、文殊和普贤菩萨的小塑像。

走向衰落

不幸的是，道光二十九年（1849年）九月初四的一场大火烧毁了整个祠庙及僧房。虽然在一年多以后，广慈寺得以重建，但是这已经成为它走向衰落的第一步。据允明禅师讲，清朝末年，当地老百姓的生活十分穷苦。再加上鸦片的泛滥，所以时常有人上山拿走庙里的东西，甚至是拆掉殿宇靠卖木头的钱为生。僧人也因此越来越少，八房僧人也只剩下了四房。《方岩指南》中也提到"清光绪年间（1875—1908年），寺僧相率而去"。在民国20和21两年，广慈寺还被后钱村来的一个道士霸占，寺内的僧人还被迫流落到附近的下属寺院。

在接下来几十年里一系列的人为灾害中，文化大革命无疑带给了广慈寺前所未有的重创。广慈寺几乎所有的建筑均在1966年8月下旬被拆除，仅有屏风阁和一幢僧房幸免于难。屏风阁原归方岩公社拆除，但是出于近千年对胡则的尊重，老街上的人拒绝这样做。于是，屏风阁被留了下来，并先后被用作大队开会的地方，林场仓库、邮电学校和602工厂。不仅如此，广慈寺内的字画和书籍从山上往下挑了十担，整整烧了一天。虽然《广慈寺寺志》在允明禅师的保护下逃过了这场浩劫，但却不明不白地遗失在永康市宗教科的各种卷宗之间。

"文革"后的复苏

文化大革命结束两年后，在1978年八月初一的凌晨，由岩下村村民黄埔林的母亲发起，她和另外三个妇女把一尊新塑的小胡公像抬上了山。"文革"后的第一任住持允明禅师于1979年十月初三上山，并凭记忆重塑了胡则像和屏风阁里其他的塑像，包括文曲星、财神、龙王和关公等。由于屏风阁还在，所以就有香客慕名来拜胡则。允明禅师用募捐来的香火钱，在1980年到1984年的五年间，买了四座祠堂，用以恢复广慈寺的建筑。第一个是在1980年用3000多元买的古竹畈村的一个厅，用来重建屏风阁前的大雄宝殿。

紧接着用比较便宜的价格买下了因建杨汗水库而被迫拆除的汗下周村的周氏祠堂。随后用4000多元买了龙油县山買村的牛王殿，用来恢复天王殿、步云亭、后山的亭子和做一些修修补补的工作。最后买了永康市里的徐氏祠堂，在观音阁原址上重建了现在的广慈寺观音殿。

English

2. History of Guangci temple

Early Founding Period

Guangci temple, originally named Dabei temple, is a famous Tiantai sect temple with over a thousand years of history. It was founded in the fourth year of Tang Xuanzong's Dazhong era (850), that is, the fourth year after the end of the "Huichang Persecution" and the restoration of Buddhism.

Since Buddhism was introduced to China in the Western Han Dynasty, it had developed to its peak by the Sui and Tang dynasties. In the first two hundred years after the founding of the Tang Dynasty, successive emperors vigorously promoted Buddhism and给予 it extremely high treatment.

In the late Tang Dynasty, as Buddhism's influence continued to expand and its economic power grew increasingly, as a religious belief it gradually threatened the central rule of the Tang Dynasty.

Moreover, Tang Wuzong (840-846) himself believed in Daoism, so after ascending the throne he implemented a series of "Buddha-destroying" policies, including ordering the demolition of temples and forcing monks and nuns to return to lay life.

Guangci temple's founding patriarch Master Zhengde was also forced to leave Guoqing temple on Tiantai Mountain at that time. Master Zhengde was originally a fire-head monk in the temple, i.e. the monk responsible for cooking.

Before leaving Guoqing temple, he had studied Zhiguan (the two studies of samadhi and prajna, the foundation of cultivating all Buddhist dharma) under the twelfth-generation patriarch Master Wuwai of Guoqing temple.

In 847 AD, the Buddhism-devout Tang Xuanzong ascended the throne, changed the reign title to the first year of Dazhong, and immediately ordered the restoration of all Buddhist temples abolished during the Huichang period.

Since Guoqing temple on Tiantai Mountain could not be restored immediately, Master Zhengde may therefore have come to nearby Fangyan Mountain to practice dhutanga (one of the Buddhist ascetic practices), and lived in what is now Luohan Cave on Fangyan Mountainside.

He saw Fangyan Mountain as "still a chaotic desperate realm, so he sought guidance from mountain farmers, climbed up by vines, crawled and clambered, through the cave only then saw the mountain top with ancient trees towering to the sky, withered trees lying horizontally, thorns everywhere, utterly desolate.

Exploring to the concave place he saw the upper, middle and lower pools with water clear enough to see the bottom, the stream gurgling endlessly." Thus, Master Zhengde first built thatched huts by the upper and middle pools to propagate the Dharma, and in the fourth year of Dazhong (850) founded Dabei temple on the original site.

For over a hundred years thereafter, Fangyan Mountain was quiet and peaceful, with only the morning bell and evening drum and wooden fish and chime sounds echoing in the temple.

Hu Ze Borrows to Study

In the spring of the first year of Northern Song Duangong (988), Hu Ze of Yongkang Huku came to stay in the temple, concentrating on reading to prepare for the imperial examination. In return, he taught the young monks in the study hall to read and write; precisely because of this, he was widely respected by the monks in the temple.

According to former Guangci temple abbot Master Yunming, when Hu Ze left, the then abbot funded Hu Ze's travel expenses to the capital for the exam, and urged him that if he failed, he must come back; if he passed, he must also come back to report the good news.

Hu Ze thus left with the old monk's exhortations and the young monks' hopes, and the story of his studying here has been passed down generation after generation from old monks to young monks.

Before his departure, on the first day of the eighth lunar month, Hu Ze "composed a twelve-rhyme five-character poem, written on the wall below," titled "Farewell to Fangyan," to express his reluctance to part.

dwelling at Peak temple, unknowingly passing hot days. Mountain elders frequently made appointments, forest monks often emerged from meditation.

Empty mind recalling past events, sitting in stillness ceasing all conditions. Image-illuminating niche lamp dim, through the night chime tones transmitted.

Quiet mind nourishing solitude, polishing phrases to utmost profundity. Picking mushrooms beyond cold clouds, brewing tea before green bamboos.

Distant shade facing mountain trees, clear sound falling on rock springs. Remote path with no visitors, late autumn full of chaotic cicadas.

Pine wind generating well waves, stream rain growing moss coins. Self-reflecting on floating worldly life, ultimately hard to stay forever young.

Traveling everywhere once winding, wanting to leave again lingering. Tomorrow east-west roads, reluctantly alone and dejected.

Hu Ze passed the jinshi examination in the second year of Northern Song Duangong (989), and immediately began his official career. He served in Muzhou, Wenzhou, Xinzhou, Fuzhou, Hangzhou, Chizhou and other places, but never returned to Dabei temple on Fangyan Mountain to visit.

Hu Ze died in a foreign land in the second year of Northern Song Baoyuan (1039), and was buried at Longjingyuan, Lütai Township, South Mountain, Qiantang County, Hangzhou.

When the news reached Dabei temple, the young monks who had been taught by Hu Ze had all become old monks; out of nostalgia for their teacher, they erected a memorial tablet for Hu Ze, enshrined in the study hall.

Regarding the location of Hu Ze's study hall, in the Ming Jiajing "Yongkang County Gazetteer," there is this description: beside Thousand-Man Pit east of Guangci temple "there is a small path, climbing along the cliff, walking over two hundred paces, there is a stone cave, called Study Hall.

Commonly said to be the place where Marquis Hu recited and studied." In the Qing Guangxu "Yongkang County Gazetteer," the study hall is listed in the geography chapter, antiquities section: "Study Hall: about a hundred paces from Tingquan Tower, there is a small stone cave on the mountainside where Vice-Minister Hu studied.

Enthusiasts built flying eaves as a place for tourists to gaze from." But former Guangci temple abbot Master Yunming said the study hall is located west of Changsheng pond; and since the "Song Gaozong Imperial Edict Granting Heling temple Plaque" mentions "thus at the old study place by the pond of Yongkang Fangyan temple a shrine was established," it can be considered that the small stone cave on the mountainside may only be Hu Ze's "recitation place," while the study hall west of Changsheng pond was where he studied and taught, and was later renamed Youshun Marquis shrine and Heling temple.

Hu Ze's Transformation from "Man" to "God"

In the eighty-some years after Hu Ze's death, he may have had only a memorial tablet enshrined by the temple monks in the former study hall, enjoying some incense from local people; the study hall may also have continued to be used as a place for young monks to study.

During this period, Dabei temple caught fire in the second year of Northern Song Zhiping (1065), and after reconstruction in the same year was renamed Guangci temple. According to former abbot Master Yunming, from that time Hu Ze's memorial tablet and a small statue were placed in front of the Tathagata Buddha.

Nearly sixty years later, during the Fang La Uprising that broke out in the second year of Northern Song Xuanhe (1120), Guangci temple on Fangyan Mountain served as the place where Zhao Qi, magistrate of Yongkang, retreated and ultimately defeated the uprising army.

This seemingly accidental and unrelated event became the key first step in Hu Ze's transformation from "man" to "god"; in fact, there are profound historical reasons implicit between these two events.

Since the Han Dynasty, Chinese emperors of all dynasties have exclusively honored Confucianism, not only establishing it as the state religion but also using it as a spiritual tool to maintain the ruling class.

But the concept of moral education occupies the main component in Confucian doctrine, while its understanding of life is quite superficial, unable even to answer fundamental questions about life, death, misfortune, and blessing that concerned everyone from emperor to commoner.

Therefore, while exclusively honoring Confucianism, successive emperors also used Buddhism or Daoism to satisfy these worldly needs, further consolidating their rule.

In other words, "the primitive religious concepts of heavenly mandate, ghosts and spirits, witchcraft, etc., under the covert protection of imperial power, were largely preserved and found suitable living space among the people.

Feudal emperors never uniformly rejected the deities in folk beliefs, and even canonized many locally emerging gods." The well-known "Mazu" (960-987), who lived in the same era as Hu Ze, is a powerful proof.

"Mazu's" real name was Lin Mo; due to numerous legends about her before and after death, emperors of Song, Yuan, Ming, and Qing dynasties all canonized her multiple times, with titles from "Lady," "Heavenly Consort," "Empress of Heaven" to "Heavenly Holy Mother," and she was列入 state sacrifices.

Successive emperors not only established the status of gods through canonization, thereby consolidating their own rule, but more importantly used this to prove a power status far higher than "imperial power granted by god" — namely "divine power granted by emperor."

The first few emperors after the founding of the Song dynasty all promoted Buddhism, but Emperor Huizong of Song (reigned 1100-1125) revered Daoism; he once ordered Buddhism and Daoism to merge, converting temples into Daoist abbeys, and making Buddha titles and monk/nun names Daoist-ized.

Historically, whenever a new emperor abandoned Buddhism for Daoism, Buddhism — originating in a foreign land — for survival had to compromise with the native Daoism of China, inviting some Daoist or folk deities into the Buddhist hall to share incense.

In this regard, Guangci temple was no exception, which is also one of the reasons it could endure a thousand years with prosperous incense. An event during Emperor Huizong's reign恰好 allowed Guangci temple to naturally incorporate a folk deity into its domain.

On the ninth day of the tenth month in the second year of Xuanhe (1120), Fang La, a peasant from Shezhou, dissatisfied with the corrupt rule of the Northern Song, launched an uprising in Qingxi, Muzhou under the banner of Manichaeism.

Manichaeism, also called Mingjiao (Bright Religion), was banned during the Tang Huichang period, then转入 secret folk circulation, and through the Five Dynasties to the Song dynasty still had wide followers in the Fujian-Zhejiang region; therefore this uprising quickly received enthusiastic support and response from the broad peasantry.

The Northern Song rulers, when suppressing this religiously-colored peasant uprising, also fully utilized the power of local deities, propagating myths of divine-human assistance.

Because this uprising was completely suppressed in just one and a half years, all battles within it could ultimately end with the victory of orthodox deities, thereby killing two birds with one stone by striking at "heretical religion" and "bandits." During this period, the Northern Song dynasty vigorously rewarded local deities, so "there were no few shrines that manifested spirit to protect official troops during the Fang La Uprising and were reported to the court requesting commendation," such as "Shangyu Yide temple, Shengxian Xianying temple, etc." Even in places not harassed by "bandits," local rulers would claim it was because deities secretly assisted.

During the Fang La Uprising, Chen Shisi of Yongkang Fangyan also rose up. They breached Yongkang county seat, forcing Magistrate Zhao Qi to retreat to Fangyan Mountain.

Although Zhao Qi ultimately fled Fangyan Mountain in panic, because Chen Shisi's uprising army was soon suppressed, it can be inferred that Magistrate Zhao Qi also followed the emperor's wishes and fabricated a myth of deities assisting the royal army to destroy bandits on Fangyan Mountain.

And Hu Ze, a Yongkang native who had "served three dynasties, held ten prefectural seals, six envoy credentials," and was already familiar and beloved by locals, naturally became the protagonist of the divine-assistance myth.

Moreover, Hu Ze already had a memorial tablet in the Guangci temple study hall, so deifying him as the Fangyan Mountain god was most fitting.

These myths have many versions; their authenticity and logic are not the focus of this discussion, so we may as well cite the official version recorded in the "Song Gaozong Imperial Edict Granting Heling temple Plaque": "The people suffered from repeated warfare; the god alone appeared in dreams to the commander, manifesting spirit and secretly assisting.

Moreover, the bandit leader dreamed of a giant, in white clothes riding a white horse drinking at the pond; at dawn the water was seen dry, his followers were terrified, and thus suffered a great defeat." In the fourth year of Emperor Huizong's Xuanhe (1122), the year after the Fang La Uprising was fully suppressed, Hu Ze was enfeoffed as "Youshun Marquis" — the first title he received after death.

Perhaps because at the same time numerous local deities received titles for similar reasons, "the investigating official Wang at that time requested without proper verification, resulting in the imperial edict not stating the name," and "only used 'Fangyan God' in the report."

Perhaps this canonization was indeed unimportant, failing to attract the attention of Hu's descendants, because the edict for this canonization was not included in the "Kuchuan Hu clan Genealogy." Guangci temple, under the prevailing background of promoting Daoism at that time, may have used this canonization to call Hu Ze "Fangyan God Youshun Marquis" and incorporate him into the Buddhist holy land, with "incense offerings made with great reverence." Meanwhile, some areas near Fangyan also began to worship Hu Ze.

As a local deity, Hu Ze was "whether flood or drought or plague or epidemic, every request is answered, every prayer is answered." As his fame grew locally, Hu's descendants may have been unsatisfied with this unnamed title, so "the scholars of the entire county petitioned the authorities." In the twenty-first year of Shaoxing (1151), Hu Tingzhi, who was the assistant magistrate of Jian'an County and also Hu Ze's great-grandson, "detailed the whole story to the then Song Gaozong, striving to rectify the name." After verifying with Zhao Shan, registrar of Quzhou, and Zhao Yanlun, registrar of Zhuji County in Shaoxing Prefecture, the Ministry of Rites, following the imperial edict promulgated in the third year of Jianyan (1129) that "divine shrines with spiritual manifestations should first be granted plaques," recommended to the emperor granting "Heling temple" as the plaque for Hu Ze's shrine.

In the second month of the twenty-second year of Shaoxing (1152), Song Gaozong issued the edict "Imperially Granting Heling temple."

This canonization of Hu Ze was only the beginning of his future series of canonizations. In the Song dynasty, the canonization system had gradually been perfected, stipulating that for spiritually responsive shrines "those without noble titles should be granted plaques, those already granted plaques should be added noble titles.

First enfeoffed as marquis, then as duke, then as king." There were also limits on the number of characters in titles within the same rank, starting with two characters, gradually increasing to a maximum of eight. These specific requirements of the canonization system can be seen from Hu Ze's successive canonizations.

In the third year of Song Shaoxi (1192), Hu Ze was granted "Youshun Jiaying Marquis" by Song Guangzong; subsequently in the third year of Jiatai (1203) and the third year of Jiading (1210), he was canonized in two stages by Song Ningzong to "Youshun Jiaying Fuze Xianling Marquis." From the choice of words in these titles, one can see the change in attitude toward commending Hu Ze over time.

"Youshun" is an objective recognition from the emperor's perspective; "Jiaying" represents approval and affirmation; "Fuze" is a wholehearted praise from the people's standpoint; finally "Xianling" is a super-objective deification.

Meanwhile, Hu Ze was "worshipped in temples between Quzhou and Wuzhou, no fewer than several dozen." By the sixth year of Chunyou (1246), Song Lizong enfeoffed Hu Ze as "Xianying Duke," subsequently "added Zhenghui, in the early Baoyou period further added Zhongyou."

While Hu Ze was being加封 again and again, the study hall on Fangyan Mountain enshrining Hu Ze was "as years passed things changed, the site remained but the hall was ruined." In the third year of Song Jingding (1262), local Wang "could not bear its ruin, and undertook to renew it." In this "Record of Fangyan Study Hall" written by Wu Sui, the author also mentions that after the study hall was rebuilt, he "reverently bowed to the lord's remaining image in this hall." This shows that the Hu Ze statue inside the study hall existed at the latest after the reconstruction.

According to former abbot Master Yunming, Hu Gong temple was formerly located at the study hall site.

Thus, the study hall with the Hu Ze statue gradually evolved into the "Youshun Marquis shrine" mentioned in the Ming Jiajing "Yongkang County Gazetteer" and the "Heling shrine" referred to in the Qing Daoguang "Yongkang County Gazetteer." Nearly a hundred years later, the "Record on the Back of Hu Shilang temple Stele" written in the ninth year of Yuan Zhizheng (1349) again mentions that local Hu Ze temples "spread throughout the prefecture, no fewer than several dozen to a hundred districts," and Hu Ze was further evolved into an omnipotent god who "can summon clouds and bring wind and rain."

Continued Development in Ming and Qing Dynasties

After experiencing the Yuan dynasty which revered Lamaism, the Ming dynasty was a period when various Buddhist sects gradually recovered and developed.

During this period, Guangci temple's incense also gradually reached its peak; noteworthy is Master Yugu, who served as abbot during the Ming Wanli period (1589-1619), and two events that occurred during his tenure.

It is said that Master Yugu bought eight children as disciples, reforming the "descendant school" implemented since the temple's founding, dividing it into eight houses: Qian, Tian, Cheng, Di, Ren, Cai, Bao, Zhi, taking turns managing temple incense and daily affairs.

The "descendant school" stipulated that only children under five years old could be received for conversion; young novices lived and ate with their masters from childhood, like father and son, and were buried together after death.

Master Yugu's reform both maintained the highly affiliative characteristics of the "descendant school" and enabled temple management to supervise each other, and on this basis developed numerous "subordinate temples." This is also the main reason Guangci temple could endure various tribulations and stand here as a thousand-year ancient temple.

The other event was a "heavy blow" to Guangci temple. According to Master Yunming, in the fifth year of Ming Longqing (1571), a nearby Wenlou village produced a bangyan (second-place imperial examination graduate) named Cheng Zhengyi, who later served as Left Administrative Commissioner of Sichuan.

Cheng Zhengyi's father was Cheng Fangfeng (1498-1585), who took a fancy to the precious land of Fangyan Mountain and had his son borrow land from the then abbot Master Yugu for a grave.

Master Yugu was initially unwilling, but considering himself a grandson-generation of Cheng Fangfeng, wrote a land deed of "selling ten zhang of land." Some years later, Master Yugu passed away, and Cheng Fangfeng also died; the Cheng descendants changed "selling ten zhang" to "selling a thousand zhang," and began building Cheng Fangfeng ancestral hall beside Mother-and-Child Camphor by the front street, giving rise to a lawsuit.

The temple monks used the argument that the entire Fangyan Mountain did not even have a thousand zhang to prove "selling a thousand zhang" was false.

The trial result was "selling land is true, a thousand zhang is false," and ruled that with Thousand-Man Pit and Changsheng pond as the boundary, the south belonged to the Cheng family, the north to Guangci temple. This trial result is also clearly recorded in the Qing Guangxu "Yongkang County Gazetteer": "Mr.

Cheng Fangfeng's tomb: on Fangyan, from the flowing water of Thousand-Man Pit as boundary, to the peak gate cliffs all belong to the tomb site.

Granted during Wanli (1573-1620)." Sometime after this event, the Cheng family of Wenlou village saw business opportunities on Fangyan Mountain and began selling incense candles, offerings and other goods on Tianjie.

With half of Fangyan Mountain belonging to others, Guangci temple's incense and income were more or less affected. In the eleventh year of Qing Shunzhi (1654), Heling temple underwent a renovation with the help of Hu's descendants.

But decades later, the Qing Kangxi "Yongkang County Gazetteer" already recorded that Heling shrine, due to long neglect, was nearly collapsed; the temple monks then moved Hu Ze's statue into Guangci temple.

Also moved should have been Hu Ze's memorial tablet and the imperially-granted "Heling temple" plaque, which were together enshrined in the then front hall of Guangci temple, thereby truly achieving temple-shrine unity.

Thereafter, in the over one hundred years from the seventh year of Yongzheng (1729) to the twenty-seventh year of Daoguang (1847), the Kuchuan Hu descendants, who revered Hu Ze as their great-uncle, repeatedly funded the repair or reconstruction of Guangci temple's front hall and main hall, with six recorded instances.

Among them, the reconstruction and expansion of the front hall in the twenty-fourth year of Jiaqing (1819) basically established the final planar layout of Guangci temple's central axis.

According to Master Yunming's recollection, Guangci temple's central axis had four halls, connected by corridors between each hall.

The first hall was the Guangci temple front hall, hanging the "Guangci temple" plaque; the second hall was the front殿, hanging the "Heling temple" plaque, with the meat-eating "Xinggong" (Traveling Duke) Hu Ze in the center, Skanda behind, and Hu Ze's ten sworn brothers on both sides; the third hall was the Mahavira Hall, with Tathagata Buddha in the center, Hu Ze's statue recast in the third year of Qianlong (1738) in front, Manjushri and Samantabhadra Bodhisattvas beside, and Eighteen Arhats on both sides of the hall; the fourth hall was Pingfeng Pavilion (Screen Wind Pavilion), mainly enshrining the Jade Emperor; east of the Mahavira Hall was also built Guanyin Pavilion.

The monk quarters of the eight houses were located around these halls, forming a "Bagua" (Eight Trigrams) pattern in plan.

In the second year of Xuantong (1910), in response to criticism of "humans and gods sharing a temple," the temple monks moved the Jade Emperor to the Ksitigarbha Hall on the west side, and separately enshrined the Hu Ze statue in front of the Tathagata in Pingfeng Pavilion, as the vegetarian "Zuogong" (Sitting Duke) Hu Ze.

Although Hu Gong was moved out of the Mahavira Hall, according to Master Yunming, even now behind the Hu Gong statue there are still small statues of Tathagata, Manjushri and Samantabhadra.

Decline

Unfortunately, a great fire on the fourth day of the ninth month in the twenty-ninth year of Daoguang (1849) destroyed the entire shrine-temple and monk quarters. Although Guangci temple was rebuilt over a year later, this had already become the first step toward its decline.

According to Master Yunming, in the late Qing dynasty, local people's lives were very poor. Coupled with the spread of opium, people often went up the mountain to take things from the temple, even demolishing halls to sell wood for a living. Monks therefore became fewer and fewer, and of the eight houses only four remained.

"Fangyan Guide" also mentions "during the Qing Guangxu period (1875-1908), temple monks left one after another." In the 20th and 21st years of the Republic, Guangci temple was even occupied by a Daoist from Houqian village, and the monks inside were forced to wander to nearby subordinate temples.

Among a series of man-made disasters in the following decades, the Cultural Revolution undoubtedly brought Guangci temple an unprecedented heavy blow. Almost all buildings of Guangci temple were demolished in late August 1966, with only Pingfeng Pavilion and one monk quarter surviving.

Pingfeng Pavilion was originally assigned to Fangyan Commune for demolition, but out of nearly a thousand years of respect for Hu Ze, people on the old street refused to do so. Thus, Pingfeng Pavilion was preserved, and was successively used as a brigade meeting place, forestry warehouse, post and telecommunications school, and Factory 602.

Moreover, the calligraphy, paintings and books inside Guangci temple were carried down the mountain in ten shoulder loads and burned for a whole day. Although the "Guangci temple Chronicle" escaped this catastrophe under Master Yunming's protection, it was inexplicably lost among the various files of the Yongkang Municipal Religious Affairs Section.

Recovery after the Cultural Revolution

Two years after the end of the Cultural Revolution, in the early morning of the first day of the eighth lunar month in 1978, initiated by the mother of Yanxia village villager Huang Pulin, she and three other women carried a newly molded small Hu Gong statue up the mountain.

The first post-Cultural Revolution abbot Master Yunming went up the mountain on the third day of the tenth lunar month in 1979, and from memory recast the Hu Ze statue and other statues in Pingfeng Pavilion, including Wenqu Star, God of Wealth, Dragon King and Guan Gong. Because Pingfeng Pavilion still stood, pilgrims came to worship Hu Gong.

Master Yunming used donated incense money, during the five years from 1980 to 1984, to buy four ancestral halls to restore Guangci temple's buildings.

The first was a hall in Guzhufan village bought for over 3,000 yuan in 1980, used to rebuild the Mahavira Hall in front of Pingfeng Pavilion.紧接着 he bought at a relatively cheap price the Zhou clan ancestral hall of Hanxia Zhou village, which was forced to be demolished due to the construction of Yanghan Reservoir.

Subsequently he spent over 4,000 yuan buying the Niuwang (Ox King) Hall of Shanmai village in Longyou County, used to restore the Heavenly King Hall, Buyun Pavilion, rear mountain pavilions and do some repair work.

Finally he bought the Xu clan ancestral hall in Yongkang city, rebuilding the current Guangci temple Guanyin Pavilion on the original site of the old Guanyin Pavilion.

本篇术语 Key Terms

广慈寺 / Guangci Temple 大悲寺 / Dabei Temple (original name) 天台宗 / Tiantai sect 止观 / Zhiguan (samadhi and prajna) 头陀行 / Dhutanga (ascetic practice) 胡则 / Hu Ze (963-1039) 佑顺侯 / Youshun Marquis (Hu Ze title) 赫灵庙 / Heling Temple 摩尼教 / Manichaeism (Mingjiao) 方腊起义 / Fang La Uprising (1120) 神权君授 / Divine power granted by emperor 子孙派 / Descendant school (monastic system) 八房 / Eight houses (monastic management) 聿孤禅师 / Master Yugu (Wanli era abbot) 程方峰 / Cheng Fangfeng (1498-1585) ...等22个
3
广慈寺和胡公庙现状
中文

三、广慈寺和胡公庙现状

在二十世纪八十年代中叶，以发展旅游业为目的，胡公庙从广慈寺中被分离出来，作为一个独立的寺院，再次和广慈寺肩并肩地坐落在方岩山顶上。胡公庙在西侧，占据了广慈寺原有中轴线的位置，即以屏风阁为最后一进大殿。在屏风阁前刚刚建好没几年的大雄宝殿再次被拆除，取而代之的是两幢新建的胡公庙前殿和大殿。广慈寺则被迫向东平移，改以重建的观音殿为中轴线，并在后面补建了大雄宝殿。

广慈寺

这里的老人们都说，广慈寺以前的各个殿堂之间也都是有围廊相连的，下雨都不会湿鞋。不仅如此，允明禅师讲，以前从山脚到山顶都是有长廊的，是太平村的一个村民每年来修，用了很多年才建成。现任住持释明修也说，在他去"应聘"广慈寺住持的时候，也曾经见过一张广慈寺的图，上面画着从山下到寺院都有走廊相连。

如今的广慈寺有三进殿堂。一进前厅和二进观音殿为允明禅师于1984年复建；第三进殿堂是在寺、庙分离之后补建的大雄宝殿。观音殿与前厅有侧廊相连，中间围合成一个小的院落。由于是利用祠堂建筑的木料重建，所以无论从大木构件或小木雕饰中都可以看到世俗建筑的影子。一进前厅会让人不禁想起祠堂里的戏台。前厅正中间的神龛里目前供奉着弥勒佛，神龛本身看上去很像戏台上使用的建筑道具。神龛正上方是一个通常只用于祠堂戏台顶上的精致藻井，藻井里的斗拱呈螺旋形层叠上升。

边上两间各有一组九格天花，四周还有小斗拱做支撑，天花上绘制的都是《西游记》中的经典场面。壁画颜色以黄、绿色为底，线条精美，退晕渲染也十分讲究。观音殿和两侧围廊中祠堂建筑的痕迹就更加明显。这其中的大木构件不仅用神牛、寿桃、喜鹊做雕饰主题，还可以看到富有张力的月梁，极富装饰效果的"蟺梁"以及活灵活现的狮子舞球。广慈寺的这座建筑是中国建筑构造特点和设计理念的最好体现：一幢建筑不仅能拆了异地重建，而且还能把所有构建重新排列组合，建造成平面和功能都不尽相同的新建筑。

方岩山上另外一幢没有在文化大革命期间被拆毁的建筑就是位于现广慈寺东南角的僧房。这幢仅存的僧房是在清道光二十九年（1849年）的那场大火后重建的。僧房高三层，坡屋顶，平面为异形，中间却围合了一个四四方方的小院，院内正中是一个巨大的"放生池"，池上有小拱桥。说是放生池，其实其主要目的是预防火灾。最值得一提的是，僧房的外立面明显受到西方古典主义建筑的影响：通高的壁柱，三角形山墙，以及山墙中圆形的装饰母题。

胡公庙前殿和大殿

胡公庙的第一进殿是一个三开间的一层木建筑，挂"胡公祠"横匾。虽然我们无法区分建筑的哪些构建来自于拆掉的大雄宝殿，哪些来自于其他建筑，而哪些又是后来新做的，更不用说被允明禅师重建的大雄宝殿本身就是利用拆除后的一幢民居为建筑原料，但是这些都并不重要。乡土建筑的一大魅力就在于它创作中的自由性和追求物尽其用的特点，这种"重新组装"的做法不但延续了原有建筑物的生命，而且这种"再创造"本身就具有非常的意义。

前殿大木雕饰所采用的题材，都明显带有当地祠堂建筑的痕迹。例如，两个中榀檐柱的牛腿上就雕有当地祠堂建筑中通常采用的狮子戏球；牛腿上向各个方向飞扬伸展的叠拱和插叶所采用的卷草图案也和世远公祠里的非常相似。不过，前殿里最有特点的部分还要算天花。这里建筑的梁架通常是露明的，但是前殿的中榀的两个金柱中间却有天花，天花中间还形成一个八面锥体的藻井。藻井正中绘有盘龙，八个面上不但绘有如来佛、弥勒佛、观音、太上老君，还有《西游记》里的四个主要人物；四周的天花上则画满《西游记》中的一些主要故事情节。

可以说，在这几米见方的二维画板上，已经完全看不到佛教、道教与世俗尘世之间的任何界限。前殿的屋面应该是新的，但在这里人、神之间的界限也是模糊的。垂脊的末端各有一尊小的雕像，前面分别是哪吒、托塔李天王，后面则是两名天兵天将。

胡公庙大殿是一个用钢筋混凝土做支撑结构的两层高建筑，坐落在一个半米多高的基础上，俨然一副官式建筑的气派。大殿正中有一个两米多高、玉皇大帝扮相的胡公正襟端坐在一个一米多高的须弥座上，他略微低头，审视着跪倒在他面前的虔诚香客。依照惯例，这尊胡公是"吃荤"的，也就是说，三牲之类的供品都会摆放在须弥座前面的香案上。胡公的头顶上也有一个半球形的藻井，上面绘着龙和凤。硕大的一个殿堂，只有一尊塑像在中间，平时显得空荡荡的，但是在庙会期间，据说就会挤得水泄不通。

屏风阁

最后一进殿是屏风阁，现存建筑基本上是清道光二十九年（1849年）那场大火之后重建的。允明禅师说，重建的时候，一些主要构件改用石材以防火灾。可是到清朝末年，寺院变得十分不景气，原本的八房僧人也只剩下了四房，每房的人数也少了许多。于是就有人说是因为"狮子（石子的谐音）吃人"，所以在宣统年间（1909—1911年），屏风阁又改回了木构。

屏风阁也是利用天然岩洞建造的，但是它和罗汉洞或五峰书院有着根本的不同。屏风阁的建筑空间不但延伸到了石洞以外，而且还利用石洞上的巨石作为"楼板"，在上面加建了二层。允明禅师说，二层以前供的是玉皇大帝，直到宣统二年（1910年），才把玉皇大帝移到了现在的地藏殿。屏风阁一、二层之间没有任何垂直的联系，像搬佛像这样大的变故，可能只有在重建或大修的时候才可以进行。把这种可望而不可即的位置留给玉皇大帝本是无可厚非，但是在佛家圣地的正殿里高高供奉道教的"众神之皇"，这不得不归功于中国式"宗教"无与伦比的博大和宽容。

屏风阁和它前面殿堂之间的空间比较小，再加上这里总是烟雾缭绕，所以很难看清它的全貌。屏风阁正面原本有墙，香客通过左右两侧的拱形门洞进出。院墙和凹字形的建筑围合了一个小院，院内靠墙的一侧有香炉。后来因为香客太多，香炉离建筑太近，有火灾隐患，所以拆除了围墙。现在从屏风阁前面的铺地上还可以清楚地看到墙基和门的位置。

屏风阁的大木雕饰奢华精美到了极点。由于一层屋檐有两根挑檐枋承托，所以又多了许多可以用以雕饰的叠斗、穿子、雀替、垂花等构件。抬头审视檐下、柱头的这一圈，眼睛找不到可以小憩的地方：凤凰、牡丹、仙鹤、神仙……又一只凤凰。鎏了金的木雕，在经历了几十年，甚至上百年的烟熏之后，没有了最初的那种浮华亮丽，多了些凝重和斑驳。木雕中最抢眼的就是在两个中榀檐柱上的龙形"牛腿"。一条金龙盘旋在五彩的云海里，头上顶着蜀柱，龙身"穿过"檐柱，又变成一条短梁用以连接后面的蜀柱，短梁和两个梁托也都雕有云海的图案。

屏风阁里面，沿石洞内壁一共排列着大大小小的塑像二十三尊。正中间的一个当然是红面长须、笑容可掬的胡公大帝，他的两个儿子陪坐在两边，后面是他的三个夫人。据说胡则原本只有两个夫人，第三位只是猝死在胡公大帝面前的一位女香客，人们见她如此虔诚，便封她为胡公的第三任夫人。在胡公的右侧依次是关公、龙王和平安菩萨；左边则是文曲星、婚姻美满神和财神。除了婚姻美满神是一对夫妻塑像以外，其他每一个神仙和菩萨两边还配有侍从两人。

基本说来，这里可以满足人们生活中的一切所求，不论是求财还是求雨，求前程似锦还是求出入平安，抑或是求家庭美满还是求功名利禄。就算是各路神仙都帮不了忙，还可以求无所不能的胡公大帝。在每组神像前都有一个巨大的蜡烛台，上面插满了高高低低的蜡烛，胡公大帝面前的蜡烛更是多到看不见他的脸。在这黑黢黢的山洞里，扑朔迷离的光影变化加上烟雾缭绕的视觉效果，给诸神平添了些神秘的色彩。

据允明禅师说，自从把胡公从大雄宝殿请到屏风阁之后，他的后面一直有如来、文殊和普贤菩萨的三尊小塑像。如此说来，人们在祭拜胡公大帝的同时，其实是在求助如来佛祖的保佑。在香客眼里，胡公大帝是无所不能的一方神祇；在广慈寺眼里，无所不能的仍旧是胡则后面的如来佛祖，胡则只是能带给寺院旺盛人间香火的护法神。

English

3. Current State of Guangci temple and Hu Gong temple

In the mid-1980s, for the purpose of developing tourism, Hu Gong temple was separated from Guangci temple, becoming an independent temple, once again standing side by side with Guangci temple on top of Fangyan Mountain.

Hu Gong temple is on the west side, occupying Guangci temple's original central axis position, with Pingfeng Pavilion as the final hall. The Mahavira Hall that had just been built in front of Pingfeng Pavilion a few years earlier was again demolished, replaced by two newly built Hu Gong temple front hall and main hall.

Guangci temple was forced to shift eastward, changing to the rebuilt Guanyin Pavilion as its central axis, and adding a Mahavira Hall behind it.

Guangci temple

Elders here all say that in the past, the various halls of Guangci temple were also connected by surrounding corridors, so that one's shoes would not get wet in the rain.

Moreover, Master Yunming said that in former times there were long corridors from the foot of the mountain to the top, built by a villager from Taiping village who came to repair every year, taking many years to complete.

Current abbot Shi Mingxiu also said that when he went to "apply" for the position of Guangci temple abbot, he once saw a drawing of Guangci temple showing corridors connecting from the foot of the mountain to the temple.

Today's Guangci temple has three halls. The first hall (front hall) and the second hall (Guanyin Pavilion) were rebuilt by Master Yunming in 1984; the third hall is the Mahavira Hall added after the temple separation. Guanyin Pavilion and the front hall are connected by side corridors, enclosing a small courtyard in the middle.

Because they were rebuilt using timber from ancestral hall buildings, traces of secular architecture can be seen in both large wooden components and small wood carvings. The first front hall inevitably reminds one of the stage in an ancestral hall.

In the central shrine of the front hall, Maitreya Buddha is currently enshrined; the shrine itself looks much like a theatrical prop used on a stage. Directly above the shrine is an exquisite caisson ceiling usually only used above ancestral hall stages; the dougong (bracket sets) in the caisson rise in spiral layers.

The two side rooms each have a set of nine-grid ceilings, supported by small dougong around the perimeter; the ceilings are painted with classic scenes from "Journey to the West." The murals use yellow and green as base colors, with exquisite lines and very refined gradient rendering.

The traces of ancestral hall architecture are even more obvious in Guanyin Pavilion and the surrounding corridors.

Among them, the large wooden components not only use divine oxen, peaches of longevity, and magpies as carving themes, but also show tension-filled moon beams, highly decorative "shan beams," and vividly lifelike lions playing with balls.

This building of Guangci temple is the best embodiment of Chinese architectural construction characteristics and design concepts: a building can not only be dismantled and rebuilt in a different location, but also all its components can be rearranged and combined to construct new buildings with different plans and functions.

Another building on Fangyan Mountain that was not demolished during the Cultural Revolution is the monk quarter located at the southeast corner of the current Guangci temple. This only surviving monk quarter was rebuilt after the great fire in the twenty-ninth year of Qing Daoguang (1849).

The monk quarter is three stories high, with a pitched roof and an irregular plan, yet enclosing a square courtyard in the middle; in the center of the courtyard is a huge "free-life pond" with a small arched bridge over it. Called a free-life pond, its main purpose is actually fire prevention.

Most noteworthy is that the facade of the monk quarter was clearly influenced by Western classical architecture: full-height pilasters, triangular gables, and circular decorative motifs in the gables.

Hu Gong temple Front Hall and Main Hall

The first hall of Hu Gong temple is a three-bay single-story wooden building, hanging the horizontal plaque "Hu Gong shrine." Although we cannot distinguish which components of the building came from the demolished Mahavira Hall, which from other buildings, and which were newly made later — not to mention that the Mahavira Hall rebuilt by Master Yunming itself used a demolished dwelling as building material — none of this is important.

One of the great charms of vernacular architecture lies in the freedom of its creation and the pursuit of making the best use of everything; this "reassembly" approach not only extends the life of original buildings, but this "re-creation" itself is of extraordinary significance.

The themes used in the large wood carvings of the front hall clearly bear traces of local ancestral hall architecture.

For example, the ox-legs (bracket supports) on the two middle-frame eave columns are carved with lions playing with balls, commonly used in local ancestral hall architecture; the scrolling grass patterns used on the stacked brackets and inserted leaves extending in all directions from the ox-legs are also very similar to those in Shiyuan Gong Hall.

However, the most distinctive part of the front hall must be the ceiling. The beam framework here is usually exposed, but between the two gold columns of the middle frame in the front hall there is a ceiling, and in the center of the ceiling an eight-sided pyramidal caisson is formed.

In the center of the caisson is painted a coiling dragon; on the eight faces are painted not only Tathagata Buddha, Maitreya Buddha, Guanyin, and the Supreme Lord Lao Zi, but also the four main characters from "Journey to the West"; the surrounding ceilings are filled with major story episodes from "Journey to the West." It can be said that on this few-meter-square two-dimensional canvas, any boundary between Buddhism, Daoism, and the secular world has completely disappeared.

The roof of the front hall should be new, but here the boundary between humans and gods is also blurred. At the ends of the vertical ridges are small statues: in front are Nezha and Pagoda-Bearing Heavenly King Li, behind are two heavenly soldiers.

The main hall of Hu Gong temple is a two-story building using reinforced concrete as its supporting structure, sitting on a foundation over half a meter high, with the imposing air of an official-style building.

In the center of the main hall, a Hu Gong statue over two meters tall, dressed as the Jade Emperor, sits solemnly on a sumeru pedestal over one meter high; he slightly lowers his head, examining the devout pilgrims kneeling before him.

By convention, this Hu Gong "eats meat" — that is, offerings such as the three sacrificial animals are placed on the incense table in front of the sumeru pedestal. Above Hu Gong's head there is also a hemispherical caisson, painted with dragons and phoenixes.

The enormous hall has only one statue in the center, appearing empty on ordinary days, but during temple fairs it is said to be packed so tightly that water cannot pass through.

Pingfeng Pavilion (Screen Wind Pavilion)

The final hall is Pingfeng Pavilion; the existing building was basically rebuilt after the great fire in the twenty-ninth year of Qing Daoguang (1849). Master Yunming said that during reconstruction, some main components were changed to stone to prevent fire.

But by the late Qing dynasty, the temple became very不景气; the original eight houses of monks were reduced to four, and the number of people in each house also decreased greatly.

So some people said it was because "lion (homophone of stone) eats people," and therefore during the Xuantong period (1909-1911), Pingfeng Pavilion was changed back to wooden structure.

Pingfeng Pavilion was also built using a natural rock cave, but it is fundamentally different from Luohan Cave or Wufeng academy. The architectural space of Pingfeng Pavilion not only extends beyond the rock cave, but also uses the giant rock above the cave as a "floor slab," adding a second story on top.

Master Yunming said the second story formerly enshrined the Jade Emperor, and it was not until the second year of Xuantong (1910) that the Jade Emperor was moved to the current Ksitigarbha Hall.

There is no vertical connection between the first and second stories of Pingfeng Pavilion; a major change like moving a Buddha statue could probably only be done during reconstruction or major repair.

Leaving this unattainable position to the Jade Emperor is understandable, but enshrining the Daoist "Emperor of All Gods" high in the main hall of a Buddhist holy land must be attributed to the unparalleled broadness and tolerance of Chinese-style "religion."

The space between Pingfeng Pavilion and the hall in front of it is relatively small, and coupled with the constant smoke and incense here, it is difficult to see its full appearance. The front of Pingfeng Pavilion originally had walls, with pilgrims entering and exiting through arched doorways on the left and right sides.

The courtyard wall and the U-shaped building enclosed a small courtyard, with an incense burner on the wall side inside. Later, because there were too many pilgrims and the incense burner was too close to the building, posing a fire hazard, the courtyard wall was demolished.

Now the wall foundation and door positions can still be clearly seen on the paving in front of Pingfeng Pavilion.

The large wood carvings of Pingfeng Pavilion are luxuriously exquisite to the extreme. Because the first-story eave is supported by two eave-purlins, there are many more components available for carving: stacked brackets, penetrating pieces, sparrow braces, pendant flowers, etc.

Looking up at the circle under the eaves and column heads, the eyes find no place to rest: phoenix, peony, red-crowned crane, immortal… another phoenix. The gilded wood carvings, after decades or even a century of smoke exposure, have lost their original flashy brilliance and gained a sense of gravity and mottling.

The most eye-catching wood carvings are the dragon-shaped "ox-legs" on the two middle-frame eave columns.

A golden dragon coils in a sea of five-colored clouds, with a shu column (short column) on its head; the dragon body "passes through" the eave column and becomes a short beam connecting to the shu column behind; the short beam and two beam brackets are also carved with cloud-sea patterns.

Inside Pingfeng Pavilion, along the inner wall of the rock cave, a total of twenty-three statues of various sizes are arranged. The one in the center is of course the red-faced, long-bearded, smiling Hu Gong the Great; his two sons sit beside him, and behind him are his three wives.

It is said Hu Ze originally had only two wives; the third was just a female pilgrim who died suddenly before Hu Gong the Great; seeing her such devotion, people enfeoffed her as Hu Gong's third wife.

On Hu Gong's right side are依次 Guan Gong, Dragon King, and Peace Bodhisattva; on the left are Wenqu Star (God of Literature), Marriage Happiness God, and God of Wealth. Except for the Marriage Happiness God which is a couple statue, each other god and bodhisattva has two attendants on both sides.

Basically, this place can satisfy all requests in people's lives — whether seeking wealth or rain, seeking a bright future or safe travels, or seeking family happiness or fame and fortune. Even if all the various gods cannot help, one can still seek the omnipotent Hu Gong the Great.

In front of each group of statues there is a huge candlestick, filled with candles of various heights; the candles before Hu Gong the Great are so many that his face cannot be seen. In this dark cave, the confusing changes of light and shadow coupled with the visual effect of swirling smoke add a mysterious color to the gods.

According to Master Yunming, ever since Hu Gong was moved from the Mahavira Hall to Pingfeng Pavilion, behind him there have always been three small statues of Tathagata, Manjushri and Samantabhadra. Thus, while people worship Hu Gong the Great, they are actually seeking the protection of Tathagata Buddha.

In the eyes of pilgrims, Hu Gong the Great is an omnipotent local deity; in the eyes of Guangci temple, the omnipotent one remains Tathagata Buddha behind Hu Ze, and Hu Ze is merely a Dharma-protecting god who can bring the temple prosperous worldly incense.

本篇术语 Key Terms

广慈寺现状 / Current state of Guangci Temple 胡公庙 / Hu Gong Temple 屏风阁 / Pingfeng Pavilion (Screen Wind Pavilion) 藻井 / Caisson ceiling (zaojing) 九格天花 / Nine-grid ceiling 月梁 / Moon beam (yueliang) 蟺梁 / Shan beam (decorative beam) 牛腿 / Ox-leg bracket (niutui/corbel) 叠斗 / Stacked brackets (diedou) 雀替 / Sparrow brace (queti) 垂花 / Pendant flower (chuihua) 须弥座 / Sumeru pedestal (xumizuo) 胡公大帝 / Hu Gong the Great (deified Hu Ze) 三牲 / Three sacrificial animals 西游记 / Journey to the West ...等20个

第六章 五峰坳里的书院

1
寿山往事
中文

一、寿山往事

书院作为文教建筑的一种，是中国实行科举制度后的产物。科举制度开始于隋大业元年（605年），到清朝光绪三十一年（1905年）举行最后一科进士考试为止，是中国历代皇朝采用的取士制度。尽管科举制度有许多弊端和局限性，但是其最大意义就在于它给广大平民百姓提供了改变自己以至整个家族社会地位的唯一途径。于是乎，在科举制度实行的一千三百年里，中华大地上就出现了无以计数的学子，不计十年寒窗，孜孜苦读。相伴而来的，就是私塾、书院、书斋、文馆、文庙、文昌阁等文教建筑的广泛普及。

唐末至五代期间，战乱频繁导致官学衰败，所以许多读书人就避居山林，以模仿佛教、禅林的讲经制度创立书院。浙江境内始建于唐朝的书院，现知有五处，分别是象山蓬莱书院、诸暨溪山书院、龙游九峰书院、建德青山书院和绍兴丽正书院。这些书院通常是住宅的一部分，建筑形式也十分简朴。

宋代是书院林立的时代。在北宋期间书院多以讲学为主，而随着理学的发展，南宋的书院逐渐成为各学派活动的场所。书院作为一种正式的教育制度，几乎取代官学而成为当时主要的教育机关。浙江地区当时有书院三十余家，数量仅次于江西，居全国第二。

书院制度以及书院建筑在明、清两代又有了进一步的发展，但是已经逐渐从学者云集、自由讲学的私立书院，发展到受统治阶级控制的官学化书院。直至光绪二十七年（1901年），皇帝诏令各省的书院改为大学堂，各府、厅、直隶州的书院改为中学堂，各州县的书院改为小学堂，书院这种教育制度便退出了历史舞台。

在和尚、陈亮、县令、胡氏后人、国民党政府、还有我们这一行人进入寿山坑之前，坑里这五座山峰就已经屹立在那里了。有了这五座山峰，才有了美丽的传说和故事；再然后，才有了接下来所有的事情……

寿山在方岩北三里，有五峰皆石壁平地拔起，周围如城郭，曰固厚、曰瀑布、曰桃花、曰覆釜、曰鸡鸣。固厚之下有大石洞，高敞轩豁，可容千人……

瀑布之上有龙湫，水四时不竭，直下数十丈如练，及霁久水杀，飘飘飏之，溅洒四出若雾雨然，可望而不可即，亦奇观也。

固厚峰，主峰也。悬崖千仞，峭壁如削，较诸峰为淳固深厚，故其名。其下石洞在焉。

瀑布峰，在固厚之右，上有飞泉下注……

覆釜峰，在瀑布之下，巅广而圆，如大釜覆其上。

桃花峰，与覆釜峰相连，专专耸妙，其岩色赤白相间，状类桃花……

鸡鸣峰，……午风微过，嘐然一声，彼唱此应，遂神其说耳……

天蜜，……悬崖直下，天风偶吹，大者如珠，细者如雾，其味甘美故名。

位于五峰之间、石洞之中、瀑布之下的这块世外桃源，终于在南北朝时期的梁普通元年（520年）迎来了它的第一位主人，即吃素念经的和尚们。固厚峰下有两个大石洞，上文中提到的是西边这个，靠东还有一个，叫外洞。这些出家人利用这两个天然的石洞创建了永康县境内的第一座寺院——桃岩寺，并于日后改名为寿山寺。关于寿山寺的具体情况已经无从查证，只知道固厚峰下的这个山洞里曾经有过一个罗汉堂，里面的罗汉还是在明朝为建造五峰书院时撤掉的；外洞"之前有台三层，甚壮伟"，猜想或许是大雄宝殿的位置。据《库川胡氏宗谱》中关于一场官司的记录，"寿山寺自正德以前无论已"，那三层的高台也在"明万历间始圮"。

到了南宋乾道八年（1172年），永康人陈亮（1143—1195年）开始在寿山坑里设帐授学，宣讲他的"商籍农而立，农赖商而行"、"义利双行"和"各务其实"等有悖于传统儒学和经学的新思想。在接下去的两三年中，陈亮的挚友吕祖谦（1137—1181年），即事功学派下金华学派的创始人，也曾来这里讲学。宋淳熙九年（1182年）秋，刚刚上任一年的浙东提举朱熹（1130—1200年）回访陈亮于五峰。这是朱熹与陈亮的第二次会面，第一次是在这年正月，朱熹来婺州赈灾，陈亮赶去相见，并与朱熹畅谈十日。

第二次会面的盛况在《五峰书院志》中略有记载，陈亮"请晦翁主讲席，从游者数百人"。相传固厚峰下外洞岩壁上的"兜率台"三个朱砂大字就是朱熹当时题写的。这里的"兜率"二字同"兜率天"，为梵语音译，代表佛教中的第四层天，这层天的内院是弥勒菩萨的净土，外院是天上众生所居之处；"台"应该指的是当时在这里的三层高台。宋绍熙四年（1193年），年已五十一岁的陈亮金榜题名中了状元，却不幸在一年多后死在赴任的途中。

在这以后，我们无从查证寿山坑里是否依然回荡有士大夫高亢的辩论；或是仅留下与晨钟暮鼓相伴的木鱼声；还是一切都静了下来，只听得见水激顽石和风吹树叶的声音。

English

1. Past Events of Shoushan

Academies, as a type of cultural-educational architecture, are a product of China's imperial examination system.

The imperial examination system began in the first year of Sui Daye (605) and lasted until the last jinshi examination in the thirty-first year of Qing Guangxu (1905), serving as the scholar-selection system adopted by successive Chinese dynasties.

Although the imperial examination system had many drawbacks and limitations, its greatest significance was that it provided the broad common people with the only path to change their own and even their entire family's social status.

Thus, during the thirteen hundred years of the imperial examination system, countless scholars appeared across China, enduring ten years of hard study without counting the cost.

Accompanying this was the widespread普及 of cultural-educational architecture such as private schools, academies, study rooms, literary halls, Confucian temples, and wenchang pavilions.

During the late Tang to Five Dynasties period, frequent warfare led to the decline of official schools, so many scholars retreated to mountain forests and established academies modeled on Buddhist and Chan forest lecture systems.

In Zhejiang, there are currently five known academies founded in the Tang Dynasty: Xiangshan Penglai academy, Zhuji Xishan academy, Longyou Jiufeng academy, Jiande Qingshan academy, and Shaoxing Lizheng academy. These academies were usually part of residences, with very simple architectural forms.

The Song dynasty was an era of numerous academies. During the Northern Song, academies were mainly for lectures; with the development of Neo-Confucianism, Southern Song academies gradually became venues for various schools of thought.

As a formal educational system, academies almost replaced official schools as the main educational institutions of the time. Zhejiang region had over thirty academies at that time, second only to Jiangxi, ranking second nationwide.

The academy system and academy architecture further developed in the Ming and Qing dynasties, but gradually evolved from private academies with gathered scholars and free lectures into official-style academies controlled by the ruling class.

Until the twenty-seventh year of Guangxu (1901), the emperor ordered provincial academies to be converted into university halls, prefectural and departmental academies into middle school halls, and county academies into primary school halls; the academy educational system then exited the historical stage.

Before the monks, Chen Liang, the county magistrate, Hu descendants, the Nationalist government, and our group entered Shoushan Ravine, the five peaks in the ravine had already stood there. With these five peaks came beautiful legends and stories; and then came everything that followed……

Shoushan is three li north of Fangyan, with five peaks all of stone cliffs rising abruptly from the plain, surrounding like city walls: called Guhou (Solid-Thick), Pubu (Waterfall), Taohua (Peach Blossom), Fufu (Overturned Cauldron), and Jiming (Rooster Crow). Below Guhou is a large stone cave, high and spacious, capable of holding a thousand people……

Above the waterfall is Longqiu (Dragon Pool), its water never exhausted in all four seasons, plunging straight down several dozen zhang like white silk; when the weather clears and the water lessens, it flutters and drifts, splashing in all directions like mist and rain, visible but unreachable — also a wondrous sight.

Guhou Peak is the main peak. With thousand-ren cliffs and sheer walls like cut, compared to the other peaks it is pure, solid and deep, hence its name. Below it is the stone cave.

Pubu Peak, to the right of Guhou, has a flying spring pouring down from above……

Fufu Peak, below the waterfall, has a broad and round summit, like a large cauldron overturned on top.

Taohua Peak, connected to Fufu Peak, stands uniquely graceful; its rock color alternates red and white, resembling peach blossoms……

Jiming Peak, ……when the noon wind passes slightly, with a cry of "jiao," one answers the other, hence the myth……

Tianmi (Heavenly Honey), ……plunging straight down the cliff, occasionally blown by heavenly wind, large as pearls, fine as mist, its taste sweet and delicious hence the name.

This paradise located between the five peaks, within the stone caves, and below the waterfall finally welcomed its first masters in the first year of Liang Putong (520) during the Southern and Northern Dynasties — the vegetarian, scripture-chanting monks.

Below Guhou Peak there are two large stone caves; the one mentioned above is the western one, and to the east there is another called the Outer Cave. These monks used these two natural stone caves to found the first Buddhist temple in Yongkang County — Taoyan temple, later renamed Shoushan temple.

The specific circumstances of Shoushan temple can no longer be verified; we only know that there was once an Arhat Hall in this cave below Guhou Peak, and the arhats inside were removed when Wufeng academy was built in the Ming dynasty; the Outer Cave "previously had a three-tier platform, very grand," conjectured to perhaps be the location of the Mahavira Hall.

According to a record of a lawsuit in the "Kuchuan Hu clan Genealogy," "Shoushan temple before Zhengde is beyond discussion," and that three-tier high platform also "began to collapse during the Ming Wanli period."

In the eighth year of Southern Song Qiandao (1172), Chen Liang (1143-1195), a native of Yongkang, began setting up tents to teach in Shoushan Ravine, propagating his new ideas such as "merchants depend on farmers to stand, farmers rely on merchants to act," "righteousness and profit both pursued," and "each attends to reality" — ideas contrary to traditional Confucianism and classical studies.

In the following two to three years, Chen Liang's close friend Lü Zuqian (1137-1181), founder of the Jinhua School under the School of Practical Statecraft, also came here to lecture.

In the autumn of the ninth year of Song Chunxi (1182), Zhu Xi (1130-1200), who had just taken office as Eastern Zhejiang Commissioner for one year, visited Chen Liang at Wufeng.

This was the second meeting between Zhu Xi and Chen Liang; the first was in the first month of that year, when Zhu Xi came to Wuzhou for disaster relief and Chen Liang hurried to meet him, talking with Zhu Xi for ten days.

The grand occasion of the second meeting is briefly recorded in the "Wufeng academy Annals": Chen Liang "invited Master Huiweng (Zhu Xi) to give the main lecture, with hundreds of followers." Legend has it that the three large cinnabar characters "Doushuai Terrace" on the rock wall of the Outer Cave below Guhou Peak were inscribed by Zhu Xi at that time.

The word "Doushuai" here is the same as "Tusita Heaven," a Sanskrit transliteration representing the fourth heaven in Buddhism; the inner court of this heaven is Maitreya Bodhisattva's pure land, and the outer court is where heavenly beings dwell; "Terrace" should refer to the three-tier high platform that was here at the time.

In the fourth year of Song Shaoxi (1193), Chen Liang, already fifty-one years old, passed the examination as zhuangyuan (first-place jinshi), but unfortunately died over a year later on his way to take office.

After this, we cannot verify whether Shoushan Ravine still echoed with the impassioned debates of scholar-officials; or whether only the sound of wooden fish accompanied by morning bells and evening drums remained; or whether everything fell silent, with only the sound of water striking stubborn stones and wind blowing leaves.

五峰书院
中文

二、五峰书院

书院历史

再次找到有关于寿山坑的文字记载，已经到明正德年间（1506—1521年）。

尚宝丞应典，周视壁上有龙川书（今遍观壁上，但有时少章记龙川书，或在兜率台上，然无登览矣），志晦翁、东莱行迹，乃即堂东偏之隙建祠，以祀朱、吕、及张南轩、陆象山，而龙川配焉，曰丽泽祠。

丽泽祠，正德间应石门先生建，太守张公钺题此额，嗣守姚公文炤记……

应典字天彝，号石门，浙江永康芝英人，明正德（1506—1521年）九年（1514年）进士，曾任丽泽书院和白鹿书院山长。这也就是说丽泽祠应该建于1514年到1521年之间。这里的朱、吕、张指的是朱熹、吕祖谦和张栻，在宋乾道、淳熙时期（1165—1189年），他们三人被并称为"东南三贤"。除了这三个人之外，还以陆九渊和陈亮配祭。

"丽泽"这个名字，可能取自于位于金华明招山的丽泽书院。丽泽书院原名丽泽堂，为吕祖谦在宋乾道初年（约1165—1166年）创建。书院前有两个湖泊，而"丽泽"的原意为两个沼泽相连，所以恰到好处。更重要的是，《易·兑》中写道，"丽泽，兑。君子以朋友讲习"。由此可见，"丽泽"之意深远，朱熹还曾经做过如下的评价，"两泽相丽，互相滋益，朋友讲习，其象如此"。

十几年之后，丽泽祠东侧又建造了一幢规模稍大的建筑，取名为"桃岩丽泽精舍"。当时的永康知县洪坦写道，"嘉靖癸巳秋，余偕虚谷姚公访石门子于岩，览遗迹名胜，作而叹曰：……朱子欲屋之而未成，盖有所待也，乃命耆老吕瑗购材兴工，因其中岩下为楼数间，以广来学者，犹欲知先人丽泽之义，即名桃岩丽泽精舍"。嘉靖十五年（1536年），桃岩丽泽精舍建成，并改名为"五峰书院"。"五峰书院，嘉靖丙申太守姚公命耆老吕瑗建，原名桃岩丽泽精舍。邑侯洪坦记，嗣守陈公京书。"相比丽泽祠要盖在"堂东偏之隙"，桃岩丽泽精舍的建造则是以"撤去阿罗汉象"为代价的。

明末，永康学者周佑德在丽泽祠西侧又建造了一幢与丽泽祠规模相当，也是三间两层的建筑，取名为"学易斋"。

书院活动

在明朝最后的一百年里，有许多著名的学者都曾在五峰书院讲学，其中包括尚宝丞应典、江西抚州教授周桐、著名学者卢可久、周莹、应玠等人。这其中还有与岩下程氏同祖的三位程氏后人，他们是吏部左侍郎程文德（1497—1559年）、四川左布政程正谊（1531—1610年）和他的父亲程梓（1498—1585年）。来讲学的人大都是王阳明的弟子，讲习内容以倡导"致良知"学说为主，同时也包括"正心诚意"和程朱理学的部分内容。五峰书院在这段时间培养了许多优秀的人才，其中包括三十多位进士。

五峰书院除了"广来学者"和提供讲学场所之外，祭祀活动也是很重要的一个功能。丽泽祠创建之初共祀五人，但是程文德、应延育、周桐以陆九渊和张栻从未到过五峰为缘由，改仅祀朱熹、吕祖谦和陈亮三人。五峰书院则主祀"阳明王文成公；配祀石门应先生，方峰程先生"。学易斋的建造原本就是以纪念婺州学者何基、王柏、金履祥、许谦和章懋为初衷的。除了这五人之外，学易斋里还先后共配祀二十三人，其中不仅包括曾来五峰书院讲学的周桐、周莹等著名学者，还有程銈等当地乡绅。

随着五峰书院的名气越来越大，来赐额题联的名人也越来越多。清康熙二十二年（1683年），永康知县姬肇燕赐额"阐明理学"。乾隆四十五年（1780年），永康太平吕大宗祠和程、应、卢三姓后裔共同出资，重修五峰书院的三幢建筑，并请提督学使彭元瑞赐匾。次年一月彭元瑞亲自书写"丽泽如新"匾额。文楼村的程氏进士们也先后多次题写对联，例如"五峰书院"这幢建筑中就挂有程兆选题写的"学则数言矩矱遥承鹿洞；心传一脉渊源近溯姚江"，还有《五峰书院志》的作者程尚斐题写的"学术启良知，恍示鸢飞鱼跃；讲堂开胜地，何殊鹿洞鹅湖"。

进入清代中后期，讲学的气氛日渐衰退，每年的春秋两会便成了五峰书院最隆重的事情。会期各三天，届时前来讲学、奉祀的地方官绅多达数百人，其盛况不亚于朱熹来这里讲学的日子。关于"春会"的时间，《五峰书院志》里并没有提到，依惯例或许是在清明；秋天则在阴历九月初九，"每岁重阳日，祀朱、吕、陈三子，次日祀阳明王子，三日祀何、王、金、许诸儒"。关于祭祀的费用，则来自于应典、程梓和卢可久三人创置的会田，并由应、程、卢的后人负责料理会田和组织祭祀活动。

会田创立之后，陈亮、程文德、王柏和吕祖谦的后人也相继捐出了部分田地。但是由于会田的收入还要支付日常开销，以及建筑修缮的费用，所以依旧入不敷出。清康熙二十年（1681年），五峰书院不得不取消春会，并把秋会祭祀的时间改为阴历九月十二、十三两日。五峰书院"秋会"不仅包括启龛、迎神、读祝文、献礼、献饭等通常意义上的祭祀活动，更是各路学者交流和学习的绝好机会。为此会规里有明确的规定，"十二日早膳后各出质言以相参证，其式正楷大书，登名于尾。凡与会者各带纸笔，午后互相抄录完时，实粘壁上。

十三日早膳后，老成者各拈书一章，性理一章。三日内余功，或质疑问难、或阐发格言，言具登载于簿"。五峰书院"秋会"基本每年举行，一直由应、程、卢的后人负责，直到民国时期停办。

书院建筑

五峰书院的三幢建筑均位于固厚峰下能容千人的大石洞内，面南自东向西一字排开，均为三开间两层楼宇。其建筑的最大特点就是"即洞支木为之，不施椽瓦而雨雪霜露自然莫及"。丽泽祠的建造者应典还给这个山洞起名为"朝阳洞"，因为"朝日初出，苍苍凉凉，光射户牖"。

最东边的是"五峰书院"，其平面接近正方形，一层有廊，二层有栏以供凭眺。虽然屡经修缮，但是梁架结构并没有改变，所以仍然可以看到明代建筑的特点，例如梁架采用穿斗式，横梁不加装饰，梁下采用丁头拱，使用有覆盆的鼓形柱础等。建筑东墙外有一间小室，曾作为厨房。

丽泽祠和学易斋规模相当，都是进深较短的长方形平面，只是丽泽祠一层有廊。在门窗的处理上，学易斋和五峰书院较为类似，都是步步锦，丽泽祠则采用较为简单的"满天星"样式。

English

2. Wufeng academy

academy History

Finding written records about Shoushan Ravine again brings us to the Ming Zhengde period (1506-1521).

Shangbaocheng (Keeper of the Imperial Seal) Ying Dian, examining the walls, found Longchuan (Chen Liang) calligraphy (now observing the walls everywhere, but sometimes only Zhang's record of Longchuan calligraphy, or on Doushuai Terrace, yet no place to climb and view); recording the traces of Huiweng (Zhu Xi) and Donglai (Lü Zuqian), he then built a shrine in the gap east of the hall, to worship Zhu, Lü, as well as Zhang Nanxuan (Zhang Shi) and Lu Xiangshan (Lu Jiuyuan), with Longchuan (Chen Liang) as accompanying sacrifice — called Lize shrine.

Lize shrine, built by Mr. Ying Shimen during the Zhengde period, Prefect Zhang Yue inscribed this plaque, subsequent Prefect Yao Wenzhao wrote a record……

Ying Dian, courtesy name Tianyi, art name Shimen, a native of Zhiying, Yongkang, Zhejiang, became jinshi in the ninth year of Ming Zhengde (1514), and served as head of Lize academy and White Deer academy. This means Lize shrine should have been built between 1514 and 1521.

Here Zhu, Lü, and Zhang refer to Zhu Xi, Lü Zuqian, and Zhang Shi; during the Song Qiandao and Chunxi periods (1165-1189), these three were collectively called the "Three Sages of the Southeast." Besides these three, Lu Jiuyuan and Chen Liang were also worshipped as accompanying sacrifices.

The name "Lize" may have been taken from Lize academy located at Mingzhao Mountain in Jinhua. Lize academy was originally named Lize Hall, founded by Lü Zuqian in the early years of Song Qiandao (approximately 1165-1166). In front of the academy there were two lakes, and "Lize" originally meant two marshes connected, hence perfectly fitting.

More importantly, the "Book of Changes · Dui" states, "Lize, Dui. The superior man uses it for friends to discuss and practice." Thus it can be seen that "Lize" has profound meaning; Zhu Xi once made the following evaluation, "Two marshes adjacent, mutually nourishing and benefiting, friends discussing and practicing — such is its image."

Over a decade later, a slightly larger building was built east of Lize shrine, named "Taoyan Lize Jingshe" (Taoyan Lize Retreat). The then Yongkang County Magistrate Hong Tan wrote, "In the autumn of Jiajing Guisi (1533), I accompanied Mr.

Yao Xugu to visit Shimenzi at the rock, viewing relics and scenic spots, and sighed: ……Master Zhu wanted to build a house but did not complete it, perhaps waiting for something; so he ordered elder Lü Yuan to purchase materials and start construction, building several rooms under the middle rock to welcome coming scholars, still wishing to know the meaning of ancestors' Lize, hence named Taoyan Lize Jingshe." In the fifteenth year of Jiajing (1536), Taoyan Lize Jingshe was completed and renamed "Wufeng academy." "Wufeng academy, in Jiajing Bingshen (1536) Prefect Yao ordered elder Lü Yuan to build, originally named Taoyan Lize Jingshe.

County Marquis Hong Tan wrote record, subsequent Prefect Chen Jingshu calligraphy." Compared to Lize shrine which had to be built in "the gap east of the hall," the construction of Taoyan Lize Jingshe came at the cost of "removing the Arhat statues."

In the late Ming, Yongkang scholar Zhou Youde built another building west of Lize shrine, of similar scale to Lize shrine, also three bays and two stories, named "Xueyi Zhai" (Study of Changes Studio).

academy Activities

In the last hundred years of the Ming dynasty, many famous scholars lectured at Wufeng academy, including Shangbaocheng Ying Dian, Jiangxi Fuzhou Professor Zhou Tong, famous scholars Lu Kejiu, Zhou Ying, Ying Jie and others.

Among them were three Cheng descendants sharing the same ancestor as the Yanxia Cheng family: Cheng Wende (1497-1559), Left Vice Minister of Personnel; Cheng Zhengyi (1531-1610), Left Administrative Commissioner of Sichuan; and his father Cheng Zi (1498-1585).

Most of those who came to lecture were disciples of Wang Yangming; the lecture content mainly advocated the doctrine of "innate knowledge" (zhiliangzhi), while also including "rectifying the mind and making intentions sincere" and parts of Cheng-Zhu Neo-Confucianism.

During this period, Wufeng academy cultivated many outstanding talents, including over thirty jinshi.

Besides "welcoming coming scholars" and providing lecture venues, sacrificial activities were also a very important function of Wufeng academy.

Lize shrine originally worshipped five people at its founding, but Cheng Wende, Ying Yanyu, and Zhou Tong, citing that Lu Jiuyuan and Zhang Shi had never been to Wufeng, changed to worship only Zhu Xi, Lü Zuqian, and Chen Liang. Wufeng academy mainly worshipped "Wang Wencheng Gong of Yangming (Wang Yangming); accompanying sacrifices: Mr.

Ying Shimen, Mr. Cheng Fangfeng." The construction of Xueyi Zhai was originally intended to commemorate Wuzhou scholars He Ji, Wang Bai, Jin Lüxiang, Xu Qian, and Zhang Mao.

Besides these five, Xueyi Zhai successively had twenty-three accompanying sacrifices, including not only famous scholars such as Zhou Tong and Zhou Ying who had lectured at Wufeng academy, but also local gentry such as Cheng Ji.

As Wufeng academy's fame grew, more and more famous people came to grant plaques and inscribe couplets.

In the twenty-second year of Qing Kangxi (1683), Yongkang County Magistrate Ji Zhaoyan granted the plaque "Clarifying Neo-Confucianism." In the forty-fifth year of Qianlong (1780), the Yongkang Taiping Lü Grand ancestral hall and descendants of the Cheng, Ying, and Lu families jointly funded the renovation of the three buildings of Wufeng academy, and requested Education Commissioner Peng Yuanrui to grant a plaque.

In January of the following year, Peng Yuanrui personally wrote the plaque "Lize as New." The Cheng jinshi of Wenlou village also successively inscribed couplets multiple times; for example, the building "Wufeng academy" hangs a couplet by Cheng Zhaoxuan: "Learning follows few words, standards remotely inherited from Ludong; mind transmits one lineage, origin traced back to Yaojiang"; and another by Cheng Shangfei, author of the "Wufeng academy Annals": "Learning initiates innate knowledge, vaguely showing kites flying and fish leaping; lecture hall opens at a scenic spot, no different from Ludong and Ehu."

Entering the mid-to-late Qing dynasty, the atmosphere of lecturing gradually declined, and the annual spring and autumn meetings became the most solemn events of Wufeng academy.

Each meeting lasted three days, with local officials and gentry coming to lecture and worship numbering up to several hundred — the grand occasion no less than when Zhu Xi came here to lecture.

Regarding the time of the "Spring Meeting," the "Wufeng academy Annals" does not mention it; by convention it may have been at Qingming; autumn was on the ninth day of the ninth lunar month, "every year on Double Ninth, worship Zhu, Lü, and Chen three masters; next day worship Prince Yangming (Wang Yangming); third day worship He, Wang, Jin, Xu various Confucians." Regarding sacrificial expenses, they came from the meeting fields established by Ying Dian, Cheng Zi, and Lu Kejiu, and the descendants of Ying, Cheng, and Lu were responsible for managing the meeting fields and organizing sacrificial activities.

After the establishment of meeting fields, descendants of Chen Liang, Cheng Wende, Wang Bai, and Lü Zuqian also successively donated part of their fields. But because the income from meeting fields also had to cover daily expenses and building renovation costs, it still fell short of expenditures.

In the twentieth year of Qing Kangxi (1681), Wufeng academy had to cancel the Spring Meeting and change the Autumn Meeting sacrifice time to the twelfth and thirteenth days of the ninth lunar month.

Wufeng academy's "Autumn Meeting" not only included usual sacrificial activities such as opening shrines, welcoming gods, reading congratulatory texts, presenting gifts, and presenting meals, but was also an excellent opportunity for scholars of various schools to exchange and learn.

For this, the meeting rules had clear stipulations: "On the twelfth day after breakfast, each presents substantive words for mutual reference and verification, in formal regular script large characters, with name signed at the end. All participants bring paper and pen; after noon, after mutual copying is complete, actually paste on the wall.

On the thirteenth day after breakfast, the experienced each select one chapter of a book, one chapter of nature-principle.

Within the three days, remaining time may be for questioning and answering difficulties, or elucidating maxims; words are all recorded in the register." Wufeng academy's "Autumn Meeting" was basically held every year, always managed by descendants of Ying, Cheng, and Lu, until it was discontinued during the Republic period.

academy Architecture

The three buildings of Wufeng academy are all located in the large stone cave below Guhou Peak that can hold a thousand people, facing south and arranged in a row from east to west, all three-bay two-story buildings.

The greatest feature of their architecture is that they are "built by supporting wood in the cave, without applying rafters or tiles, yet rain, snow, frost and dew naturally cannot reach." Ying Dian, builder of Lize shrine, also named this cave "Chaoyang Cave" (Morning Sun Cave), because "when the morning sun first emerges, cool and clear, light shines through the windows and doors."

The easternmost is "Wufeng academy," with a nearly square plan, corridors on the first floor, and railings on the second floor for leaning and viewing.

Although repeatedly renovated, the beam framework structure has not changed, so Ming dynasty architectural features can still be seen, such as the beam framework采用 column-and-tie construction, horizontal beams without decoration, ding-tou brackets under beams, and drum-shaped column bases with overturned basins.

Outside the east wall of the building there is a small room that once served as a kitchen.

Lize shrine and Xueyi Zhai are of similar scale, both rectangular plans with shorter depth, except Lize shrine has corridors on the first floor. In the treatment of doors and windows, Xueyi Zhai and Wufeng academy are relatively similar, both using "step-by-step brocade" (bubujin) lattice patterns, while Lize shrine uses the simpler "sky full of stars" (mantianxing) pattern.

本篇术语 Key Terms
