3
胡公家庙
中文

三、胡公家庙

来龙去脉

五峰书院东边不远就是外洞，"此寿山寺旧刹也，今为胡公庙"。据《库川胡氏宗谱》记载，寿山寺在明正德（1506—1521年）以前就已经名存实亡，十多年后为了盖桃岩丽泽精舍又移走了洞里的罗汉像。

到了"嘉靖三十七年（1558年），奉例清查寿山废寺产业，买价入官"，一个叫徐一勤的人用五十一两白银买下了寿山坑里的五十七亩田、二十一亩多的地、十八亩多的山林和近九亩的塘。四年之后，胡氏的后人以寿山为"祖墓所在"，并且"寿山寺虽久废，所存田地山场等业系故祖胡士胜舍助，却被异姓官买"为由，开始了一场长达十八年之久的官司。这块田地山塘在这期间又被转手多次，从徐家转到程家，再从程家转到吕家，最终胡氏在万历八年（1580年）十一月，以一百九十两赎回。

万历三十六年（1608年），库川胡氏后人聚集在寿山坑里，决定利用洞口原有三层高台的基础建造一个"胡公家庙"。或许是广慈寺害怕这个"正宗"的胡公会抢了自己的香火，或许老街上的程氏也担心这个半路"杀"出来的胡公家庙会让老街从此变得萧条，于是一个叫如昌的僧人便把胡氏告上了公堂。从以程氏代管山林的"一审"结果来看，程氏应该也是这场官司的幕后支持或是怂恿者。胡氏当然不服，把官司告上了金华府，"二审"结果虽然保住了胡氏的田地山塘，但是建胡公家庙这件事，还是维持原判。原因是"方岩有像，四方香火供奉……寿山去彼一里许，又安得重建祠宇耶……惟将寿山新建未成者拆毁……"。

经过这次教训之后，远在胡库的胡氏似乎明白了要想在寿山坑站稳脚跟，就要讨好邻山广慈寺的道理，于是在清顺治十一年（1654年），胡氏慷慨资助广慈寺修理前厅。随后，在康熙二十二年（1683年），寿山坑里的胡公家庙就建成了。康熙四十一年（1702年），胡氏又捐田助寺。在这之后的一百多年里，胡氏不仅多次资助广慈寺翻修大殿，双方还协定"方岩祭礼约"。这种友好的关系一直延续到1840年，在程氏的介入下双方再次对簿公堂。虽然胡氏胜诉，但是广慈寺"于道光二十九年（1849年）九月初四合庙并僧房尽皆同禄"，胡氏认为这是天意，决定从此放弃方岩大殿，仅去寿山祭祀。

建筑

民国23年（1934年），胡氏在当时永康罗骏超的建议下开始筹划重建胡公家庙。重建工程自1935年始，至1937年完工，于是就有了今天矗立在固厚峰下的"重楼"。

新建成的胡公家庙坐落在一个用石材垒砌的高大基座上，基座东西两侧的地面高差有一层多高。它的一层有五间，二层、三层原本各三间。三层利用岩壁围合空间，岩壁上"兜率台"三个大字清晰可见。建筑一层和三层进深较浅，二层不但空间高，而且进深为前后二进，一直深入洞中。重楼在结构上采用砖木混合形式，外立面用砖墙做竖向承重结构，辅以木梁和木质门窗，内部做法比较传统，以木构为主。建筑式样上则明显受到"新时代"的影响，东侧入口处为砖砌发券，西洋式小门楼，二层三层栏杆的做法也显然是机器加工的产品。

民国27年（1938年），浙江省政府迁至方岩临时办公，其中财政厅进驻胡公家庙的二层和三层。例如因房间不够，二层两侧便被各增加一间；为解决采光的问题，所有的门窗还被装上了玻璃。国民政府进驻期间，胡公家庙还得匾"春帆楼"，省政府主席黄绍竑还亲自题联"庙貌壮灵岩，历宋元明清，史册相传，会栋宇更新，欣兹后起多才争辉谱谍；官廨邻胜境，仰聪明正直，风微若接，待湖山重整，好与斯邦父母共荐馨香"。

English

3. Hu Gong family temple

Origins and Development

Not far east of Wufeng academy is the Outer Cave, "this was the old site of Shoushan temple, now it is Hu Gong temple." According to the "Kuchuan Hu clan Genealogy," Shoushan temple had already existed in name only before the Ming Zhengde period (1506-1521); over a decade later, the Arhat statues in the cave were moved away to build Taoyan Lize Jingshe.

By "the thirty-seventh year of Jiajing (1558), following the order to investigate the properties of the abandoned Shoushan temple, the purchase price was paid into the official treasury," a man named Xu Yiqin bought fifty-seven mu of fields, over twenty-one mu of land, over eighteen mu of mountain forest, and nearly nine mu of ponds in Shoushan Ravine for fifty-one taels of silver.

Four years later, the Hu descendants, citing Shoushan as "where ancestral tombs are located" and that "although Shoushan temple has long been abandoned, the remaining fields, land, mountains and other properties were donated by ancestor Hu Shisheng, but were bought by an outsider through official channels," began a lawsuit lasting eighteen years.

During this period, the fields, land, mountains and ponds changed hands multiple times — from the Xu family to the Cheng family, then from the Cheng family to the Lü family — and finally the Hu family redeemed them in the eleventh month of the eighth year of Wanli (1580) for one hundred and ninety taels.

In the thirty-sixth year of Wanli (1608), the Kuchuan Hu descendants gathered in Shoushan Ravine and decided to build a "Hu Gong family temple" on the foundation of the original three-tier high platform at the cave entrance.

Perhaps Guangci temple feared this "authentic" Hu Gong would steal its incense, or perhaps the Cheng family on the old street worried that this Hu Gong family temple, appearing out of nowhere, would cause the old street to become desolate — so a monk named Ruchang sued the Hu family in court.

From the "first trial" result, in which the Cheng family managed the mountain forest on their behalf, it appears the Cheng family was also the behind-the-scenes supporter or instigator of this lawsuit.

The Hu family, of course, refused to accept it and appealed to Jinhua Prefecture; although the "second trial" result preserved the Hu family's fields, land, mountains and ponds, the matter of building the Hu Gong family temple still upheld the original verdict.

The reason was "Fangyan has the statue, with incense offerings from all directions …… Shoushan is about one li from there, how could another shrine be rebuilt …… only the unfinished new building at Shoushan should be demolished ……"

After this lesson, the Hu family, far away in Huku, seemed to understand that to gain a firm foothold in Shoushan Ravine, they must please the neighboring Guangci temple. So in the eleventh year of Qing Shunzhi (1654), the Hu family generously funded Guangci temple to repair its front hall.

Subsequently, in the twenty-second year of Kangxi (1683), the Hu Gong family temple in Shoushan Ravine was completed. In the forty-first year of Kangxi (1702), the Hu family again donated fields to assist the temple.

In the following hundred-plus years, the Hu family not only repeatedly funded Guangci temple to renovate its main hall, but both sides also agreed on the "Fangyan Sacrificial Ritual Pact." This friendly relationship continued until 1840, when with the intervention of the Cheng family, both sides again went to court.

Although the Hu family won the lawsuit, Guangci temple "on the fourth day of the ninth month in the twenty-ninth year of Daoguang (1849), the entire temple together with monk quarters was all destroyed by fire"; the Hu family considered this the will of heaven and decided from then on to abandon the Fangyan main hall, only going to Shoushan for sacrifices.

Architecture

In the 23rd year of the Republic (1934), the Hu family, under the suggestion of Luo Junchao of Yongkang at that time, began planning to rebuild the Hu Gong family temple. The reconstruction project began in 1935 and was completed in 1937, resulting in today's "Chonglou" (Multi-Story Tower) standing below Guhou Peak.

The newly built Hu Gong family temple sits on a tall stone-masonry base, with a ground level difference of over one story between the east and west sides of the base. Its first floor has five bays; the second and third floors originally had three bays each.

The third floor uses the rock wall to enclose space, with the three large characters "Doushuai Terrace" clearly visible on the rock wall. The first and third floors have shallow depths; the second floor not only has high space but also a depth of two front-to-back bays, extending deep into the cave.

The Chonglou adopts a mixed brick-wood structural form; the exterior facade uses brick walls as vertical load-bearing structure, supplemented by wooden beams and wooden doors and windows; the interior practice is relatively traditional, mainly wooden construction.

The architectural style was clearly influenced by the "new era": the east entrance is a brick arch with a Western-style small gatehouse, and the railings on the second and third floors are obviously machine-made products.

In the 27th year of the Republic (1938), the Zhejiang Provincial Government moved to Fangyan for temporary office work, with the Finance Department stationed on the second and third floors of the Hu Gong family temple.

For example, due to insufficient rooms, one bay was added to each side of the second floor; to solve lighting problems, all doors and windows were fitted with glass.

During the Nationalist Government's occupation, the Hu Gong family temple also received the plaque "Chunfan Tower" (Spring Sail Tower), and Provincial Government Chairman Huang Shaohong personally inscribed a couplet: "The temple's appearance strengthens the spiritual rock, through Song, Yuan, Ming and Qing, passed down in history annals; with the hall renewed, rejoice that later talents rise to glorify the genealogy; the government office neighbors the scenic spot, looking up to intelligence and integrity, the subtle influence seems close; when the lakes and mountains are restored, we may together offer fragrance with the parents of this land."

本篇术语 Key Terms

胡公家庙 / Hu Gong Family Temple 重楼 / Chonglou (Multi-Story Tower) 外洞 / Outer Cave 寿山寺 / Shoushan Temple 库川胡氏 / Kuchuan Hu Clan 春帆楼 / Chunfan Tower (Spring Sail Tower) 黄绍竑 / Huang Shaohong (Zhejiang governor) 砖木混合结构 / Mixed brick-wood structure 西洋式门楼 / Western-style gatehouse 方岩祭礼约 / Fangyan Sacrificial Ritual Pact
4
没有了进士的五峰书院
中文

四、没有了进士的五峰书院

没有了科举考试，没有了进士，书院曾经举办的各种活动在民国时期就全部停止了。与胡公家庙一起，五峰书院在民国27年（1938年）到31年（1942年），也曾作为省政府的临时办公场所。或许是因为近千年来，曾经在这里徘徊的进士和学者们都久久不愿离去，所以政府工作人员走后的第一年，五峰书院就作为永康师范学校的校舍，一直到1962年。

如今，学生们也走了。空荡荡的五峰书院变成了展览馆，设有书院历史、书院制度、书院功能、书院与文房四宝、书院与科举制度、五峰书院与永康学派等专题展览，甚至还有祭堂。瀑布下、山洞里、水塘边还设有茶室，倒也颇具些意境。但是不知怎的，没有了进士和学者们穿梭其间，没有了"晦翁主讲席，从游者数百人"的热闹场面，没有了"或质疑问难、或阐发格言"的高声论辩，五峰书院便寂静的可怕。静得连偶尔来参观的游客，都不愿意在这本应该是诗意盎然的茶室里小坐，便毅然转身离去，只留下他们匆匆的脚步声回荡在寿山坑里。

English

4. Wufeng academy Without jinshi

Without the imperial examination, without jinshi, the various activities once held by the academy all ceased during the Republic period. Together with the Hu Gong family temple, Wufeng academy also served as a temporary office for the provincial government from the 27th year (1938) to the 31st year (1942) of the Republic.

Perhaps because for nearly a thousand years, the jinshi and scholars who once wandered here were reluctant to leave for a long time, in the first year after the government staff left, Wufeng academy became the school building of Yongkang Normal School, remaining so until 1962.

Today, the students have also left. The empty Wufeng academy has become an exhibition hall, with thematic exhibitions on academy history, academy system, academy functions, academies and the four treasures of the study, academies and the imperial examination system, Wufeng academy and the Yongkang School, and even a sacrificial hall.

Below the waterfall, in the caves, and by the pond, tea rooms have also been set up, which does have some poetic charm.

But somehow, without jinshi and scholars穿梭其间, without the lively scene of "Huiweng (Zhu Xi) giving the main lecture with hundreds of followers," without the loud debates of "questioning and answering difficulties, or elucidating maxims," Wufeng academy becomes terrifyingly silent.

So silent that even occasional visiting tourists are unwilling to sit for a while in this tea room that should be full of poetry, but resolutely turn and leave, leaving only their hurried footsteps echoing in Shoushan Ravine.

本篇术语 Key Terms

永康师范学校 / Yongkang Normal School 展览馆 / Exhibition hall 文房四宝 / Four treasures of the study 永康学派 / Yongkang School

第七章 建筑的雕饰与装修

1
大木作雕饰
中文

一、大木作雕饰

老街上的建筑装饰，主要以木雕为主，石雕和砖雕的应用并不普遍。彩画虽然曾经被广泛应用在砖墙的檐下，但是能历经风雨保留下来的却很少。祠堂里的大木雕饰使用范围广泛，而且通常都非常精美。但是由于祠堂的主要空间均开敞，所以小木装修仅在厢房上才会使用，多数比较简单。住宅中的精美木雕主要位于门、窗、栏杆等处，部分住宅中的"马腿"、插叶、梁下巴等小型大木构件也雕饰得独具匠心、美轮美奂。

老街上过去由于家家都在忙着做香客的生意，所以没有什么匠人，过去盖房子需要的匠人都要从东阳请。解放后日子困难了，老街上的男孩子们才开始背井离乡出门学手艺，而这其中又以铁匠居多。老街上现在只有一位做大木的木匠，叫程高明（1942年生，第22代，圣字辈）。

马腿

"马腿"即江南民居中通常所说的"牛腿"，它位于檐柱上方，向天井垂直出挑。"马腿"的下半部分固定在马腿垫和檐柱上，用以承托上面的金枋。

金枋一般分为两部分，后面一段雕饰比较简单，通常只沿轮廓做几道线脚，内部辅以花卉题材为主的浅浮雕。但是这里也可以作为精雕细琢的重点，程昭夫祠堂中的部分金枋两侧就雕有生动的人物故事，绍常祠堂的一些金枋上则雕有三维的亭台楼阁。金枋尽端一般都有一个和金枋截面大小相当的一组三维人物或鸟兽，它们通常呈向庭院内伸展或端望的态势。金枋上面是金枋垫，上面承载着平行于立面的叠拱和沿进深方向的插叶。如果叠拱或插叶采用卷草纹造型，则金枋垫下面的这部分金枋垫还会做成花篮形状，使整个构件更为生动统一。

通体透雕的"马腿"可以说是整幢建筑中最精美的一块木雕。这个构件基本上只是对金枋起到一个斜撑的作用，所以它的上半部分，甚至整块木雕都可以和后面的柱子脱离，富于想象的匠人便利用到了这个特点，因地制宜地把部分雕刻内容也"转"到了"马腿"的背面。这样一来整组雕刻便显得更为生动，特别是对于雕有一组人物故事的"马腿"，如此处理后还增加了人物的立体感。

这不禁让人想到希腊神庙三角山墙上的人物石雕，这种群雕的最大特点之一就是所有人物的姿态，以及人与人之间的相互关系，都能自然贴合屋顶的斜线，于是每一组山墙上的群雕都成了雕塑构图中的精品。比起希腊神庙三角山墙上的石雕，雕"马腿"的难度就要大多了。不仅所有的雕刻内容要控制在一个厚度单一的倒楔形体内，而且还要照顾到从正面和两侧三个面的视觉效果。由于人的视线较低，所以"马腿"的设计还会利用楔形的这个斜面和下面的观望者产生一种交流。

简单一点的"马腿"会雕"三狮戏球"，类似的母题一般用于祠堂中榀的两个檐柱上。世远公祠里的"马腿"是以一头成年狮子作为木雕的主体，它前腿着地，后腿高抬，守着眼前的绣球；它毛发冲天，略微低头，虎视眈眈地注视着下面的来访者。两只小狮子则在"马腿"整体空间构成的"空隙"中玩耍嬉戏，其实从某种意义上来讲，小狮子的位置和姿态才是设计的难点。

复杂的"马腿"会雕一组人物，并辅以相应的背景。程昭夫祠堂正堂中榀前檐柱上的"马腿"，一边是"王母骑凤"下凡，另一边是"玉皇大帝乘龙"到人间，跟随他们俩的还各有七个和五个辅助人物，分别是侍女、随从、天兵天将等人。王母和玉皇大帝等主要人物均面向中间，并稍适低头；他们身后还各有一个天兵驻守在"马腿"的另一侧，左盼右顾；更让人不禁拍案叫绝的是，在"马腿"的外下角，各有两个侍从，或低头、或坐、或弯腰、或俯身，似乎在暗自体察凡界中的冷暖是非。

这些人物造型，个个都精雕细琢，惟妙惟肖；人物和人物之间主次分明，空间层次清楚。更难得的是，整组雕刻不但在立面上形成一个倒楔形，而且全部人物都控制在一个十多厘米的厚度里。

马腿垫上的雕饰一般比较简单，通常沿替木外围勾勒出一些线脚，内部浅雕花卉，或刻上诸如福、禄、寿之类的吉祥字眼，有的则是用几个"马腿"上的替木组成一句四字成语。

随着"马腿"的装饰性越来越大，它的设计也越来越灵活。在很多情况下，马腿垫和"马腿"可以简化为一块木头，只是在靠下的部分做了一个木楔子，插到柱子里。这样做的好处是在设计上可以比较自由，而且又多了一个面可以发挥匠人的创造性。程昭夫祠堂天井角部两个"马腿"的雕刻主题均为"仙人骑鱼"，鱼头向下，做纵身跃起状。"马腿"下原有的垫木在这里演化成了翻滚的浪花，如此一来，"鱼跃龙门"的态势便更加栩栩如生。胡公庙里的"盘龙马腿"则是用统一的图案把金枋、"马腿"和马腿垫连成一体，金枋雕龙头，"马腿"为龙身，马腿垫和后面的短梁化成彩云，整个"马腿"浑然一体，颇具匠心。

梭梁

梭梁的中部微微向上拱起，形如月亮，所以也被称为月梁。梭梁被普遍使用在祠堂的主要开敞空间里，还有部分住宅的檐廊下，甚至是厅堂里。梭梁上的雕饰并不多，基本只是集中在梁头两侧的"寥寥几刀"。但是不得不承认，不论是梭梁两侧弧形的截面，还是它两端卷杀的本身，就已经是绝美的雕刻作品。

梭梁两端卷杀的部分又被称作气堂，这里气堂上的雕饰有几种不同的做法。最简单的就是在梭梁两端雕上三道凹槽，这三条曲线仿佛是有弹性的梭梁在受力时形成的"褶皱"。雕饰讲究的建筑，或是跨度大一点的梭梁，凹槽末端还会衔接上一小束浅浅的阳刻卷草纹饰。这些纹饰紧紧地贴在梭梁尽端，仿佛是从柱子和梭梁的接缝中生长出来的。如果这样还不能尽兴，还可以在梁身上做文章。义丰雅记正堂中的五架梁，不但梭梁尽端的雕饰分外妖娆、极富装饰性，在凹槽的另外一侧又伸出一支更为舒展的卷草纹饰。

程立重宅院一进院正堂中榀五架梁梁头的雕饰虽然比较简单，但是气堂内侧的卷草花纹则有半米多长。一些院落的檐下梭梁中部还雕有凤凰、蝙蝠、画卷、扇面等图案。

梭梁两端气堂的做法在不同时期也会略有不同。建造年代较早的建筑，如作勳公祠和世远公祠梭梁气堂中的凹槽较浅，凹槽和梭梁尽端阳刻卷草花纹的衔接也比较自然。相比而言，一百多年后建造的程昭夫祠堂中的梭梁，气堂的凹槽较深，其截面呈三角形，与阳刻卷草花纹的过渡也比较生硬，甚至凹槽本身的曲线也缺乏了那种柔韧之美，变得"硬邦邦"的。

梁下巴

梁下巴，即通常所说的梁托。它的雕刻通常比较程式化：在侧面沿轮廓刻一两道线脚，里面雕上一些简单的花卉，或是阳刻一个吉祥的字，大跨梁架下的梁下巴还可能在曲形底面上浅刻一些纹饰。老街上不论是祠堂还是住宅中的梁下巴大体都如此模式，唯独义丰南院正房檐廊下的四组梁下巴与众不同。这些梁下巴中的雕饰以城市或建筑为主题，并配有花草树木、人物鸟兽。这些建筑本身不但是三维立体的，而且建筑和其他事物之间的空间关系，在视觉上也表现得非常正确。

这些梁下巴中所雕刻的建筑类型既有两进的农家小院，又有带长廊的深宅大院；既有小型的亭台楼阁，又有建筑在高台上的城楼殿宇；不仅如此，还有各式的拱桥和渔船若干。其雕刻的精细程度，更是让人叹为观止，三四厘米高的"房子"不但门、窗比例正确，而且可以看到屋面上的瓦线和屋檐下的雀替。就连通常只是一条凹槽的梁下巴轮廓线脚，在这里也变成了几十个紧密排列的"花瓣"。梁下巴的曲形底面上还深雕有精美菊花。

蟺梁

程高明老人说，他不知道"转梁"中的"转"字如何写，只听人说"转"字代表一种动物，由此推断可能是"蟺梁"。蟺梁通常位于梁架结构靠上的部分，一端固定在柱子上，用来在纵向上牵引支撑桁条的叠拱。蟺梁一般只在祠堂建筑中使用，老街上仅有绍常祠堂、程昭夫祠堂和周氏宗祠使用了这用蟺梁。即便如此，这几幢建筑只有在最重要的空间，即正堂的梁架结构中才会使用到蟺梁，由此可见其"级别"之高。在这三幢祠堂建筑中，绍常祠堂中的蟺梁好似盘龙吐水，程昭夫祠堂里的也雕有一个龙头，只有周氏宗祠仅采用抽象的卷纹。

同一幢建筑中的蟺梁造型相似，只是大小会根据桁条和桁条之间的距离做相应的调整。

程高明老人讲，通常如果用"三抽"，即三架梁，就不用蟺梁。绍常祠堂正堂，中榀主要采用抬梁式结构，所以用"三抽"代替正脊下的蟺梁。边榀则为穿斗结构，即采用蟺梁而没有"三抽"。除此之外，每两个桁条或桁枋之间都有蟺梁。每个盘龙形蟺梁都接近一个圆形，龙身高高拱起，视觉上仿佛在支撑着上面的椽子，整个造型极富张力。龙身虽然向屋脊方向盘旋，但是龙头却高高昂起望向檐口，而且有五彩的水花从嘴里喷出。龙身由红、白、蓝、绿等颜色逐层退晕描画，龙身上还有彩绘花饰。

程昭夫祠堂的蟺梁相对抽象，只能通过一双炯炯有神、望向地面的龙眼来确定整条龙的姿态。正堂中榀虽然也用了"三抽"，但是为了造型的统一，在"三抽"上依旧用蟺梁做雕饰。这组蟺梁已经没有结构作用，再加上"三抽"和椽子之间的空间矮小，所以这组蟺梁的尾部向檐口方向旋绕，整体看上去好像"躺"了下来。

周氏宗祠里的蟺梁在外形上与程昭夫祠堂里的蟺梁相似，基本上都呈上大下小的椭圆形，但是它们没有了龙头，梁上的线脚也更为简洁，而且刻槽较浅，使得整个构件造型比较饱满，不乏质朴之美。

米梁、桁条、挑檐枋

米梁是指位于柱子之间的横向结构部件。桁条即檩子，如果截面接近方形也被称作桁枋。这些构件和挑檐枋都是主要的承重结构，一般不做任何雕饰，但是有些时候，这些构件的底面也会成为雕饰的载体。通常来说，这类雕饰只存在于祠堂建筑中，因为这里主要空间的梁架均露明，所以米梁、桁条和挑檐枋抬头可见。周氏宗祠拜厅天井一侧檐柱和金柱之间的米梁上都雕有浅浅的抽象的几何图案。程昭夫祠堂正堂的两个上金桁枋、前下金桁枋和檐枋，还有天井四周的挑檐枋底面都浮雕花草、凤凰、仙鹤、飞鸟、鱼纹和狮子戏球等图案。

绍常祠堂在同样的位置也有雕饰：上金桁枋对称雕饰彩色勾连花卉；前下金桁枋和挑檐枋上各透雕一组花卉；檐枋上的透雕鱼纹、花鸟更是精美绝伦。据程高明老人讲，桁条一般都做成圆形，但是为了能在上面做雕饰，所以改成方形。雕饰的部分通常需要额外加上两层、甚至是三层木板，层数越多，越能雕出通透的感觉。

其他

雀替、叠拱、插叶、梁斗、梁斗垫、柱斗等部位也可以成为大木雕饰中的重点。叠拱和插叶是位于米梁、桁条或枋下面一组相互垂直的构件。支撑它们的可以是位于柱子上方的柱斗，也可以是位于梭梁上的梁斗。这些部位采用的雕饰母题多为卷草、花卉或抽象的回纹图案，有时候还会辅以寓意吉祥的字。

English

1. Large Timber carvings and decorations

Architectural decoration on the old street is mainly wood carving; stone carving and brick carving are not commonly applied. Although painted decorations were once widely used under the eaves of brick walls, very few have survived the wind and rain. Large wood carvings in ancestral halls are widely used and usually very exquisite.

However, because the main spaces of ancestral halls are all open, small wood joinery is only used in the wing rooms, mostly relatively simple.

Exquisite wood carvings in residences are mainly located at doors, windows, railings and other places; in some residences, small large-timber components such as "horse legs" (corbels), inserted leaves, and beam brackets are also carved with unique ingenuity and beauty.

In the past, because every family on the old street was busy doing business with pilgrims, there were few craftsmen; craftsmen needed for building houses in the past had to be hired from Dongyang. After liberation, when times became hard, boys on the old street began to leave home to learn trades, and among them blacksmiths were the majority.

There is now only one carpenter doing large timber work on the old street, named Cheng Gaoming (born 1942, 22nd generation, Sheng character generation).

Horse Legs (corbels)

"Horse leg" is what is commonly called "ox leg" (niutui) in Jiangnan residences; it is located above the eave column, projecting vertically toward the courtyard. The lower part of the "horse leg" is fixed on the horse-leg pad and eave column, used to support the gold beam (jinfang) above.

The gold beam is generally divided into two parts; the rear section has relatively simple carving, usually only a few moldings along the outline, with shallow relief of floral themes inside.

But this can also be the focus of meticulous carving: some gold beams on both sides in Cheng Zhaofu ancestral hall are carved with vivid human stories, while some gold beams in Shaochang ancestral hall are carved with three-dimensional pavilions and towers.

The end of the gold beam generally has a group of three-dimensional figures or birds and beasts of the same size as the gold beam cross-section, usually in a posture extending toward the courtyard or gazing inward.

Above the gold beam is the gold-beam pad, carrying stacked brackets parallel to the facade and inserted leaves along the depth direction. If the stacked brackets or inserted leaves use scrolling grass patterns, the part of the gold-beam pad below will also be made into a flower basket shape, making the entire component more vivid and unified.

The fully openwork-carved "horse leg" can be said to be the most exquisite wood carving in the entire building.

This component basically only serves as a diagonal brace for the gold beam, so its upper part, even the entire wood carving, can be detached from the column behind; imaginative craftsmen took advantage of this feature, adapting to local conditions by "turning" part of the carving content to the back of the "horse leg." This makes the entire group of carvings more vivid, especially for "horse legs" carved with a group of human stories; this treatment also increases the three-dimensionality of the figures.

This inevitably brings to mind the human figure stone carvings on the triangular pediments of Greek temples; one of the greatest features of such group carvings is that the postures of all figures, and the relationships between them, naturally conform to the sloping lines of the roof, so each group of pediment carvings becomes a masterpiece of sculptural composition.

Compared with stone carvings on Greek temple triangular pediments, carving "horse legs" is much more difficult. Not only must all carving content be controlled within a single-thickness inverted wedge-shaped body, but also the visual effects from the front and both sides must be considered.

Because people's line of sight is low, the design of "horse legs" also uses this wedge-shaped slope to create a kind of communication with the viewers below.

Simpler "horse legs" are carved with "three lions playing with a ball"; similar motifs are generally used on the two eave columns of the middle frame in ancestral halls.

The "horse legs" in Shiyuan Gong Hall use an adult lion as the main subject of the wood carving, with its front legs on the ground and hind legs raised high, guarding the embroidered ball before it; its hair bristles upward, head slightly lowered, glaring fiercely at the visitors below.

Two lion cubs play and frolic in the "gaps" formed by the overall space of the "horse leg"; in fact, in a sense, the position and posture of the cubs are the difficult part of the design.

Complex "horse legs" are carved with a group of figures, supplemented by corresponding backgrounds.

The "horse legs" on the front eave columns of the middle frame of the main hall in Cheng Zhaofu ancestral hall have, on one side, the "Queen Mother riding a phoenix" descending to earth, and on the other side, the "Jade Emperor riding a dragon" coming to the human world; following them are seven and five auxiliary figures respectively, namely maids, attendants, heavenly soldiers and generals.

The main figures such as the Queen Mother and Jade Emperor all face the center, with heads slightly bowed; behind them each has a heavenly soldier guarding the other side of the "horse leg," looking left and right; even more amazing is that at the outer lower corners of the "horse legs," there are two attendants each, either bowing heads, sitting, bending, or leaning, seemingly secretly observing the warmth and cold, right and wrong of the mortal world.

These figure models are all meticulously carved, lifelike; between figures, primary and secondary are distinct, spatial layers clear. What is even rarer is that the entire group of carvings not only forms an inverted wedge on the facade, but all figures are controlled within a thickness of just over ten centimeters.

The carvings on horse-leg pads are generally relatively simple, usually outlining some moldings along the periphery of the substitute wood, with shallow-carved flowers inside, or auspicious characters such as fortune, prosperity, and longevity carved in relief; some use several substitute woods on the "horse legs" to form a four-character idiom.

As the decorative nature of "horse legs" grew, their designs became increasingly flexible. In many cases, the horse-leg pad and "horse leg" can be simplified into a single piece of wood, with only a wooden wedge made in the lower part to insert into the column.

The advantage of this is greater design freedom, and one more surface on which to exert the craftsman's creativity. The carving theme of the two "horse legs" at the courtyard corners of Cheng Zhaofu ancestral hall is both "immortal riding a fish," with the fish head downward in a leaping posture.

The original pad wood below the "horse leg" here evolved into rolling waves, making the "fish leaping through the dragon gate" posture even more lifelike.

The "coiled dragon horse leg" in Hu Gong temple uses a unified pattern to connect the gold beam, "horse leg," and horse-leg pad into one body: the gold beam is carved as a dragon head, the "horse leg" as the dragon body, and the horse-leg pad and short beam behind transform into colorful clouds; the entire "horse leg" is浑然一体, quite ingenious.

Shuttle Beams (Moon Beams)

The middle part of a shuttle beam arches slightly upward, shaped like a moon, hence also called moon beam. Shuttle beams are commonly used in the main open spaces of ancestral halls, under the eaves of some residences, and even in halls.

There are not many carvings on shuttle beams, basically concentrated only in the "few strokes" on both sides of the beam head. But it must be admitted that whether it is the curved cross-section on both sides of the shuttle beam or the rolling cuts at both ends itself, it is already a beautiful carving work.

The rolling-cut parts at both ends of the shuttle beam are also called qitang (air hall); there are several different practices for the carvings here. The simplest is to carve three grooves at both ends of the shuttle beam; these three curves seem like "wrinkles" formed when the elastic shuttle beam is under load.

In buildings with exquisite carvings, or shuttle beams with larger spans, the ends of the grooves also connect with a small束 of shallow positive-carved scrolling grass patterns. These patterns cling tightly to the ends of the shuttle beam, as if growing out of the joint between the column and shuttle beam.

If this is still not enough, one can also work on the beam body. The five-frame beam in the main hall of Yifeng Yaji not only has particularly enchanting and highly decorative carvings at the shuttle beam ends, but also extends a more舒展 scrolling grass pattern on the other side of the groove.

Although the carvings at the beam heads of the five-frame beam on the middle frame of the main hall in the first courtyard of Cheng Lizhong Mansion are relatively simple, the scrolling grass pattern on the inner side of the qitang is over half a meter long.

The middle parts of shuttle beams under the eaves of some courtyards are also carved with phoenixes, bats, painting scrolls, fans and other patterns.

The practice of qitang at both ends of shuttle beams also varies slightly in different periods.

In buildings of earlier construction dates, such as Zuoxun Gong Hall and Shiyuan Gong Hall, the grooves in the shuttle beam qitang are shallower, and the connection between the grooves and the positive-carved scrolling grass pattern at the shuttle beam ends is more natural.

In comparison, in the shuttle beams of Cheng Zhaofu ancestral hall built over a hundred years later, the qitang grooves are deeper, with triangular cross-sections, and the transition to the positive-carved scrolling grass pattern is more rigid; even the curve of the groove itself lacks that flexible beauty, becoming "stiff."

Beam Chin (beam bracket)

Beam chin, commonly known as beam bracket (liangtuo). Its carving is usually quite formulaic: on the side, one or two moldings are carved along the outline, with some simple flowers carved inside, or an auspicious character in positive relief; beam chins under large-span beam frames may also have shallow-carved patterns on the curved bottom surface.

Whether in ancestral halls or residences on the old street, beam chins generally follow this pattern, with the sole exception of the four groups of beam chins under the eave corridor of the main house in Yifeng South courtyard.

The carvings in these beam chins take cities or buildings as themes, supplemented by flowers, plants, trees, figures, birds and beasts. These buildings themselves are not only three-dimensional, but the spatial relationships between buildings and other things are also represented very correctly visually.

The building types carved in these beam chins include both two-courtyard peasant courtyards and deep mansion compounds with long corridors; both small pavilions and towers, and city gate towers and palace halls built on high platforms; moreover, there are various arched bridges and fishing boats.

The fineness of the carving is even more breathtaking: the "houses" only three or four centimeters high not only have correct door and window proportions, but also the tile lines on the roof and the sparrow braces under the eaves can be seen.

Even the beam chin outline molding, usually just a single groove, here becomes dozens of closely arranged "petals." The curved bottom surface of the beam chin is also deeply carved with exquisite chrysanthemums.

Shan Beams (Eel Beams)

Elder Cheng Gaoming said he does not know how to write the "zhuan" character in "zhuanliang" (turning beam), only hearing that the character represents an animal, from which he推断 it may be "shanliang" (eel beam).

Shan beams are usually located in the upper part of the beam frame structure, with one end fixed on the column, used to longitudinally牵引 and support the stacked brackets of the purlins.

Shan beams are generally only used in ancestral hall architecture; on the old street, only Shaochang ancestral hall, Cheng Zhaofu ancestral hall, and Zhou clan ancestral hall use shan beams.

Even so, these buildings only use shan beams in the most important space, namely the beam frame structure of the main hall, which shows how high their "status" is.

Among these three ancestral halls, the shan beams in Shaochang ancestral hall resemble a coiled dragon spitting water; those in Cheng Zhaofu ancestral hall are also carved with a dragon head; only Zhou clan ancestral hall uses abstract scrolling patterns.

Shan beams in the same building have similar shapes, only their sizes are adjusted according to the distance between purlins.

Elder Cheng Gaoming said that usually if "three chou" (three-frame beams) are used, shan beams are not used. In the main hall of Shaochang ancestral hall, the middle frame mainly adopts post-and-lintel construction, so "three chou" is used instead of the shan beam under the main ridge. The side frames are column-and-tie construction, i.e.

using shan beams without "three chou." In addition, there are shan beams between every two purlins or purlin beams. Each coiled-dragon shan beam is nearly circular, with the dragon body arched high, visually seeming to support the rafters above, the entire shape extremely tense.

Although the dragon body coils toward the ridge direction, the dragon head is raised high looking toward the eave, and colorful water sprays from its mouth. The dragon body is painted with layered color gradients of red, white, blue, green and other colors, and there are also painted floral decorations on the dragon body.

The shan beams in Cheng Zhaofu ancestral hall are relatively abstract; the posture of the entire dragon can only be determined through a pair of bright eyes looking toward the ground.

Although the middle frame of the main hall also uses "three chou," for the sake of造型 unity, shan beams are still used for carving on the "three chou." This group of shan beams no longer has a structural function; coupled with the small space between the "three chou" and the rafters, the tails of this group of shan beams coil toward the eave direction, and the whole looks as if "lying down."

The shan beams in Zhou clan ancestral hall are similar in shape to those in Cheng Zhaofu ancestral hall, basically all presenting an ellipse larger at the top and smaller at the bottom, but they lack dragon heads, the moldings on the beams are simpler, and the carved grooves are shallower, making the entire component shape relatively full, not lacking in rustic beauty.

Rice Beams, Purlins, Eave-Protruding Beams

Rice beam refers to the horizontal structural component located between columns. A purlin is a rafter; if its cross-section is nearly square it is also called a purlin beam.

These components and eave-protruding beams are all main load-bearing structures, generally without any carvings, but sometimes the bottom surfaces of these components also become carriers for carvings.

Generally speaking, this type of carving only exists in ancestral hall architecture, because the beam frames in the main spaces here are all exposed, so rice beams, purlins and eave-protruding beams are visible when looking up.

The rice beams between the eave column and gold column on one side of the courtyard in the worship hall of Zhou clan ancestral hall are all carved with shallow abstract geometric patterns.

The bottom surfaces of the two upper gold purlin beams, front lower gold purlin beams and eave beams in the main hall of Cheng Zhaofu ancestral hall, as well as the eave-protruding beams around the courtyard, are all relief-carved with flowers, phoenixes, red-crowned cranes, flying birds, fish patterns and lions playing with balls.

Shaochang ancestral hall also has carvings in the same positions: the upper gold purlin beams are symmetrically carved with colored interlocking flowers; the front lower gold purlin beams and eave-protruding beams each have an openwork-carved group of flowers; the openwork-carved fish patterns and flower-and-bird designs on the eave beams are even more exquisite.

According to Elder Cheng Gaoming, purlins are generally made round, but to enable carving on them, they are changed to square. The carved part usually requires adding two or even three extra layers of wood boards; the more layers, the more transparent the carving can be.

Others

Sparrow braces, stacked brackets, inserted leaves, beam brackets, beam bracket pads, column brackets and other parts can also become the focus of large wood carvings. Stacked brackets and inserted leaves are a group of mutually perpendicular components located below rice beams, purlins or beams.

What supports them can be column brackets above the columns, or beam brackets on shuttle beams. The carving motifs used in these parts are mostly scrolling grass, flowers or abstract meander patterns, sometimes supplemented with auspicious characters.

本篇术语 Key Terms

2
小木作装修
中文

二、小木作装修

门、窗

老街上的住宅，正房明间通常有六扇通高并可摘除的门扇，以便在需要的时候，使正堂和明堂在空间上连成一体。除了正房明间以外，余下各间的门窗处理大体相同。每一开间被分为三部分：中间的一扇上面为窗，下面为板；左右两侧上面是高窗，下面为板，其中一侧板的三分之二可以开启为门。整体看起来对比强烈、重点突出，却又疏密有致、匀称均衡。可以说对于老街上大部分的住宅，每间正中的那扇窗就是整幢建筑中最为精美的一件木雕。两侧的高窗一般采用和主窗类似的雕刻主题，但会相对简洁。

以义丰雅记为例，早期住宅的窗子分为上下两屉。下屉窗的位置恰好在视线高度，面积较小，为固定窗。檐廊下常有人走动，做成固定窗，既不会影响房间里的人休息，又不会妨碍檐下的人走路。上屉窗为两扇平推窗，窗下檐高过一般人的头顶。每扇平推窗又会沿水平、垂直、水平方向上依次分格，每次分成比例匀称的小、大、小三部分，并雕以不同的纹饰。整扇窗结构、层次清楚，又重点突出。早期的窗花通常以抽象的几何回纹图案为主，线条匀称、构图大方。类似的回纹构图在附近村落同一时期的住宅里也可以看到。

十九世纪中期以后建造的住宅，上屉平推窗的尺寸在两个方向上都有所减小，三边余下的部分和下屉窗一起组成了一个大的窗套。平推窗本身只沿水平方向做一次划分，窗套的上半部分会再做两次到三次格，成为固定的雕花小窗。从雕饰的题材上来看，含有吉祥寓意的鸟兽、花草、字画，甚至是亭台楼阁等具象图案成为雕刻的主题和重点。这些图案通常位于每个分格的中间部位，四周衬以抽象纹饰。下屉固定窗的面积较大，通常还会嵌上三组这样的图案。用做背景的回纹也变得更为精致、细腻、富有层次，但同时却少了过去的那种朴素之美，略显矫揉造作。

栏板

老街上的早期建筑，可能由于二层原本并不住人，所以二层栏板不做任何雕饰，只是把十多厘米宽的木板竖向排列在栏板的框架之间。清末民初，由于车床的引进，带螺纹的木质栏杆成为一种时尚。对于大户有钱人家，这种千篇一律的机械式加工显然有失水准，所以便出现了手工雕刻的栏板。老街上有两幢中西合璧式木构单体"别墅"，新仁昌旅店和程铿别墅，每一幢都有精美的雕花栏板。这一方面可能是受到了西方建筑装饰的影响，更重要的是这两幢建筑的居住空间都不仅仅局限在一层，所以高层空间也就自然而然地产生了装饰装修的需求。

新仁昌旅店原为四层高单体建筑，现仅剩两层；建筑三开间，雕花栏板位于二层前廊。明间栏板分成三段，每段内有雕花栏板三块，每块约二十五厘米宽、七十厘米高，两块之间的间隔比栏板宽度略小；次间有同样大小的雕花栏板各十块，均匀排列。雕刻的主题大部分以吉祥鸟兽为主，并配以不同的花草，例如狮子、凤凰、仙鹤、鸳鸯、喜鹊、雄鸡等。其中明间正中的一组分别是凤凰、狮子、凤凰，并且两凤凰面面相对。明间栏板的边框处理还和次间略有不同，两侧做竹节状线脚，并且相互间高低错落有致。

这二十九块雕花栏板之中，有三块边框的处理手法不同，而且雕的都是姿态相同的白菜，推断是上层的栏板拆下来补缺的。余下的二十六块虽然有的采用相同的主题，但是个个造型独特。

程铿别墅高两层，宽三开间，进深两开间，二层四面有廊。每开间栏板分三组，每组三块；走廊进深这一架也有栏板三块。所有栏板都以回纹为底，镂空雕饰，栏板的宽度根据其所在的位置不同，稍有变化，宽度或位置一样的栏板采用相同的回纹样式。稍宽的栏板在回纹中间嵌雕有宝瓶、仙鹤、月季等图案。

English

2. Small Timber Joinery and decoration

Doors and Windows

In residences on the old street, the central bay of the main house usually has six full-height removable door leaves, so that when needed, the main hall and the open hall can be spatially integrated. Besides the central bay of the main house, the door and window treatment of the remaining bays is generally the same.

Each bay is divided into three parts: the middle one has a window above and a panel below; the left and right sides have high windows above and panels below, with two-thirds of one side panel able to open as a door. Overall it looks strongly contrasting with highlighted focus, yet dense and sparse in harmony, symmetrical and balanced.

It can be said that for most residences on the old street, the central window of each bay is the most exquisite wood carving in the entire building. The high windows on both sides generally use similar carving themes as the main window, but relatively simpler.

Taking Yifeng Yaji as an example, the windows of early residences are divided into upper and lower drawers. The lower drawer window is positioned exactly at eye level, with a smaller area, and is a fixed window.

People often walk under the eave corridor; making it a fixed window neither affects the rest of people in the room nor hinders people walking under the eaves. The upper drawer window consists of two sliding casement windows, with the lower eave of the window higher than an average person's head.

Each sliding casement window is again divided sequentially along horizontal, vertical, and horizontal directions, each time divided into proportionally symmetrical small, large, and small parts, carved with different patterns. The entire window structure has clear layers and highlighted focus.

Early window patterns are usually dominated by abstract geometric meander patterns, with symmetrical lines and generous composition. Similar meander compositions can also be seen in residences of the same period in nearby villages.

In residences built after the mid-nineteenth century, the dimensions of the upper sliding casement windows are reduced in both directions, and the remaining parts on three sides together with the lower drawer window form a large window frame.

The sliding casement window itself is only divided once along the horizontal direction; the upper part of the window frame is further divided into two to three grids, becoming fixed carved small windows.

In terms of carving themes, representational patterns with auspicious meanings such as birds and beasts, flowers and plants, calligraphy and paintings, and even pavilions and towers become the themes and focus of carving. These patterns are usually located in the middle of each grid, surrounded by abstract patterns.

The lower drawer fixed window has a larger area, usually embedded with three groups of such patterns. The meander pattern used as the background also becomes more refined, delicate, and layered, but at the same time loses the simple beauty of the past, appearing somewhat contrived.

railing Panels

In early buildings on the old street, perhaps because the second floor was originally not for living, the second-floor railing panels had no carvings, only vertical arrangement of wooden boards over ten centimeters wide within the railing frame.

In the late Qing and early Republican periods, due to the introduction of lathes, threaded wooden railings became fashionable. For wealthy large families, this monotonous mechanical processing was clearly substandard, so hand-carved railing panels appeared.

There are two Chinese-Western style single wooden "villas" on the old street, Xinrenchang Inn and Cheng Keng Villa, each with exquisite carved railing panels.

On the one hand this may have been influenced by Western architectural decoration; more importantly, the living spaces of both buildings are not limited to the first floor, so the high-level spaces naturally generated a demand for decoration and finishing.

Xinrenchang Inn was originally a four-story single building, now only two stories remain; the building is three bays wide, with carved railing panels on the second-floor front corridor.

The central bay railing panel is divided into three sections, each containing three carved railing panels, each about twenty-five centimeters wide and seventy centimeters high, with the gap between two panels slightly smaller than the panel width; the side bays each have ten carved railing panels of the same size, evenly arranged.

Most carving themes are auspicious birds and beasts, supplemented with different flowers and plants, such as lions, phoenixes, red-crowned cranes, mandarin ducks, magpies, roosters, etc. Among them, the central group of the middle bay is respectively phoenix, lion, phoenix, with the two phoenixes facing each other.

The border treatment of the central bay railing panels is also slightly different from the side bays, with bamboo-joint-shaped moldings on both sides, and mutually staggered heights.

Among these twenty-nine carved railing panels, three have different border treatment methods and are all carved with cabbages in the same posture, inferred to be railing panels from the upper floor taken down to fill gaps. The remaining twenty-six panels, although some use the same theme, each has a unique造型.

Cheng Keng Villa is two stories high, three bays wide, two bays deep, with corridors on all four sides of the second floor. Each bay's railing panels are divided into three groups, each group with three panels; the bay along the corridor depth also has three railing panels.

All railing panels use meander patterns as the base, with openwork carvings; the width of the railing panels varies slightly according to their location, and panels of the same width or position use the same meander style.

Wider railing panels have embedded carvings of treasure vases, red-crowned cranes, Chinese roses and other patterns in the middle of the meander.

砖石雕
中文

三、砖石雕

老街上建筑中的石雕很少，主要用在柱础上。这里的柱础形制比较简单，除了祠堂正堂中间的几个柱础有覆盆之外，其余的皆为鼓形柱础。柱础一般不做雕饰，最多只在上下各做一道线脚。另一处用石雕的地方则在庭院的角落处。以程成昌旅店为例，明堂四角各有一个一尺见方的石板，中间雕成阴阳相间的铜钱纹样，这样雨水就可以从这里流到院落下面的雨水道，再汇入老街两侧的雨水沟，最终流入方岩溪。

砖雕主要应用在马头墙上，还有建筑正立面旁门上，或是小窗上的眉檐。眉檐的做法通常是下面有几排"砖牙子"，然后是仿木构的椽头，上面再加一两皮挑砖，砖上放瓦。"高级"一点的眉檐可以多砌几排"砖牙子"，也可以做两层椽头相叠。程振兴四合院入口上的眉檐，不仅挑了六层"砖牙子"，而且中间还外加收分，整个眉檐还用红、白、黑三色相间描画。

老街上唯一一幢立面上饰有大量砖雕或石雕的建筑是得耕居。得耕居的建造年代较早，采用牌楼式门楼。中间的重檐牌楼为砖砌仿木结构，上面不但用砖堆砌出梁、柱、枋、柱头、柱础、椽头等构件，而且还用砖雕模拟雀替和柱头上的雕饰，甚至是梁枋上的彩画，靠近檐口的部分砖雕还残留有彩绘的印记。砖雕的主题既有一些抽象的花纹，又包括一些精美的鱼纹和鸟兽。最精美的雕饰要属门额上方的"双狮抢球"石雕，门额正中砖刻"得耕居"三字，并在两侧辅以石雕麒麟，下方衬以砖雕花鸟。

English

3. Brick and stone carvings

stone carvings in buildings on the old street are very few, mainly used on column bases. The column bases here are relatively simple in form; except for several column bases in the middle of the ancestral hall main hall which have overturned basins, the rest are all drum-shaped column bases.

Column bases generally have no carvings, at most only one molding on the top and bottom each. Another place using stone carvings is at the corners of the courtyard.

Taking Cheng Chengchang Inn as an example, each of the four corners of the open hall has a one-chi-square stone slab, with the middle carved into an alternating yin-yang copper coin pattern, so that rainwater can flow from here into the rainwater channel below the courtyard, then into the rainwater ditches on both sides of the old street, and finally into Fangyan Stream.

brick carvings are mainly applied on horse-head walls, as well as on side doors of building front facades, or on the eyebrow eaves above small windows.

The practice of eyebrow eaves is usually several rows of "brick teeth" below, then rafter heads imitating wooden structure, with one or two courses of protruding bricks on top, and tiles placed on the bricks. More "高级" eyebrow eaves can have more rows of "brick teeth" laid, or two layers of overlapping rafter heads.

The eyebrow eave above the entrance of Cheng Zhenxing's quadrangle not only protrudes six layers of "brick teeth," but also has additional entasis in the middle, and the entire eyebrow eave is painted with alternating red, white, and black colors.

The only building on the old street whose facade is decorated with a large amount of brick or stone carvings is Degeng residence. Degeng residence was built in an earlier period, adopting a paifang-style gatehouse.

The central double-eave paifang is a brick-built imitation wooden structure; on it, not only are components such as beams, columns, beams, column heads, column bases, and rafter heads piled up with bricks, but brick carvings are also used to simulate sparrow braces and carvings on column heads, even painted decorations on beams; the brick carvings near the eave still retain traces of colored painting.

The themes of brick carvings include both some abstract patterns and some exquisite fish patterns and birds and beasts.

The most exquisite carvings are the "double lions grabbing a ball" stone carvings above the door lintel; the center of the door lintel has the three characters "Degeng residence" carved in brick, flanked by stone-carved qilin on both sides, with brick-carved flowers and birds below.
