彩画
中文

四、彩画

彩画通常只位于砖墙上端靠近檐口的位置，并随檐口高低起伏，在青砖墙上形成了一个一尺左右的装饰带。由于风雨的侵蚀，能完整保留至今的彩画很少，老街上保留最完整的彩画要属程振昌旅店里照壁上的挂落式彩画。这套彩画分"三开间"，中间绘有垂莲柱、雀替等建筑构件，每一"开间"分成有韵律一些片段，每段独自为题。彩画的主题包括田园茅舍、鸟兽花草等。色彩以黑白为主，辅以少量的靛青和赭石。

English

4. Painted decorations

Painted decorations are usually only located at the upper end of brick walls near the eaves, following the ups and downs of the eaves, forming a decorative band of about one chi on the blue brick walls.

Due to wind and rain erosion, very few painted decorations have been completely preserved to this day; the most completely preserved painted decoration on the old street is the hanging-falling style painted decoration on the screen wall in Cheng Zhenchang Inn.

This set of painted decorations is divided into "three bays," with architectural components such as hanging lotus columns and sparrow braces painted in the middle; each "bay" is divided into rhythmic segments, each segment with its own theme.

The themes of the painted decorations include rural thatched cottages, birds, beasts, flowers and plants, etc. The colors are mainly black and white, supplemented by a small amount of indigo and ochre.

本篇术语 Key Terms

彩画 / Painted decoration (caihua) 挂落式彩画 / Hanging-falling style painted decoration 照壁 / Screen wall (zhaobi) 垂莲柱 / Hanging lotus column 靛青 / Indigo (pigment) 赭石 / Ochre (pigment)

第八章 文化和传统

1
胡公庙会
中文

一、胡公庙会

历史背景

阴历八九月，刚好是永康一带的农民忙完夏收夏种，而秋收冬种还未开始的农闲季节。恰巧八月十三又是方岩山顶广慈寺护法神胡则的生日。虽然广慈寺里的和尚，还有老街上的许多人都认为胡则的生日是阴历二月二十五，而八月十三这一天仅是胡公"检阅"罗汉班，抑或是被"神化"的日子，但是这个"小小"的分歧并不影响在八九月间方岩山上长达一个多月的胡公庙会。

胡公庙会的重头戏就是"打罗汉"，也称"迎案"或者"胜案"。关于"迎案"和"胜案"的区别，程崇舜老人解释，迎案是指具体的行动，胜案则是对整个过程的统称。"打罗汉"现在演化成以村为单位组织起来的"罗汉班"，以模仿古代军队出征的形式，按旗、棍、刀、叉等顺序排阵游行，并表演各种武术。

关于胡公庙会以及"打罗汉"的来历，允明禅师说，开山祖师正德禅师在的时候就有和尚习武练功，这便是"打罗汉"的由来。胡则在方岩的时候也提倡百姓练武强身。不仅如此，广慈寺最初还出资赞助村里的"打罗汉"活动。当地人则说村里"打罗汉"是从明朝末年开始的。还好这些零碎的信息之间似乎并不矛盾，中间加上一些已知的历史事件做串联，听起来似乎也有理。武举制度经唐朝的初创时期，到宋朝便日趋完善。南宋时期，武举制度更是演变成与进士科举制度相当的一种全国性的系统考试。这种变革进一步激发了民间练武的积极性。

婺州地区历史上先后出过两位武状元，其中之一就是在宋光宗绍熙元年（1190年）武科状元东阳人厉仲方。到了明末，浙江地区经常受到倭寇骚扰，再加上当时的军队素质不良，人民深受其害，习武健身便更具有保家卫国的切身目的。老街上的程氏子孙在这期间还出过几个总兵。明末抗倭名将戚继光于嘉靖三十八年（1559年）在义乌成立了以农民和矿工为主的戚家军。这支军队纪律严明，训练精良，屡战屡胜。

戚继光独创的"鸳鸯阵"和"打罗汉"还有异曲同工的意味，鸳鸯阵是以十二人为一队，并配以盾、枪、叉、钯、棍、刀等长短兵器，因敌因地变换队形，灵活作战。武举制度的完善，习武健身的传统，再加上保家卫国的需求，到明朝末年，"打罗汉"可能已经成为当地一项普及的民间活动。到了清朝，从县志中可以看出胡公庙会已经颇具规模。

八月十三日，佑顺侯胡公则生辰，各分村落为会，挂大帛为旗，长二三丈，导以鼓乐，从以伞盖，或以纸为马，登方岩。赛神而还。盖一郡香火之盛。未有若此比者。

若方岩香会，旧志不过曰挂帛为长旗，导以鼓乐伞盖而已。今则驾言用胡公卤簿，益以砲械，争道疾驰，各为党伍，其风染于临郡，重茧而至累月不休，其事皆近于兴会豪举。

这寥寥几笔的描写不但生动展示了当时庙会的盛况，而且还揭示了三百多年前胡公庙会的一些重要特点。首先，庙会期间的活动已经发展到以村落为单位。其次，胡公庙会在当时就已经超越了以简单的祭祀或是习武健身为目的的一种形式，而是成为一个集宗教、文化和娱乐为一体的"赛神"会。更重要的是，庙会的影响范围不仅扩大到了相邻的郡县，其过程更是"累月不休"。从康熙三十七年（1698年）《永康县志》中收录的"旧序"中可以知道在这之前的几本县志分别成书于清康熙十一年（1672年）和明万历九年（1581年），也就是说最晚在清初五十年，胡公庙会就已经是一派"挂帛为长旗，导以鼓乐伞盖"的热闹场面了。

作为一种带有宗教色彩的地方文化活动，它的存亡和兴衰既和支撑它的宗教信仰有关，又依赖于承载这个宗教信仰和文化活动的场所，而这三者之间的关系也是相辅相成的。作为建筑和空间的载体，广慈寺并赫灵庙一方面可以依靠与日俱增的香火钱来更好地维护并扩大其自身的建筑和领地；另一方面，修缮一新的大殿和金光灿灿的胡公大帝也能吸引更多远道而来的朝圣者。

据《库川胡氏家谱》记载，从顺治十一年（1654年）到道光二十七年（1847年）的近二百年里，奉胡则为伯祖的库川胡氏后人，先后多次出资帮助广慈寺修理或重建前厅和大殿，有记载的就有七次。库川胡氏不仅会出钱，也会亲自来方岩祭祀他们的伯祖。写于雍正九年（1731年）仲秋望日的《方岩祭说》中提道"八九月间，帜帐蔽道绎如也……他人之事公者且不远数百里，余族至亲且近……于是前辈宗长议以八月诞日拜奠于岩"。

在嘉庆十二年（1807年）八月，库川胡氏又与广慈寺签订了《方岩祭礼约》，规定了每年正月、八月在方岩祭祀时的一些细节。在胡公庙会期间的规定包括，"每年八月初一日，住僧到祠请监收油烛，胡宅备酒席如正月初四日例；八月初十日，任事人上岩收油烛，住僧备酒四席仍照旧例；八月十三日秋祭，住僧照例前后迎送"。胡氏家族历来是永康大姓，在地方上也是有钱有势。胡氏家族的介入在客观上进一步扩大了胡公庙会的影响和规模，同时使庙会的性质，在兼有习武健身、宗教、文化和娱乐等功能之后，又加上了祭祖的目的。

多种功能的相互交错是胡公庙会不但经久不衰，而且日渐兴旺的重要原因。而兴旺到连年"累月不休"的地步，则必然会形成对住宿、饮食和祭祀用品的需求。当这种需求形成一定规模，就必然会成为促就老街经济模式和发展的必然原因。

准备情况

胡公庙会的准备工作一般从阴历七月就开始了，有的村庄甚至过了正月十五就开始商议筹备。参加筹备的人多是村里德高望重的老人，过去以"族长"或是"祠堂常会"为主。这些人会选一个吉日聚在一起，吃过"拢头酒"后便开始工作。资金的筹集总是第一步。从整个庙会的准备过程到"迎案"当天，前前后后会有不小的花销。在过去，这笔开销主要由村里的祠堂负责，祠堂的收入则主要来自于同族大户人家捐给祠堂的房产、地产和林产。现在这笔开销则要靠"拢头人"挨家挨户筹集。

有了钱，就可以请拳师来训练"罗汉班"，除了师傅教的武义外，许多传统的样式多为代代相传。罗汉班通常是一直练到上方岩的前一两天。最后就是准备包括头旗、锣鼓、刀棍、铲子、宝剑、高跷等各式行头道具。由于筹备庙会耗时耗资，所以许多人少力薄的村落并不会年年"迎案"。但是在"迎案"的时间上却有一条不成文的规矩，那就是一旦你选择"迎案"，就要连续迎三年或是三年的倍数。

庙会期间，除了"迎案"之外的另一个重要的内容就是"换香火"。参加庙会的村落，都会有一个用精良木材雕刻的胡公神龛、牌位或龙庭，平时供奉在祠堂或本保庙里。到了农历八月初一就要请出来供奉在祠堂或者罗汉班的练武场，供村民祭拜。练武场一般是因地制宜的利用祠堂前的空场，或晒谷场等。到了约定上方岩的日期，"迎案"的队伍就要背着胡公的牌位上方岩，从胡公殿前的香炉里，掏一把滚烫的香灰放进背着的胡公牌位前的小香炉里，这就叫"换香火"。即使是当年不"迎案"的村子，也会组织一些人抬着胡公的牌位，敲锣打鼓上方岩"换香火"。如果连敲锣打鼓的人都请不起，至少也有大旗和香炉开道。

一些富裕的村落，还会准备几支吹唱班跟随在"迎案"队伍之后。与其说这是"娱神"，不如说这是农民在辛苦了一年之后，一种自娱自乐的表现。正因如此，随着农民生活水平的逐步提高，胡公庙会越办越兴旺。吹唱班表演的曲目多取材于民间故事传说和日常生活，例如十字莲花、十八蝴蝶、十八狐狸、十八罗汉和三十六行等。

庙会过程

胡公庙会正式开始是在八月初一，以胡则出生地胡库村"打罗汉"上方岩作为开端，其他村会在接下来的一个半月里，按照事先约定好的日期陆陆续续上方岩。下文引用了民国乙丑年（1925年）版《方岩指南》中列举的村落"迎案"时间表。

从迎案的时间安排就可以看出，胡公庙会到八月十三日会有一个高潮，而"重阳初八夜"则又是一个小高潮。当地有"八月十三，七十二个胡公上方岩"的说法，意思是说在这天会有七十二个胡公来"换香火"，而这其中绝大多数也是"迎案"的队伍。即使每支队伍平均有一百多人，总共也要七八千人，再加上随行的村民，还有远道而来的香客，总人数至少有两万左右。所以在八月十三日这天，上方岩的山路会拥挤不堪，这就让原本只有一尺多宽的"飞桥"变得更加危险。飞桥后来虽然被屡次加宽到现在的两米多，但是还会被抬着"七十二个胡公"的队伍挤得水泄不通，上下不得。

在各村"迎案"的前几天，村里的罗汉班还要先到附近各村的空场上展示一番，表演拳、刀、棍、叉等各班武义，有的还有叠罗汉和舞狮等，这个沿村表演的过程叫"游案"。"游案"一方面是"迎案"的彩排，同时也向周围的村落展示一下自己的实力。

具体到"迎案"当天的一些细节，村村有异，但大体程序基本相同。在约定好上方岩的前一天晚上，所有参与的人员要沐浴洁身，严格的还要求夫妻不得行房。到了第二天凌晨，要在练武场上向供在这里的小胡公神位举行"祭叉"仪式。全体参与庙会活动的人先用猪、鱼、鸡等供品祭拜胡公；随后便是鞭炮、鼓乐齐鸣，呐喊助威声震耳欲聋；最后罗汉班及吹唱班要在这里先演习一遍。结束以后，大家便回去吃早饭，并带上一些粽子、小麦饼之类的点心准备出发。由于"迎案"的队伍都是当天往返，所以有些路远的村庄在凌晨两三点就上路了。

以后塘巷村为例，"迎案"的队伍从前至后依次是举鹅毛旗四人、令字旗一人、抬香案两人、持锣四人、举执事牌四人、挑花烛四人、挑香十人、持开道刀铲两人、举万岁牌一人、背胡公神座一人、掌扇一人、举伞一人及降神童一人，随后才是罗汉班和吹唱班。罗汉班的人数少则四五十人，多则近百，吹唱班也是类似的规模，再加上轮替的人员以及随行的家属和看热闹的人，整个队伍多达几百人。这一队人马浩浩荡荡地来到了方岩山顶上的胡公大殿前。

在三响铁铳之后，从村里背上来的胡公神座被放在了大殿前，罗汉们于是在喧闹的锣鼓声中绕大殿顺向跳三圈、再逆向跳三圈，这叫"跳罗汉"。与此同时，降神童会在大殿前的香炉里捧一把香灰放进背来胡公神座前的小香炉里，完成"换香火"的仪式。这些都进行完毕，"迎案"的队伍便原路返回本村，这叫"打回头案"，一直到把村里的胡公神座送回平时存放它的祠堂或本保庙。至此为止，对于这个村庄来讲，胡公庙会才告结束。

但是对于岩下老街上的程氏后人，他们在连续一个多月的时间里，每天都会看到几支、甚至几十支这样的队伍从自家的门口经过，不仅如此，他们还要为招待众多来自异乡的香客而忙得不亦乐乎。这或许就是老街上的人从不"打罗汉"的原因吧。

English

1. Hu Gong temple fair

Historical Background

The eighth and ninth lunar months happen to be the slack farming season for farmers in the Yongkang area, after the summer harvest and planting are completed and before the autumn harvest and winter planting begin.

Coincidentally, the thirteenth day of the eighth lunar month is also the birthday of Hu Ze, the protective deity of Guangci temple on the summit of Fangyan Mountain.

Although the monks in Guangci temple and many people on the old street believe that Hu Ze's birthday is the twenty-fifth day of the second lunar month, and that the thirteenth day of the eighth month is merely the day when Hu Gong "reviews" the Luohan troupes or is "deified," this "minor" disagreement does not affect the month-long Hu Gong temple fair on Fangyan Mountain during the eighth and ninth months.

The highlight of the Hu Gong temple fair is "beating Luohan" (da Luohan), also called "welcoming the case" (ying'an) or "victorious case" (sheng'an).

Regarding the difference between "welcoming the case" and "victorious case," Elder Cheng Chongshun explained that "welcoming the case" refers to the specific action, while "victorious case" is the general term for the entire process.

"Beating Luohan" has now evolved into "Luohan troupes" organized by village, imitating the form of ancient military expeditions, parading in formation according to the order of flags, staffs, swords, forks, etc., and performing various martial arts.

Regarding the origin of the Hu Gong temple fair and "beating Luohan," Master Yunming said that when the founding ancestor Master Zhengde was there, monks already practiced martial arts, which is the origin of "beating Luohan." When Hu Ze was at Fangyan, he also advocated that the people practice martial arts to strengthen their bodies.

Moreover, Guangci temple initially funded and sponsored the village's "beating Luohan" activities. Local people say that village "beating Luohan" began in the late Ming dynasty. Fortunately, these fragmented pieces of information do not seem contradictory; connecting them with some known historical events in between makes it sound plausible.

The military examination system, after its initial establishment in the Tang Dynasty, became increasingly完善 in the Song dynasty. During the Southern Song period, the military examination system evolved into a nationwide systematic examination equivalent to the civil service examination.

This reform further stimulated the enthusiasm of folk martial arts practice. Historically, the Wuzhou area produced two military Zhuangyuan (top scholars), one of whom was Li Zhongfang from Dongyang, the military Zhuangyuan in the first year of Shaoxi of Emperor Guangzong of Song (1190).

By the late Ming dynasty, the Zhejiang area was frequently harassed by Japanese pirates, and coupled with the poor quality of the military at that time, the people suffered greatly; practicing martial arts for fitness thus had a more immediate purpose of defending home and country.

The Cheng descendants on the old street produced several commanders-in-chief during this period. The famous late-Ming anti-pirate general Qi Jiguang established the Qi Family Army in Yiwu in the thirty-eighth year of Jiajing (1559), composed mainly of farmers and miners. This army had strict discipline, excellent training, and won every battle.

Qi Jiguang's original "Yuanyang formation" (Mandarin Duck formation) has similarities with "beating Luohan"; the Yuanyang formation consists of twelve people per team, equipped with long and short weapons such as shields, spears, forks, tridents, staffs, and swords, changing formations flexibly according to the enemy and terrain.

The improvement of the military examination system, the tradition of practicing martial arts for fitness, and the need to defend home and country—by the late Ming dynasty, "beating Luohan" may have already become a popular folk activity in the area.

By the Qing dynasty, it can be seen from the county annals that the Hu Gong temple fair had already reached considerable scale.

On the thirteenth day of the eighth month, the birthday of You Shun Hou Hu Ze, each branch village forms an association, hanging large silk as flags, two to three zhang long, led by drums and music, followed by umbrellas and canopies, or using paper as horses, ascending Fangyan. Competing with the deity and returning.

The prosperity of incense in the entire prefecture has never been comparable to this.

As for the Fangyan incense fair, the old annals merely say hanging silk as long flags, led by drums, music, umbrellas and canopies.

Now they claim to use Hu Gong's honor guard, adding cannons and weapons, racing on the roads, each forming factions; this custom has spread to neighboring prefectures, people coming with blistered feet for months without rest, all these affairs resemble grand gatherings and heroic displays.

These few lines of description not only vividly show the grand occasion of the temple fair at that time, but also reveal some important characteristics of the Hu Gong temple fair over three hundred years ago. First, the activities during the temple fair had already developed to be organized by village.

Second, the Hu Gong temple fair at that time had already surpassed a form with simple sacrifice or martial arts fitness as its purpose, becoming a "deity-competing" fair integrating religion, culture, and entertainment.

More importantly, the influence of the temple fair not only expanded to neighboring counties, but its process was even "months without rest." From the "old preface" included in the Yongkang County Annals of the thirty-seventh year of Kangxi (1698), we can know that the previous county annals were respectively completed in the eleventh year of Kangxi (1672) and the ninth year of Wanli of Ming (1581), meaning that as late as fifty years into the early Qing, the Hu Gong temple fair was already a lively scene of "hanging silk as long flags, led by drums, music, umbrellas and canopies."

As a local cultural activity with religious overtones, its survival and rise and fall are related both to the religious belief supporting it and to the place carrying this religious belief and cultural activity, and the relationship among these three is mutually reinforcing.

As the carrier of architecture and space, Guangci temple and Heling temple can, on the one hand, rely on the ever-increasing incense money to better maintain and expand their own buildings and territory; on the other hand, the newly renovated main hall and the golden Hu Gong Great Emperor can attract more pilgrims coming from afar.

According to the Kuchuan Hu Family Genealogy, in the nearly two hundred years from the eleventh year of Shunzhi (1654) to the twenty-seventh year of Daoguang (1847), the Kuchuan Hu descendants, who regard Hu Ze as their great-uncle ancestor, repeatedly funded the repair or reconstruction of the front hall and main hall of Guangci temple, with seven recorded instances.

The Kuchuan Hu not only contributed money but also personally came to Fangyan to sacrifice to their great-uncle ancestor. The "Fangyan sacrifice Account" written on the full moon day of the eighth month in the ninth year of Yongzheng (1731) mentions that "in the eighth and ninth months, banners and tents cover the road continuously...

others who serve the lord come from hundreds of li away, our clan is close relatives and near...

so the elders and clan chiefs agreed to worship at the rock on the August birthday." In the eighth month of the twelfth year of Jiaqing (1807), the Kuchuan Hu again signed the "Fangyan sacrifice Ritual Agreement" with Guangci temple, stipulating some details of the annual sacrifices at Fangyan in the first and eighth months.

The regulations during the Hu Gong temple fair include: "Every year on the first day of the eighth month, the resident monk comes to the ancestral hall to request supervision of oil and candles, and the Hu residence prepares a banquet as on the fourth day of the first month; on the tenth day of the eighth month, the responsible persons go up the rock to collect oil and candles, and the resident monk prepares four tables of wine as usual; on the thirteenth day of the eighth month, the autumn sacrifice, the resident monk greets and sees off as usual." The Hu family has always been a major surname in Yongkang, wealthy and influential locally.

The involvement of the Hu family objectively further expanded the influence and scale of the Hu Gong temple fair, while adding the purpose of ancestor worship to the temple fair's nature, which already combined martial arts fitness, religion, culture, and entertainment.

The interweaving of multiple functions is an important reason why the Hu Gong temple fair not only endures but also thrives. And when it prospers to the point of "months without rest" year after year, it inevitably creates demand for accommodation, food, and sacrificial supplies.

When this demand reaches a certain scale, it inevitably becomes the inevitable cause promoting the economic model and development of the old street.

Preparation

Preparation for the Hu Gong temple fair generally begins in the seventh lunar month; some villages even start discussing preparations after the fifteenth day of the first month.

Those participating in preparation are mostly respected elders in the village, in the past mainly "clan chiefs" or "ancestral hall regular meetings." These people choose an auspicious day to gather together, and after drinking the "gathering head wine," they begin work. Fundraising is always the first step.

From the entire preparation process to the day of "welcoming the case," there will be considerable expenses before and after. In the past, this expense was mainly borne by the village ancestral hall, whose income mainly came from the real estate, land, and forest products donated to the hall by wealthy families of the same clan.

Now this expense relies on the "gathering head person" going door to door to collect. With money, boxing masters can be invited to train the "Luohan troupe"; besides the martial arts taught by the master, many traditional forms are passed down from generation to generation.

The Luohan troupe usually practices until a day or two before going up Fangyan. Finally, various props and equipment are prepared, including head flags, gongs and drums, swords and staffs, shovels, treasured swords, stilts, etc.

Because preparing for the temple fair is time-consuming and costly, many villages with few people and weak strength do not "welcome the case" every year. But there is an unwritten rule regarding the timing of "welcoming the case": once you choose to "welcome the case," you must do so continuously for three years or a multiple of three.

During the temple fair, another important content besides "welcoming the case" is "changing incense." villages participating in the temple fair all have a Hu Gong shrine, memorial tablet, or dragon court carved from fine wood, usually enshrined in the ancestral hall or local protection temple.

On the first day of the eighth lunar month, it is taken out and enshrined in the ancestral hall or the Luohan troupe's training ground for villagers to worship. The training ground generally makes use of the open space in front of the ancestral hall or a threshing ground according to local conditions.

On the agreed date to go up Fangyan, the "welcoming the case" team carries Hu Gong's memorial tablet up Fangyan, and from the incense burner in front of Hu Gong Hall, takes a handful of hot incense ash and puts it into the small incense burner in front of the carried Hu Gong memorial tablet—this is called "changing incense." Even villages that do not "welcome the case" that year will organize some people to carry Hu Gong's memorial tablet, beating gongs and drums, to go up Fangyan to "change incense." If they cannot even afford people to beat gongs and drums, at least there are big flags and incense burners leading the way.

Some wealthy villages also prepare several singing troupes to follow behind the "welcoming the case" team. Rather than saying this is "entertaining the deity," it is better to say it is a form of self-entertainment for farmers after a year of hard work.

Precisely because of this, as farmers' living standards gradually improve, the Hu Gong temple fair becomes more and more prosperous. The repertoires performed by singing troupes are mostly drawn from folk stories, legends, and daily life, such as Cross Lotus, Eighteen Butterflies, Eighteen Foxes, Eighteen Luohan, and Thirty-six Trades.

temple fair Process

The Hu Gong temple fair officially begins on the first day of the eighth month, starting with Huku village (Hu Ze's birthplace) "beating Luohan" going up Fangyan; other villages go up Fangyan one after another over the next month and a half according to pre-agreed dates.

The following quotes the village "welcoming the case" schedule listed in the 1925 (Yichou year of the Republic) edition of Fangyan Guide.

From the schedule of welcoming cases, it can be seen that the Hu Gong temple fair reaches a climax on the thirteenth day of the eighth month, while "the eighth night of Chongyang" is another minor climax.

Locally there is a saying "on the thirteenth of August, seventy-two Hu Gongs go up Fangyan," meaning that on this day seventy-two Hu Gongs come to "change incense," and the vast majority of them are also "welcoming the case" teams.

Even if each team averages over a hundred people, the total would be seven to eight thousand; plus accompanying villagers and pilgrims coming from afar, the total number is at least around twenty thousand.

So on the thirteenth day of the eighth month, the mountain path up Fangyan becomes extremely crowded, making the originally only one-chi-plus-wide "Flying bridge" even more dangerous.

Although the Flying bridge was later repeatedly widened to over two meters now, it is still packed solid by teams carrying "seventy-two Hu Gongs," unable to go up or down.

A few days before each village's "welcoming the case," the village's Luohan troupe first goes to the open spaces of nearby villages to perform, demonstrating various martial arts such as boxing, swords, staffs, and forks; some also have human pyramids and lion dances. This process of performing along villages is called "touring the case" (you'an).

"Touring the case" is on the one hand a rehearsal for "welcoming the case," and on the other hand demonstrates one's own strength to surrounding villages.

As for the specific details on the day of "welcoming the case," they vary from village to village, but the general procedure is basically the same. On the night before the agreed day to go up Fangyan, all participating personnel must bathe and purify themselves; strict ones also require husbands and wives not to have marital relations.

In the early morning of the next day, a "sacrificing forks" ceremony is held at the training ground for the small Hu Gong deity enshrined there.

All participants in the temple fair first worship Hu Gong with offerings such as pigs, fish, and chickens; then firecrackers and drums and music sound together, with cheering and shouting deafening; finally the Luohan troupe and singing troupe rehearse once here.

After finishing, everyone goes back to eat breakfast and brings some snacks such as zongzi and wheat cakes to prepare for departure. Since "welcoming the case" teams all return the same day, some faraway villages set out at two or three in the morning.

Taking Houtangxiang village as an example, the "welcoming the case" team from front to back is: four people carrying goose-feather flags, one person with a command flag, two people carrying the incense table, four people with gongs, four people carrying ceremonial plates, four people carrying flower candles, ten people carrying incense, two people with path-clearing swords and shovels, one person with a long-life plate, one person carrying Hu Gong's deity seat, one person with a palm fan, one person with an umbrella, and one descending deity child; then come the Luohan troupe and singing troupe.

The Luohan troupe numbers from forty to fifty at least to nearly a hundred at most, and the singing troupe is of similar scale; plus rotating personnel, accompanying family members, and onlookers, the entire team numbers several hundred people. This mighty procession arrives at Hu Gong Hall on the summit of Fangyan Mountain.

After three volleys of iron cannons, the Hu Gong deity seat carried from the village is placed in front of the main hall, and the Luohan performers then jump three circles clockwise and three circles counterclockwise around the main hall amid the noisy sound of gongs and drums—this is called "jumping Luohan." At the same time, the descending deity child takes a handful of incense ash from the incense burner in front of the main hall and puts it into the small incense burner in front of the carried Hu Gong deity seat, completing the "changing incense" ceremony.

After all this is done, the "welcoming the case" team returns to its village by the same route—this is called "beating the returning case"—until the village's Hu Gong deity seat is returned to the ancestral hall or local protection temple where it is normally stored. Up to this point, for this village, the Hu Gong temple fair comes to an end.

But for the Cheng descendants on Yanxia Old Street, over a continuous period of more than a month, they see several or even dozens of such teams passing by their doors every day; moreover, they are busy entertaining numerous pilgrims from other places. This is perhaps why people on the old street never "beat Luohan."

本篇术语 Key Terms

胡公庙会 / Hu Gong Temple Fair 打罗汉 / Beating Luohan (martial arts procession) 迎案/胜案 / Welcoming the case/Victorious case 罗汉班 / Luohan troupe 换香火 / Changing incense 游案 / Touring the case (village rehearsal) 跳罗汉 / Jumping Luohan 打回头案 / Beating the returning case 祭叉 / Sacrificing forks ceremony 鸳鸯阵 / Yuanyang Formation (Mandarin Duck Formation) 武举制度 / Military examination system 胡公神龛/龙庭 / Hu Gong shrine/Dragon court 吹唱班 / Singing troupe 拢头酒 / Gathering head wine 库川胡氏 / Kuchuan Hu clan ...等17个
2
正月
中文

二、正月

过年

老街上的人家过年，是以"祭灶"揭开序幕的。在江浙地区，关于祭灶的时间有"官三民四船家五"的说法，这也就是为什么在《永康县志》风俗篇里的记载为"十二月二十三日，祭灶"，而当地人却在二十四日"送灶君"。在这天傍晚，家家户户都会在灶台前祭拜灶君，并供上"黏糖"，以保证灶君在玉皇大帝面前只讲好话，不讲坏话，以便带回来年的好运气。

送走了灶王爷，就到了"年头"，又叫"年头禁"。在腊月二十五这一天，"是日不出财。以赤豆和米煮粥，曰蚕花粥云"；不仅如此，还要"洒扫沐浴"。过了二十五以后，每家要选一个好日子来"谢年"，又叫"送年"。谢年是一个以家庭为单位的祭神活动，通常在自家的厅堂里举行，还要准备猪头、鹅、鸡、鱼、豆腐、酒、饭等丰盛的供品。"谢年"由全家的男子参加，在祭拜的同时，大家要相互说一些祝福的话，还要把几天前送走的灶君接回来，请他老人家开始新一年的工作。

除日，即除夕，这天白天要"送年桃，符春帖，蒸饭足新年数日之食"，晚上在团圆饭之后则要全家一起"坐岁"。十二点一过，家家户户都要抢着开门放炮，因为开门最先被视为大吉。新的一年到来之后，老街上的大部分人做的第一件事就是：连夜上方岩山拜胡公、求签。一方面是看看自己在这一年的运气；另一方面也希望无所不能的胡公大帝能保佑自己一家平安，生活美满。

新年这天早上，要吃鸡蛋素面，意味着健康长寿，全家团圆。早上还要有羹，即用青菜、豆腐和米粉等煮成汤；中午要吃粽子，意为"先耕后种"，讨年年有"耕"，年年有"种"的吉利。这小小的细节，再次体现了"得耕"这两个字对老街上程氏后人的深远影响。在早餐过后，就开始一系列的仪式性活动：先是要点香烛，望空而拜，接着就是到祠堂里去"拜太公"，最后是家里人以尊卑长幼为序互拜。自初二开始的四五天里，则是邻里、亲戚互相拜访。

迎龙灯

正月十五是上元节，又称灯节，在这天前后，当地素有"迎花烛"、"迎龙灯"的习俗。这些活动一般以村为单位，临近的几个村则会把时间错开。老街所在的这个小山谷里，按规矩十三日迎花烛，十四日岩下村迎龙灯，十五日橙麓村迎龙灯。

关于迎龙灯的习俗，一些民俗学家把它一直追溯到古越人对水神的一种祭祀活动。他们认为龙灯是龙舟上岸后的一种新的形式，古人通过祭龙娱龙来祈求新一年"风调雨顺，五谷丰登，人丁兴旺"。对于老街上的人，当地话"灯"与"丁"同音，所以"迎龙灯"过程中的许多细节都和"人丁兴旺"、"添丁"的美好希冀密不可分。

老街上迎的龙灯属于"板桥灯"，这种灯的龙头、龙尾各为一节，龙身则是由几十"桥"，甚至一百多"桥"灯首尾相接而成，每桥有两盏"纱灯"。其中龙头和龙尾的做法类似，都是用一块长方形的木板作底，然后用竹篾编成骨架，外裱棉纸；随后上油彩，并辅以龙须、龙珠、龙爪等；最后涂上羊油，这样一方面能显得更为光泽，又能起到防水的作用。支撑龙头用的底板长三四米，宽不到半米，需要四个男青年协力共舞；龙尾则小一些，需要两个人抬。相比起来，龙身的制作则要简单很多。龙身的每一节称作一"桥"，由一个人迎举。

所谓的"桥"就是一块一米多长的木板，板上两端各有一个篾扎纸糊，四方八角的彩灯，每盏彩灯上再插上一面写有诸如"五谷丰登"、"风调雨顺"等字样的三角纸糊彩旗。龙头、龙身、龙尾每一桥灯的底板两端都各有一孔，插上"拴灯柱"，就可以把上百桥灯接成长龙。

迎龙灯的准备活动通常从腊月就开始了。负责舞龙头和龙尾通常是一种荣耀，这项工作叫"捻灯"，会优先让给在这一年村里第一个生儿子的人家。由于龙头和龙尾的骨架是要被重复使用的，所以平日就存放在世远公祠厢房的二层。正月初六这天，负责舞龙头和龙尾的人家就要开始着手准备了。他们先要把龙头和龙尾拿到固厚峰东面的"深塘"里去清洗；随后要到方岩山上去砍毛竹，回来请师傅修理破损的骨架；最后重新糊纸、上色、装点龙珠等。

仅仅这些工作还不够，这家人还要负责请人舞龙头、举迎牌、作领灯、司敲锣打鼓等工作，外加准备迎龙灯当天的食物、烟酒，开销可是不菲。所以，即便是件荣耀喜庆的事，也不是每个家庭都能负担得起的。如果这个家庭选择不做，就会轮到村里这一年第二个生儿子的人家，以此类推，直到有一家决定承担这项工作为止。对于轮到又选择不做的人家，就要负责出十桥灯；如果做这个也有负担，则要做一些麻酥分给当天舞灯的人吃。对于村里其他的人家，几乎每家每户都要出至少一桥灯，或者准备些点心。

正月十四日早上七点开始，负责捻灯的人家会找两个人，敲着大锣，走遍老街的每一个角落，这叫"催灯"。催灯一般上午要三四次，下午又要三四次。下午三点的时候，在得耕居内会有为晚上舞灯的人准备好的饭菜。晚饭后夜幕降临，各家各户开始放鞭炮，并举行点灯仪式。这里的规矩是"红灯出，红灯入"，家家户户点好的一桥桥的龙灯，从老街上的各个角落出发，纷纷向得耕居前的空场上聚集，开始"接灯"。得耕居前爆竹声震耳欲聋，陆陆续续到达的部分桥灯依次和龙头接好，在"接灯"的同时，龙灯已经在空场上转上了三小圈。

随即龙灯在举迎牌的、吹喇叭的、敲锣的和领灯人的引导下，走向蜥蜴山前山头上的朱公位。朱公位后有一棵大柏树，龙灯会绕着朱公位和大柏树转几圈，并向朱公磕头。下山的时候还会在八世祖程濂，即松门公墓前拜一下。后两圈比较小，基本上就是绕着空场和澈月塘转。龙灯每次回到得耕居前，就会有更多的桥灯顺次接上，到第三圈结束的时候，龙尾才会最终出现。于是，接好的龙灯在众人的簇拥下和震耳的锣号声中开始游街。由于整个游街过程长达十多个小时，所以每个舞灯手都要有另一个人随行以备轮换。

这些人，再加上围观的乡亲，组成了一支几百人浩浩荡荡的队伍。

龙灯首先沿老街向北行进，沿街的家家户户都会在门前挂纱灯，放鞭炮，摆好香烛、猪肉、鸡肉等供品迎接龙灯，龙头也会进到家里去磕头"讨彩"。讨来的钱会归总到一起，再统一发给参与迎龙灯的人家。当龙灯行进到位于下胡坑坑口的经堂前，不能进去打扰菩萨，但是会在外面"跳灯"，所谓跳灯就是龙头要往前走，龙尾却偏要往后拉，这样来来去去要反复几次，直到大家都精疲力竭了才罢休。随后龙灯会沿着老街一直走到橙麓村北，象鼻岗山脚的程氏总祠。这时龙头会被抬进祠堂摆放在大厅中间，由领香的人代为上香，祭拜程氏先祖。

迎龙灯的队伍在祠堂前稍事休息后会向西进入寿山坑，并沿方岩山北面的山路上山。上山的时候，已经是深夜了，几百桥龙灯蜿蜒近一里，纱灯里的烛火可以照亮整个方岩山。走到天门的时候，广慈寺的住持已经身着袈裟、手持法杖，带领众僧在这里迎候。龙灯被迎到寺院里，这里早就备好茶点、糖果以招待舞灯的人，寺里的和尚还会把没有烧完的蜡烛分给大家。休息片刻，龙灯便再次起程，从前山下山。下山的路十分难走，最陡峭的一段是飞桥，还不到一米宽，所以在下山以后，迎龙灯的队伍要在山门处吃茶点、小憩。

随后龙灯再次进村，并分别到世远公祠和绍常祠堂里拜祭，最后龙灯来到绍常祠堂前的空场上。这时天色已经微明，舞灯的人们在这里再做最后一次休息后，以备迎龙灯活动的高潮，即"盘龙灯"，也称"团龙灯"。盘龙灯一开始，龙头便以圆形路线向内盘旋，直到龙头被几圈桥灯团团围在中间。这时，龙头要设法逃出重围，而龙尾又要带领几百桥龙身尽力阻拦，这叫"翻龙灯"。"翻龙灯"会反反复复进行许多次，这期间舞灯者还会轮换休息，直到大家都筋疲力尽。这时绍常祠堂的管事会出来"讲好话"，在管事的好言劝说下，龙尾终于让龙头翻出重围。

当龙灯再次回到得耕居前的时候，天已经大亮了。龙灯被一桥桥的拆开，换上新的蜡烛之后，桥灯便在鞭炮声中被村民们纷纷迎回了家。最后，龙头上的"珠灯"要取下来，送给村里的新婚夫妇，或是想要儿子的人家，这叫"送灯"。由于"送灯"暗含着"送丁"的吉祥祝福，所以收到珠灯的人家要准备好红包。每年龙头上会有多少珠灯也是要提前计算好的，以保证每个想要儿子的人家都能得到祝福。到此为止，长达十几个小时的迎龙灯活动在正月十五日的早上终于结束了。人们在吉祥和喜庆的氛围下，新一年的各项工作便陆陆续续开始了。正如老街上的俗话，"灯过年过，牛归牛栏，马归马舍"。

English

2. First Lunar Month

New Year

Families on the old street begin the New Year with "sacrificing to the stove god." In the Jiangsu-Zhejiang region, regarding the time of stove god sacrifice, there is a saying "officials on the third, commoners on the fourth, boatmen on the fifth," which is why the Yongkang County Annals customs chapter records "on the twenty-third day of the twelfth month, sacrifice to the stove," while local people "send off the stove god" on the twenty-fourth.

On this evening, every family worships the stove god in front of the stove, offering "sticky candy" to ensure that the stove god only speaks good words and not bad words before the Jade Emperor, so as to bring back good luck for the coming year.

After sending off the stove god, comes the "year head," also called "year head prohibition." On the twenty-fifth day of the twelfth month, "on this day no wealth is spent.

Red beans and rice are cooked into porridge, called silkworm flower porridge"; moreover, one must "sweep and bathe." After the twenty-fifth, each family chooses an auspicious day to "thank the year," also called "sending the year." Thanking the year is a family-based deity sacrifice activity, usually held in one's own hall, with abundant offerings such as pig head, goose, chicken, fish, tofu, wine, and rice prepared.

"Thanking the year" is attended by all the men of the family; while worshipping, everyone says some blessing words to each other, and also brings back the stove god sent away a few days ago, asking the old gentleman to begin a new year of work.

On New Year's Eve, during the day one must "send New Year peaches, paste spring couplets, steam enough rice for several days of the New Year"; in the evening after the reunion dinner, the whole family must "sit through the year" together.

After twelve o'clock, every family rushes to open the door and set off firecrackers, because opening the door first is considered great fortune. After the new year arrives, the first thing most people on the old street do is: go up Fangyan Mountain overnight to worship Hu Gong and draw divination sticks.

On the one hand, to see their luck for this year; on the other hand, they hope the omnipotent Hu Gong Great Emperor will bless their family with peace and a happy life.

On New Year's morning, one eats egg plain noodles, meaning health, longevity, and family reunion. In the morning there is also soup, made from green vegetables, tofu, and rice flour; at noon one eats zongzi, meaning "first plow then plant," seeking the auspiciousness of having "plowing" and "planting" every year.

This small detail once again reflects the profound influence of the two characters "Degeng" (obtaining plowing) on the Cheng descendants on the old street.

After breakfast, a series of ritual activities begins: first lighting incense and candles, bowing toward the sky, then going to the ancestral hall to "worship the great ancestor," and finally family members bowing to each other in order of seniority. From the second day for four or five days, neighbors and relatives visit each other.

Welcoming the Dragon Lantern

The fifteenth day of the first lunar month is the Shangyuan festival, also called the Lantern festival. Around this day, locally there are customs of "welcoming flower candles" and "welcoming the dragon lantern." These activities are generally organized by village, with nearby villages staggering their times.

In the small valley where the old street is located, by custom the thirteenth day welcomes flower candles, the fourteenth day Yanxia village welcomes the dragon lantern, and the fifteenth day Chenglu village welcomes the dragon lantern.

Regarding the custom of welcoming the dragon lantern, some folklorists trace it all the way back to a sacrificial activity of the ancient Yue people to the water deity.

They believe the dragon lantern is a new form after the dragon boat comes ashore; ancient people sacrificed to and entertained the dragon to pray for "favorable weather, abundant harvests, and prosperous population" in the new year.

For people on the old street, in the local dialect "lantern" (deng) and "male child" (ding) are homophones, so many details in the process of "welcoming the dragon lantern" are inseparable from the beautiful hopes of "prosperous population" and "adding a male child."

The dragon lantern welcomed on the old street belongs to the "board bridge lantern" type; the dragon head and dragon tail of this lantern are each one section, while the dragon body is formed by dozens or even over a hundred "bridge" lanterns connected end to end, each bridge having two "gauze lanterns." The dragon head and dragon tail are made similarly: both use a rectangular wooden board as the base, then bamboo strips are woven into a skeleton, pasted with cotton paper on the outside; then painted with oil colors, supplemented with dragon whiskers, dragon pearls, dragon claws, etc.; finally coated with sheep oil, which on the one hand makes it more glossy and on the other hand serves as waterproofing.

The base board supporting the dragon head is three to four meters long and less than half a meter wide, requiring four young men to dance together; the dragon tail is smaller, requiring two people to carry. In comparison, the making of the dragon body is much simpler. Each section of the dragon body is called a "bridge," held up by one person.

The so-called "bridge" is a wooden board over one meter long, with a bamboo-framed paper-pasted square octagonal colored lantern at each end of the board; each colored lantern has a triangular paper-pasted colorful flag inserted on top, with inscriptions such as "abundant harvests" and "favorable weather." The base boards of each bridge lantern of the dragon head, body, and tail each have a hole at both ends; inserting "lantern-connecting posts" allows hundreds of bridge lanterns to be connected into a long dragon.

Preparation for welcoming the dragon lantern usually begins in the twelfth lunar month. Being responsible for dancing the dragon head and tail is usually an honor; this work is called "niandeng" (twisting the lantern), and is优先 given to the family that had the first son born in the village that year.

Since the skeletons of the dragon head and tail are to be reused, they are normally stored on the second floor of the wing room of Shiyuan Gong Hall. On the sixth day of the first lunar month, the family responsible for dancing the dragon head and tail begins preparations.

They first take the dragon head and tail to the "Deep pond" east of Guhou Peak for cleaning; then go to Fangyan Mountain to cut moso bamboo, and upon return ask a master to repair the damaged skeleton; finally re-paste paper, color, and decorate with dragon pearls etc.

This work alone is not enough; the family must also be responsible for hiring people to dance the dragon head, hold welcome plaques, serve as lead lantern, beat gongs and drums, plus prepare food, tobacco and alcohol for the day of welcoming the dragon lantern—the expenses are considerable.

So even though it is an honorous and festive matter, not every family can afford it. If this family chooses not to do it, it passes to the family that had the second son born in the village that year, and so on, until one family decides to undertake the work.

For families whose turn it is but choose not to do it, they must be responsible for providing ten bridge lanterns; if even this is a burden, they must make some sesame cakes to distribute to the people dancing lanterns that day. For other families in the village, almost every household must provide at least one bridge lantern or prepare some snacks.

Starting at seven in the morning on the fourteenth day of the first lunar month, the family responsible for niandeng finds two people to beat a large gong and walk through every corner of the old street—this is called "urging the lantern." Urging the lantern generally happens three or four times in the morning and three or four times in the afternoon.

At three in the afternoon, meals prepared for the evening lantern dancers are served inside Degeng residence. After dinner as night falls, every household sets off firecrackers and holds a lantern-lighting ceremony.

The rule here is "red lantern out, red lantern in"; the bridge-by-bridge dragon lanterns lit by every household set out from various corners of the old street, gathering one after another at the open space in front of Degeng residence to begin "connecting lanterns." The sound of firecrackers in front of Degeng residence is deafening; the bridge lanterns arriving one after another are connected to the dragon head in sequence, and while "connecting lanterns," the dragon lantern has already made three small circles in the open space.

Then the dragon lantern, guided by those holding welcome plaques, blowing trumpets, beating gongs, and the lead lantern person, heads toward Zhu Gong's position on the front hilltop of Lizard Mountain.

Behind Zhu Gong's position is a large cypress tree; the dragon lantern circles around Zhu Gong's position and the large cypress tree several times, and kowtows to Zhu Gong. When going down the mountain, it also pays respects at the tomb of the eighth-generation ancestor Cheng Lian, i.e. Songmen Gong.

The latter two circles are smaller, basically circling around the open space and Cheyue pond. Each time the dragon lantern returns to in front of Degeng residence, more bridge lanterns are connected in sequence; by the end of the third circle, the dragon tail finally appears.

Then the connected dragon lantern, surrounded by the crowd and amid deafening gongs and trumpets, begins parading through the streets. Since the entire parade process lasts over ten hours, each lantern dancer must have another person accompanying for rotation. These people, plus the watching villagers, form a mighty team of several hundred people.

The dragon lantern first travels north along the old street; every household along the street hangs gauze lanterns in front of their doors, sets off firecrackers, and arranges offerings such as incense, candles, pork, and chicken to welcome the dragon lantern; the dragon head also enters homes to kowtow and "seek fortune." The money sought is collected together and then distributed uniformly to families participating in welcoming the dragon lantern.

When the dragon lantern reaches the front of the Jingtang (scripture hall) at the mouth of Xiahu Pit, it cannot enter to disturb the Bodhisattva, but will "jump lantern" outside; so-called jumping lantern means the dragon head wants to go forward while the dragon tail insists on pulling backward, going back and forth repeatedly several times until everyone is exhausted.

Then the dragon lantern travels along the old street all the way to the Cheng clan General ancestral hall north of Chenglu village, at the foot of Xiangbi Hill.

At this time the dragon head is carried into the ancestral hall and placed in the middle of the main hall, with the lead-incense person burning incense on its behalf to worship the Cheng ancestors.

After a brief rest in front of the ancestral hall, the dragon lantern welcoming team heads west into Shoushan Pit and climbs the mountain along the path on the north side of Fangyan Mountain.

By the time of climbing, it is already deep night; hundreds of bridge dragon lanterns wind for nearly one li, and the candle flames in the gauze lanterns can illuminate the entire Fangyan Mountain. When reaching the Heavenly Gate, the abbot of Guangci temple, already in kasaya robe and holding a staff, leads the monks to welcome here.

The dragon lantern is welcomed into the temple, where tea, snacks, and candies have long been prepared to entertain the lantern dancers; the monks in the temple also distribute unburned candles to everyone. After a brief rest, the dragon lantern sets off again, descending from the front mountain.

The descent path is very difficult; the steepest section is the Flying bridge, less than one meter wide, so after descending, the dragon lantern team has tea and snacks and a brief rest at the mountain gate.

Then the dragon lantern enters the village again, and respectively goes to Shiyuan Gong Hall and Shaochang ancestral hall to worship; finally the dragon lantern comes to the open space in front of Shaochang ancestral hall.

By this time the sky is already faintly bright; the lantern dancers take one final rest here, preparing for the climax of the dragon lantern welcoming activity, namely "coiling the dragon lantern," also called "grouping the dragon lantern." At the start of coiling the dragon lantern, the dragon head spirals inward in a circular path until the dragon head is surrounded by several circles of bridge lanterns.

At this point, the dragon head tries to escape the encirclement, while the dragon tail leads hundreds of bridge dragon bodies to尽力 block—this is called "flipping the dragon lantern." "Flipping the dragon lantern" is repeated many times, during which lantern dancers rotate to rest until everyone is exhausted.

At this time the steward of Shaochang ancestral hall comes out to "speak good words"; under the steward's kind persuasion, the dragon tail finally lets the dragon head flip out of the encirclement.

When the dragon lantern returns to in front of Degeng residence again, it is already broad daylight. The dragon lantern is disassembled bridge by bridge; after replacing with new candles, the bridge lanterns are welcomed back home by villagers amid the sound of firecrackers.

Finally, the "pearl lanterns" on the dragon head are taken down and given to newlywed couples in the village, or families wanting a son—this is called "sending lanterns." Since "sending lanterns" implicitly contains the auspicious blessing of "sending a male child," families receiving pearl lanterns must prepare red envelopes.

How many pearl lanterns will be on the dragon head each year is also calculated in advance to ensure that every family wanting a son can receive the blessing. Up to this point, the over-ten-hour dragon lantern welcoming activity finally ends on the morning of the fifteenth day of the first lunar month.

Amid an atmosphere of auspiciousness and festivity, the various tasks of the new year begin one after another. As the saying on the old street goes, "when the lantern festival passes, the ox returns to the ox pen, the horse returns to the horse stable."

本篇术语 Key Terms

祭灶/送灶君 / Sacrificing to the stove god/Sending off stove god 黏糖 / Sticky candy (for stove god) 年头禁 / Year head prohibition 谢年/送年 / Thanking the year/Sending the year 坐岁 / Sitting through the year (New Year's Eve vigil) 迎龙灯 / Welcoming the dragon lantern 板桥灯 / Board bridge lantern (dragon lantern type) 捻灯 / Niandeng (responsibility for dragon head/tail) 催灯 / Urging the lantern 接灯 / Connecting lanterns 跳灯 / Jumping lantern (dragon tug-of-war) 盘龙灯/团龙灯 / Coiling the dragon lantern 翻龙灯 / Flipping the dragon lantern 送灯/送丁 / Sending lantern/Sending male child 朱公位 / Zhu Gong's position (shrine) ...等16个
3
祭祖
中文

三、祭祖

对祖先的祭祀活动一直是中国民众社会生活的重要组成部分，同时也是一种最淳朴的宗教意识的体现。人们通过一系列的丧葬仪式来安抚死者的灵魂，使他们能够顺利地进入另一个世界，并在那里安逸舒适的"生活"。仅仅这样还不够，人们还要借助定期供奉食物、送钱、送衣物的形式和祖先保持联系，祈求祖先保佑整个家族人丁兴旺，财源茂盛，五谷丰登。在一年中众多的节日里，有三个节日被认为是"阴间的节日"。在这三天里，村里都有或大或小的祭祖活动。

清明

祭祖仪式最隆重的要属清明，即每年的四月初。在清明的前三天、后四天，村里的人要到各房的祖坟上去祭扫，添土和"标青"，"标青"即是在坟前挂白纸。祭祀用的食物通常包括猪头、鹅、豆腐、糕、大麦面、金团和清明果等，其中以清明果最有特色。清明果有红、白、青三种，它是用糯米做皮，红糖或白糖做馅的点心。磨好的糯米直接用的话就可以做出白色的清明果；如果加入野菜汁（野蒿），就可以做成青色的；红色的则是加入了食用颜料。和其他地方不同的是，当地的清明果是借用馃印压制成的。金团类似于清明果，只是用豆腐、粉丝、青菜和猪肉做馅。

祭祀的过程是由在场的辈分最大的人主持，主持人通过高喊"通"和"引"来指挥其余的人。"通"是让大家准备好一起做事，而"引"则是让某个人前去祭拜。整个祭祀的程序大致包括拈香、点烛、跪拜、奠酒、进汤、献饭、读祝文和焚祝文，其中在焚祝文的同时还会打锣、吹号和放鞭炮。祭祀结束之后所有的人会在一起席地围坐吃午餐，又名"散清"。

岩下老街的程姓人中，如今多数是十五世毅庵公的后人。据民国33年（1944年）《程氏天房谱》记载，毅庵"葬四十二都小墟塘龟山墳前墈下"，距岩下村35里，现属舟山镇。因为距离远，坐轿子单程要走三四个小时，再加上开销大（雇轿子的费用由个人出一半，祖上出一半），所以每年去的人不多，一般也只有四五十人左右。能去的人多来自地位较高和家境较好的人家，而且妇女和外姓人也可以去。已经年过古稀的程镇康老先生回忆说，他小时候随父亲去过舟山两次祭祖。

让他印象最深的就是：经过的村庄都会觉得这四五十顶轿子组成的队伍很是威风。这在客观上也是对岩下程氏的一种宣传，使邻村的姑娘都愿意嫁到这样的望族。这或许也是岩下程氏在近二三百年来人丁兴旺，并且能够一直保持这样一个单姓血缘村落的原因。程毅庵的夫人被子孙们尊称为"太婆"。因为她葬在较近的金龙江，每年清明去的人就较多。

村里的一部分人还会到下胡坑里作勳公的坟地去"拜天官"。作勳公是岩下程氏第十三世上的人，岩上村的作勳公祠也是以他命名的。他之所以受到如此尊敬，是因为他"性情敦朴，节俭持躬，铢积寸累以成家业。而资雄里闬，每遇岁歉必出粟赈饥，凡有求者缓急靡不相济。人皆德之"。

在清明那天，村里的几个祠堂会打开祠堂门，挂起祖宗像，摆上猪头、鹅、豆腐等供品，等待子孙们的祭拜。比起那些有钱有地位的人，村里那些穷苦的程氏后人就只能在清明那天到祠堂里去拜拜祖宗，期待着可以分到的那点供品。现住在作勳公祠里，74岁的程茂发老人讲：他在十六岁以后就可以去祠堂祭祖，并且他有三个祠堂可以去。他可以从村里人人都有份的世远公祠和一部分人有份的作勳公祠里各领到一斤饭、两片肉和两片豆腐；他还能从本房的，一个在几家人中轮换管理的"祠堂"里领到一点钱。

1995年，永康程氏第一到第四世祖的墓被统一移到了离村子三里的北政岭。自此，遍布永康地区十七个村的程氏后人开始在清明期间进行联合祭祖。祭祖的活动由十七个村庄的程氏轮流主持，通常情况下是由村里老年协会的主席带头组织。

冬至

冬至是另外一个祭祀祖先的重要时间。不同于清明时期的宗族集体祭祖，冬至主要是以家庭为单位进行小型的祭祖活动，祭拜的对象通常也仅限于自家前四代的祖宗。在清光绪编《永康县志》中提道"旧家大族四时祭于祖庙，而冬至祭最重"。或许是因为"冬至祭最重"，所以住在岩下老街的程氏后人在"四时"之中只保留了冬至祭祖的习俗。当地人称冬至为"下世人过年"。在岩下老街和橙麓村里，关于冬至祭祖的时间有两种说法：一种说法是在冬至的当天祭祖；另一种说法则认为要在冬至的前三天或后四天中的一天中祭祖。但是他们祭祖的形式都是相似的。

每一家都要做"羹饭"，在厅堂里摆供品以招待先祖。据程崇舜老人讲，冬至祭祖的"羹饭"要比其他日子里的多一些点心。

祭祀过程多在午饭之前，有的人家还会放鞭炮。岩下街79号的程镇康家每年都在冬至这天祭祖，2008年冬至这天他家的供桌上摆了猪肉、煎豆腐、鸡蛋、自家种的橘子和用糯米做皮，豆腐、粉丝、猪肉和青菜做馅的团子。这些供品放在桌子中间，旁边整整齐齐的摆放了九副碗筷和酒杯。在靠近明堂一侧的桌子上有两个烛台和两束各三支的香。在一切都准备好以后，程家的女主人，即程镇康的夫人，拿着一束香到院门口拜三拜请祖宗们回家。如果这时外面正好有人的话，大家都会自觉地让开门口。

等祖宗们"落座"之后，程家的儿子要给每个祖宗敬三次酒，酒是用自家酿的上好黄酒。随后，家里的人开始在院子里一起给祖宗烧些纸钱和纸银锭。最后，程家的女主人要拿着另一束香，走到门口再拜三拜送祖宗们回去，然后关上院门。在整个祭祀过程结束之后，供桌上的部分美味就成了当日午餐的一部分。

中元

第三个祭祖的日子是在阴历七月十五的中元节，也称鬼节。在这一天里，家家户户也要做"羹饭"来盛筵祖先。但是不同于另外两个节日的是，在这一天，短命的和没有后代的孤魂野鬼也可以享受到祭祀。正如县志里说："中元，荐祖考、祀孤"。所以这里有些善良的人家会在祭祖之后，用篮子带上一些供品，到村庄外的路口或者空地上进行野祭，俗称"路头羹饭"。更有富裕的人家会结队去寺院或是祠堂去"拜星"，以超度饿鬼和死囚。这一切都是为了能让自己的祖先安心地享受儿孙们孝敬的香烛、食物和钱财，而不会被那些受不到祭祀的孤魂野鬼骚扰。

English

3. ancestor worship

ancestor worship activities have always been an important part of Chinese people's social life, and at the same time a manifestation of the simplest religious consciousness. Through a series of funeral rituals, people appease the souls of the dead, enabling them to smoothly enter another world and live comfortably there.

This alone is not enough; people also maintain contact with ancestors through regular offerings of food, money, and clothing, praying that ancestors will bless the entire family with prosperous population, flourishing wealth, and abundant harvests.

Among the many festivals in a year, three are considered "festivals of the underworld." On these three days, there are ancestor worship activities of varying sizes in the village.

Qingming (Pure Brightness)

The most solemn ancestor worship ceremony is Qingming, at the beginning of April each year. Three days before and four days after Qingming, villagers go to the ancestral graves of each branch to sweep, add soil, and "mark green" (biaoqing), which means hanging white paper in front of the grave.

Food for sacrifice usually includes pig head, goose, tofu, cakes, barley flour, golden dumplings, and Qingming fruit, among which Qingming fruit is the most distinctive. Qingming fruit comes in red, white, and green; it is a pastry made with glutinous rice as the skin and brown or white sugar as the filling.

Directly using ground glutinous rice makes white Qingming fruit; adding wild vegetable juice (wild mugwort) makes green; red is made by adding food coloring. Unlike other places, local Qingming fruit is pressed using a guo (cake) mold. Golden dumplings are similar to Qingming fruit, but filled with tofu, vermicelli, green vegetables, and pork.

The sacrifice process is presided over by the person of the highest generation present; the presider commands the others by shouting "tong" (connect) and "yin" (guide). "Tong" means everyone prepares to do things together, while "yin" means a certain person goes forward to worship.

The entire sacrifice procedure roughly includes lighting incense, lighting candles, kneeling and bowing, pouring libation wine, offering soup, offering rice, reading the prayer text, and burning the prayer text; during the burning of the prayer text, gongs are beaten, horns blown, and firecrackers set off.

After the sacrifice, all people sit together on the ground in a circle for lunch, also called "sanqing" (scattering Qingming).

Among the Cheng surnamed people on Yanxia Old Street, most today are descendants of the fifteenth-generation Yi'an Gong.

According to the Cheng Family Tian Branch Genealogy of the 33rd year of the Republic (1944), Yi'an "was buried at the front slope of Turtle Mountain, Xiaoxu pond, Forty-second Du," 35 li from Yanxia village, now belonging to Zhoushan Town.

Because of the distance, taking a sedan chair takes three to four hours one way, plus high expenses (sedan chair costs are borne half by the individual and half by the ancestors), so not many people go each year, generally only about forty to fifty.

Those who can go mostly come from families of higher status and better circumstances, and women and people of other surnames can also go. The over-seventy-year-old Elder Cheng Zhenkang recalled that when he was young he went to Zhoushan twice with his father for ancestor worship.

What impressed him most was that villages passed by would find the procession of forty to fifty sedan chairs very imposing. Objectively this was also a kind of publicity for the Yanxia Cheng, making girls from neighboring villages willing to marry into such a prominent clan.

This is perhaps why the Yanxia Cheng have had a prosperous population for the past two to three hundred years and have been able to maintain such a single-surname blood-relation village. Cheng Yi'an's wife is respectfully called "Grandma" by descendants. Because she was buried in the closer Jinlong River, more people go there each Qingming.

Some people in the village also go to Zuoxun Gong's grave in Xiahu Pit to "worship the heavenly official." Zuoxun Gong was a person of the thirteenth generation of the Yanxia Cheng; the Zuoxun Gong Hall in Yanshang village is also named after him.

The reason he is so respected is because he "was simple and honest in disposition, thrifty in personal conduct, accumulating bit by bit to establish his family. With wealth prominent in the neighborhood, every year of famine he would certainly give grain to relieve hunger, and anyone seeking help in urgency was never refused.

All people praised his virtue."

On Qingming day, several ancestral halls in the village open their doors, hang ancestor portraits, and arrange offerings such as pig head, goose, and tofu, waiting for descendants to worship.

Compared with those wealthy and prestigious people, the poor Cheng descendants in the village can only go to the ancestral hall on Qingming day to worship their ancestors, hoping for a share of the offerings.

Elder Cheng Maofa, 74, who now lives in Zuoxun Gong Hall, said: after he turned sixteen he could go to the ancestral hall for ancestor worship, and he had three ancestral halls to go to.

He could each receive one jin of rice, two slices of meat, and two slices of tofu from Shiyuan Gong Hall (which everyone in the village has a share in) and Zuoxun Gong Hall (which some people have a share in); he could also receive a little money from his own branch's "ancestral hall," which is managed in rotation among several families.

In 1995, the graves of the first to fourth-generation ancestors of the Yongkang Cheng were uniformly moved to Beizheng Ridge, three li from the village. Since then, Cheng descendants spread across seventeen villages in the Yongkang area have begun joint ancestor worship during Qingming.

The ancestor worship activity is hosted in rotation by the Cheng of the seventeen villages, usually organized under the leadership of the village elderly association chairman.

Winter Solstice

Winter Solstice is another important time for ancestor worship. Unlike the clan collective ancestor worship of Qingming, Winter Solstice mainly involves small-scale ancestor worship activities by family unit, and the objects of worship are usually limited to the previous four generations of one's own ancestors.

The Qing Guangxu edition of Yongkang County Annals mentions that "old prominent families sacrifice at the ancestral temple in all four seasons, but the Winter Solstice sacrifice is the most important." Perhaps because "the Winter Solstice sacrifice is the most important," the Cheng descendants living on Yanxia Old Street have only retained the Winter Solstice ancestor worship custom among the "four seasons." Locals call Winter Solstice "the lower world people's New Year." In Yanxia Old Street and Chenglu village, there are two说法 about the timing of Winter Solstice ancestor worship: one says to worship on the day of Winter Solstice; the other says to worship on one of the three days before or four days after Winter Solstice.

But their forms of ancestor worship are similar. Every family makes "soup rice" (gengfan), arranging offerings in the hall to entertain ancestors. According to Elder Cheng Chongshun, the "soup rice" for Winter Solstice ancestor worship has more pastries than on other days.

The sacrifice process mostly takes place before lunch, and some families also set off firecrackers. The Cheng Zhenkang family at No.

79 Yanxia Street worships ancestors every Winter Solstice; on Winter Solstice 2008, their offering table had pork, fried tofu, eggs, homegrown oranges, and dumplings made with glutinous rice skin and tofu, vermicelli, pork, and green vegetable filling.

These offerings were placed in the middle of the table, with nine sets of bowls, chopsticks, and wine cups neatly arranged beside. On the side of the table near the open hall were two candlesticks and two bundles of three incense sticks each.

After everything was prepared, the mistress of the Cheng family, Cheng Zhenkang's wife, took a bundle of incense and bowed three times at the courtyard gate to invite the ancestors home. If there happened to be people outside at this time, everyone would voluntarily step aside from the doorway.

After the ancestors "took their seats," the Cheng son poured wine three times for each ancestor, using fine homemade yellow rice wine. Then the family members burned paper money and paper silver ingots for the ancestors together in the courtyard.

Finally, the mistress took another bundle of incense, went to the door and bowed three times to send the ancestors back, then closed the courtyard gate. After the entire sacrifice process ended, part of the delicacies on the offering table became part of that day's lunch.

Zhongyuan (Ghost festival)

The third ancestor worship day is the Zhongyuan festival on the fifteenth day of the seventh lunar month, also called Ghost festival. On this day, every family also makes "soup rice" to feast ancestors. But unlike the other two festivals, on this day, short-lived and descendantless wandering ghosts can also enjoy sacrifice.

As the county annals say: "Zhongyuan, offer to ancestors, sacrifice to the lonely." So some kind families here, after ancestor worship, take some offerings in a basket to the crossroads or open spaces outside the village for wild sacrifice, commonly called "roadhead soup rice." Wealthier families go in groups to temples or ancestral halls to "worship stars," to超度 hungry ghosts and condemned prisoners.

All this is to enable one's own ancestors to peacefully enjoy the incense, candles, food, and money offered by descendants, without being harassed by those wandering ghosts who receive no sacrifice.

本篇术语 Key Terms

祭祖 / Ancestor worship 清明 / Qingming (Pure Brightness Festival) 标青 / Marking green (hanging white paper at grave) 清明果 / Qingming fruit (glutinous rice pastry) 金团 / Golden dumpling 散清 / Sanqing (post-sacrifice communal lunch) 羹饭 / Gengfan (soup rice offering) 冬至 / Winter Solstice 中元节/鬼节 / Zhongyuan Festival/Ghost Festival 路头羹饭 / Roadhead soup rice (wild sacrifice) 拜星 / Worshipping stars (for超度) 程毅庵 / Cheng Yi'an (15th generation ancestor) 作勳公 / Zuoxun Gong (13th generation ancestor) 程镇康 / Cheng Zhenkang (elder, born ~1930s)
4
分家
中文

四、分家

分家主要有两种方法，一种是当家里的长辈年迈或去世后，儿子们相互分家，有时也可能是长孙和叔叔们分家。在这种情况下，分家的主持人一般由家族中有威望的人来担当，更重要的是要请娘舅和姑父来作证，找会写字的人来执笔写分家契。分家的第一步是要把属于大家庭的家产和财务情况做登记。对于一般的人家，分家主要只涉及房产和地产。在分房产的时候，如果全家只有一个宅院，院里的厅堂或称西间，会留作公用，用以举行红白喜事。在西间东侧的叫上大房，会分给长子；西间西侧的叫下大房，会分给次子；余下房间的分法并无特定的模式。

对于一些大户人家来讲，分家时家具和器物也都要逐一登记，并力图平均分配。例如，1945年老街上最大的旅店程振兴被一分为二。据说，因为东西和钱财太多，分家前前后后用去了三个多月的时间。一般来说，长子和有长孙的儿子，在分家时会得到较好的一份。如果当时家里还有未成家的男子，也会得到相应的照顾。这种照顾多局限在钱财或器物上，例如，母亲可能把自己的私房钱分给未婚的儿子。在分家后，如果父母还在世，一般会跟长子一起生活；有些人家，父母也会轮流到几个儿子家居住，享受子女的照顾。

另外一种分家的方法是每当一个儿子结婚以后，父母就会拿出一些钱财和器物，让小两口单过。据程镇康老人讲，其太公程乐涞就是这样给他的七个儿子分家的。当程乐涞帮长子程昭斌成亲后，就给了他两千铜钱、一条扁担、两个布袋、一个锅、几个盆和碗，让他们出去租房单过。别看这几样再普通不过的东西，里面却饱含老人的一片苦心。扁担和布袋是给他到杭州和金华去做茶叶生意；两千铜钱当然就是起家和做生意的本钱；有了锅、盆和碗，就可以起灶做饭，填饱肚子。老人没给被褥之类的东西，是因为这些都来自于随嫁的嫁妆。

对于接下来的五个儿子，老人都是如此：帮他们娶亲，给他们一些本钱和生活的必需品，让他们出去单过。区别只是当家境好的时候，一条扁担可能变成了两条扁担；家境不好的时候，两千铜钱或许变成了五百铜钱；等到最小的儿子程昭祥成家的时候，老人把他留在了身边，帮助料理，并最终继承了家里的小旅店。有趣的是，这六个被"扫地出门"的儿子，日后都成了颇有成就的大商人，远比备受照顾的小儿子家境好。其中有三个在老街上做起了旅店生意，而且都是赫赫有名的大旅店；另外三个分别经营着茶叶生意、南货店和雨伞店。

English

4. Family Division

Family division mainly has two methods. One is when the elders of the family are elderly or deceased, the sons divide the family among themselves; sometimes it may also be the eldest grandsons and uncles dividing the family.

In this case, the presider of family division is generally a person of prestige in the clan; more importantly, maternal uncles and paternal uncles by marriage are invited to testify, and a literate person is found to write the family division contract.

The first step of family division is to register the family property and financial situation belonging to the large family. For ordinary families, family division mainly only involves real estate and land.

When dividing real estate, if the whole family has only one courtyard, the hall in the courtyard, also called the west room, is kept for common use, for holding weddings and funerals.

The one east of the west room is called the upper great room, assigned to the eldest son; the one west of the west room is called the lower great room, assigned to the second son; the division of the remaining rooms has no specific pattern.

For some large families, furniture and utensils must also be registered one by one during family division, and an effort is made to distribute them equally. For example, in 1945, the largest inn on the old street, Cheng Zhenxing, was split in two.

It is said that because there were too many things and money, the family division took over three months from start to finish. Generally speaking, the eldest son and the son who has an eldest grandson receive a better share during family division.

If there were still unmarried men in the family at that time, they would also receive corresponding care. This care is mostly limited to money or utensils; for example, a mother might distribute her private savings to unmarried sons.

After family division, if parents are still alive, they generally live with the eldest son; in some families, parents also take turns living with several sons, enjoying the care of their children.

Another method of family division is that whenever a son marries, the parents give some money and utensils to let the young couple live separately. According to Elder Cheng Zhenkang, his great-grandfather Cheng Lelai divided the family this way for his seven sons.

After Cheng Lelai helped his eldest son Cheng Zhaobin marry, he gave him two thousand copper coins, one carrying pole, two cloth bags, one pot, several basins and bowls, letting them go out to rent a house and live separately. Don't underestimate these most ordinary things; they contain the old man's painstaking care.

The carrying pole and cloth bags were for him to do tea business in Hangzhou and Jinhua; the two thousand copper coins were of course the capital for starting up and doing business; with the pot, basins, and bowls, they could start a stove to cook and fill their stomachs.

The old man did not give things like bedding because these came from the dowry. For the next five sons, the old man did the same: helped them marry, gave them some capital and daily necessities, and let them go out to live separately.

The difference was only that when the family circumstances were good, one carrying pole might become two; when circumstances were bad, two thousand copper coins might become five hundred; when the youngest son Cheng Zhaoxiang married, the old man kept him by his side to help manage, and he eventually inherited the family's small inn.

Interestingly, these six sons who were "swept out the door" all became quite accomplished big merchants later, with far better family circumstances than the much-cared-for youngest son.

Three of them started inn businesses on the old street, all famous large inns; the other three respectively operated tea business, a southern goods store, and an umbrella shop.

本篇术语 Key Terms

分家 / Family division (fenjia) 分家契 / Family division contract 西间 / West room (common hall) 上大房/下大房 / Upper great room/Lower great room 程乐涞 / Cheng Lelai (1823-1882, great-grandfather) 程昭斌 / Cheng Zhaobin (eldest son, tea merchant) 程昭祥 / Cheng Zhaoxiang (youngest son, inherited inn)

附录A 浙江省政府各厅处、机关、附属机构在方岩的办公场所

浙江省政府迁至方岩办公后，其各厅处位置：

浙江省政府，位于五峰书院，主席黄绍竑。纪念周及省政府会议均在山洞中进行。

浙江省政府秘书处，位于五峰书院，秘书长初为张梁任，后为李立民。

浙江省民政厅，初位于岩下街新隆兴旅店，民国29年（1940年），迁至绍常祠堂。厅长初为王先强，后为阮毅成。

浙江省财政厅及省营贸易局，位于五峰书院旁重楼，又大部分在老仁昌旅店办公。厅长初为程远帆，后为黄祖培。

浙江省教育厅，位于程振兴旅店，厅长许绍棣。

浙江省政府会计处，位于新仁昌旅店，会计长陈宝麟。

浙江省动员委员会，位于五峰路。

浙江省建设厅、浙江省政府社会处、浙江省粮食管理局、浙江省赈济会，位于永康境内其他地区。

浙江省保安处、军官区司令部、浙江省抗日自卫队总司令部，留在金华。

同时迁到方岩的省政府平行机关：

中国国民党浙江省执行委员会，位于五峰旁的空地上，一座由胡库人新建的木构二层楼房内。

浙江省高等法院，位于岩下街南端回龙桥边程氏宗祠（又说程铿别墅），院长郑文礼。

浙江省高等法院检察处，位于岩下街南端，一周姓人家院内，首席检察长王秉彝。

位于岩下街和寿山坑内的浙江省各厅处附属机构：

浙江省地政局、土地测量总队，在民政厅内，即新隆兴旅店，后迁至绍常祠堂。

浙江省警察大队，位于周氏宗祠。

浙江省民政厅公馀进修会，位于岩下街。

浙江省电报局、邮政局及电话局，位于五峰路，租用程中财家3间半民房。

浙江省民政厅卫生处、医疗防疫大队、省会卫生事务所及卫生试验所，位于五峰路及世雅。其中抗战医院位电报、电话局对面，租用四间半民房。

浙江省立建国小学（政府工作人员子弟学校），位于五峰书院内。

浙江省立方岩小学，位于岩下街北端民房内。

浙江省史料征集委员会，位于鸡鸣峰南麓的万成庙内。

浙江省国民通讯社，位于程振兴旅店。

浙江省天赋管理处，位于岩下街北端金标祠堂。

民族夜校，位于岩下街北端，橙麓村民房内，后为程元昌旅店。

省政府主席黄绍竑公馆，位于寿山坑北面山腰，即现在五峰宾馆所在地。

抗战阵亡将士纪念碑，1939年7月7日建于桃花峰北。

Appendix A: Office Locations of Zhejiang Provincial Government Departments, Agencies, and Affiliated Institutions at Fangyan

After the Zhejiang Provincial Government moved to Fangyan for office, the locations of its various departments:

Zhejiang Provincial Government, located at Wufeng academy, Chairman Huang Shaohong. Memorial weeks and provincial government meetings were all held in caves.

Zhejiang Provincial Government Secretariat, located at Wufeng academy, Secretary-General initially Zhang Liangren, later Li Limin.

Zhejiang Provincial Department of Civil Affairs, initially located at Xinlongxing Inn on Yanxia Street; in the 29th year of the Republic (1940), moved to Shaochang ancestral hall. Department Head initially Wang Xianqiang, later Ruan Yicheng.

Zhejiang Provincial Department of Finance and Provincial Trade Bureau, located at the multi-story building beside Wufeng academy, with most offices at Laorenchang Inn. Department Head initially Cheng Yuanfan, later Huang Zupei.

Zhejiang Provincial Department of Education, located at Cheng Zhenxing Inn, Department Head Xu Shaodi.

Zhejiang Provincial Government Accounting Office, located at Xinrenchang Inn, Accounting Chief Chen Baolin.

Zhejiang Provincial Mobilization Committee, located at Wufeng Road.

Zhejiang Provincial Department of construction, Zhejiang Provincial Government Social Affairs Office, Zhejiang Provincial Grain Management Bureau, Zhejiang Provincial Relief Committee, located in other areas within Yongkang.

Zhejiang Provincial Security Office, Military District Command, Zhejiang Provincial Anti-Japanese Self-Defense Force General Command, remained in Jinhua.

Provincial government parallel agencies simultaneously moved to Fangyan:

Chinese Kuomintang Zhejiang Provincial Executive Committee, located on the open ground beside Wufeng, in a newly built two-story wooden building by people from Huku.

Zhejiang Provincial High Court, located at Cheng clan ancestral hall by Huilong bridge at the southern end of Yanxia Street (also said to be Cheng Keng Villa), President Zheng Wenli.

Zhejiang Provincial High Court Prosecutor's Office, located at the southern end of Yanxia Street, in the courtyard of a Zhou family home, Chief Prosecutor Wang Bingyi.

Affiliated institutions of Zhejiang provincial departments located in Yanxia Street and Shoushan Pit:

Zhejiang Provincial Land Bureau, Land Survey Corps, within the Department of Civil Affairs, i.e. Xinlongxing Inn, later moved to Shaochang ancestral hall.

Zhejiang Provincial Police Brigade, located at Zhou clan ancestral hall.

Zhejiang Provincial Department of Civil Affairs Spare-time Further Education Association, located on Yanxia Street.

Zhejiang Provincial Telegraph Bureau, Post Office and Telephone Bureau, located at Wufeng Road, renting three and a half civilian houses from the Cheng Zhongcai family.

Zhejiang Provincial Department of Civil Affairs Health Office, Medical Epidemic Prevention Brigade, Provincial Capital Health Affairs Office and Health Laboratory, located at Wufeng Road and Shiya. Among them, the Anti-Japanese War Hospital was opposite the telegraph and telephone bureau, renting four and a half civilian houses.

Zhejiang Provincial Jianguo Primary School (school for children of government workers), located within Wufeng academy.

Zhejiang Provincial Fangyan Primary School, located in civilian houses at the northern end of Yanxia Street.

Zhejiang Provincial Historical Materials Collection Committee, located at Wancheng temple on the southern foot of Jiming Peak.

Zhejiang Provincial National News Agency, located at Cheng Zhenxing Inn.

Zhejiang Provincial Natural Endowment Management Office, located at Jinbiao ancestral hall at the northern end of Yanxia Street.

Ethnic Night School, located at the northern end of Yanxia Street, in civilian houses in Chenglu village, later became Cheng Yuanchang Inn.

Provincial Government Chairman Huang Shaohong's residence, located on the northern mountainside of Shoushan Pit, i.e. the current location of Wufeng Hotel.

Civil Affairs Department Head Ruan Yicheng's residence, located on the southern foot of Jiming Peak, in front of Wancheng temple.

Anti-Japanese War Fallen Soldiers Memorial Monument, built on July 7, 1939, north of Taohua Peak.

附录B 胡公庙会吹唱班部分曲目
中文

附录B 胡公庙会吹唱班部分曲目

十八狐狸

"十八狐狸"又名"大面姑娘"，它是方岩庙会中不可或缺的，而且是最受欢迎的节目。此舞蹈根据民间故事改编，相传唐朝有位官员原有4子18孙。后因门庭败落，17个孙子相继夭亡，撇下17个寡妇。不得已，最小的孙子迫使17位嫂嫂加上其妻共18个人开妓院、卖唱。这一舞蹈由20名男演员组成。18人男扮女装，头戴各种表情的女性面具，上穿蓝色花边大襟衣，下系黑色百褶裙，脚穿绣鞋，一手拿纸扇，一手拿绸帕。一人扮演妓院老板娘，戴羊角髻大面具，穿红色花边大襟衣，系彩带，穿彩裤，着绣鞋，一手捧水烟筒，一手执麦秸扇。

另一人扮嫖客，头戴瓜皮帽面具，身穿咖啡色长衫、紫黑色马褂，脚穿黑布鞋，手握长烟管。表演时，嫖客游弋于狐狸中间，丑态百出地进行挑逗调情，狐狸有的斥责反抗，有的卖弄风骚；妓院老板娘则前窜后跳，对嫖客巴结逢迎，对狐狸软哄硬压。在《望乡台》的打击乐伴奏下，只演不唱。

十八蝴蝶

"十八蝴蝶"由18人饰"蝴蝶"，2～4人饰"花神"。蝴蝶"翅膀"用竹篾扎成骨架，覆以纱布或丝绸，上绘各种花纹图案，"翅膀"固定在肩背上，可以自由扇动。"花神"为婺剧花旦打扮，上穿红色对襟水袖衫，下着百褶长裙，手曳披肩长飘带。音乐采用现成的民间小调，如《孟姜女》、《姑娘相思》等，乐队多由四人组成，两人拉二胡，一人敲钹，一人打鼓，演员只舞不唱。

十八鲤鱼

"十八鲤鱼"是由18名女青年扮演的大道具舞蹈。演员身背可以用双手控制自由开合的红色鲤鱼壳。鲤鱼壳的骨架用竹篾扎制，糊上彩纸或纱绸，绘上鱼身。表演时用打击乐，如大小锣、大小钹、小鼓等伴奏。

讨饭莲花

"讨饭莲花"又名永康莲花，一般由18～36个男演员扮演。一人扮演"讨饭头"，其重彩丑脸，赤膊赤脚，头戴"红缨帽"或"草辫箍"，身穿破白裙。身穿破烂衣衫的"讨饭帮"随后，他们每人手执一副莲花竹板，唱《永康莲花》调。通常由"讨饭头"领唱一句，"讨饭帮"跟一句腔，唱词一般有"顺十字"、"倒十字"，或即兴随编。

三十六行

"三十六行"是由38名男性扮演的舞蹈。其中36人扮演36个行业的特征，他们手拿各行各业的典型工具，摆出各自的架势。另有2个男演员各人头戴"草辫箍"，脸施重彩，衣衫不整，腰系稻草绳，赤脚，各打一面大锣，领头开路。

English

Appendix B: Selected Repertoires of the Singing Troupes at the Hu Gong temple fair

Eighteen Foxes

"Eighteen Foxes," also known as "Big-Face Girl," is an indispensable and most popular program at the Fangyan temple fair. This dance is adapted from a folk story: legend has it that an official in the Tang Dynasty originally had 4 sons and 18 grandsons. Later, due to the decline of the family, 17 grandsons died one after another, leaving 17 widows.

Out of necessity, the youngest grandson forced the 17 sisters-in-law plus his wife, totaling 18 people, to open a brothel and sing for a living. This dance consists of 20 male actors.

Eighteen men dress as women, wearing female masks of various expressions, blue lace-front robes, black pleated skirts, and embroidered shoes, holding paper fans in one hand and silk handkerchiefs in the other.

One person plays the brothel madam, wearing a large ram's-horn bun mask, red lace-front robe, colored ribbons, colored pants, and embroidered shoes, holding a water pipe in one hand and a wheat-straw fan in the other.

Another person plays a client, wearing a melon-rind cap mask, brown long gown, purple-black mandarin jacket, black cloth shoes, and holding a long pipe.

During performance, the client roams among the foxes, making a fool of himself with flirting and teasing; some foxes scold and resist, some show off their charms; the brothel madam dashes back and forth, fawning over the client and coaxing or coercing the foxes. Accompanied by percussion music of "Wangxiangtai," it is danced only, not sung.

Eighteen Butterflies

"Eighteen Butterflies" has 18 people playing "butterflies" and 2-4 people playing "flower gods." The butterfly "wings" are framed with bamboo strips, covered with gauze or silk, painted with various floral patterns; the "wings" are fixed on the shoulders and back and can flap freely.

The "flower gods" are dressed as Wuju opera female leads, wearing red front-opening water-sleeve tops, long pleated skirts, and holding long shawl ribbons.

The music uses existing folk tunes such as "Meng Jiangnu" and "Girl's Longing"; the band usually consists of four people: two playing erhu, one playing cymbals, one playing drums; actors only dance, not sing.

Eighteen Carp

"Eighteen Carp" is a large-prop dance performed by 18 young women. Actors carry on their backs red carp shells that can be freely opened and closed with both hands. The carp shell skeleton is made of bamboo strips, pasted with colored paper or gauze silk, and painted with fish bodies.

During performance, it is accompanied by percussion instruments such as large and small gongs, large and small cymbals, and small drums.

Begging Lotus

"Begging Lotus," also known as Yongkang Lotus, is generally performed by 18-36 male actors. One person plays the "begging head," with heavily made-up clown face, bare-chested and barefoot, wearing a "red-tassel hat" or "straw-braid band," and a tattered white skirt.

The "begging gang" in tattered clothes follows, each holding a pair of lotus bamboo clappers, singing the "Yongkang Lotus" tune. Usually the "begging head" leads one line, and the "begging gang" follows with a refrain; lyrics generally include "forward ten characters," "reverse ten characters," or improvised verses.

Thirty-Six Trades

"Thirty-Six Trades" is a dance performed by 38 males. Among them, 36 people portray the characteristics of 36 trades, holding typical tools of each trade and striking their respective poses. Another 2 male actors each wear a "straw-braid band," heavy face paint, disheveled clothes, straw rope around the waist, barefoot, each beating a large gong, leading the way.

本篇术语 Key Terms

十八狐狸 / Eighteen Foxes (dance) 十八蝴蝶 / Eighteen Butterflies (dance) 十八鲤鱼 / Eighteen Carp (dance) 讨饭莲花/永康莲花 / Begging Lotus/Yongkang Lotus 三十六行 / Thirty-Six Trades (dance) 吹唱班 / Singing troupe 婺剧 / Wuju opera (Jinhua opera)

附录C 方岩旅店情况统计
中文

附录C 方岩旅店情况统计（仅限于新中国成立之前）

本统计表综合了《永康县志》（1991）、《永康名店名厂》（1995）、《方岩指南》（1925）、方岩明清街示意图（应业湘提供）及村民访谈等来源。房间数目可能会随家庭成员人数的增减而稍有变化。表中"分拍"之意即开分店。

清道光末年至咸丰年间（1821-1861）开业的早期旅店：

程隆兴，创建人程岳标（后传程昭卿、程昭亨），清道光末年（1821-1850年），据程岳标曾孙程里鹏回忆，两层高合院建筑（已坍塌），位于上坑口北侧，老街西侧。

马大和，创建人程茂发的曾祖父，姓马，来自东阳，清咸丰年间（1851-1861年），合院建筑（已烧毁），位于老街南端转弯处，1925年之前被烧毁。

程义丰，创建人程乐镛（1832-1866年），后传三子程昭明，清咸丰年间（1851-1861年），房间数10间，两层高三合院，位于蜈蚣山西麓，老街西侧。

高永兴，清咸丰年间（1851-1861年），仅在《永康县志》被提到，《方岩指南》中未提及，在老街上的位置不详。

清光绪年间（1875-1908）开业的旅店：

程义丰雅记，创建人程昭俊（程乐镛二子），两层高三合院，位于蜈蚣山西麓，老街西侧。

Appendix C: Statistics of Fangyan Inns (Limited to Before the Founding of New China)

This statistical table comprehensively draws from sources including Yongkang County Annals (1991), Yongkang Famous Stores and Factories (1995), Fangyan Guide (1925), Fangyan Ming-Qing Street Map (provided by Ying Yexiang), and villager interviews. Room numbers may vary slightly with changes in family member count. "Fenpai" in the table means opening a branch store.

Early inns opened in the late Daoguang to Xianfeng period (1821-1861):

Cheng Longxing, founded by Cheng Yuebiao (later passed to Cheng Zhaoqing, Cheng Zhaoheng), late Daoguang period (1821-1850), according to recollection of Cheng Yuebiao's great-grandson Cheng Lipeng, two-story courtyard building (collapsed), located north of Shangkengkou, west side of old street.

Ma Dahe, founded by the great-grandfather of Cheng Maofa, surname Ma, from Dongyang, Xianfeng period (1851-1861), courtyard building (burned down), located at the southern end turn of old street, burned before 1925.

Cheng Yifeng, founded by Cheng Leyong (1832-1866), later passed to third son Cheng Zhaoming, Xianfeng period (1851-1861), 10 rooms, two-story three-sided courtyard, located at western foot of Centipede Mountain, west side of old street.

Gao Yongxing, Xianfeng period (1851-1861), only mentioned in Yongkang County Annals, not mentioned in Fangyan Guide, location on old street unknown.

Inns opened during Guangxu period (1875-1908):

Xin Longxing, founded by Cheng Zhaoxiang, Cheng Zhaoye (eldest and third sons of Cheng Yuebiao), early Guangxu period (1875-1882), 14 rooms, three-story courtyard building (burned in recent years), located at Xiahukeng mouth, west side of old street.

Cheng Zhenxing, founded by Cheng Zhaofu (1857-1939), third son of Cheng Lelai in 1939, sixth year of Guangxu (1880), two two-story quadrangles (1880, 1909), one ancestral hall (1924), two-story Western-style building (1924-1942), three-story Western-style building (1942), located north of Xiahukeng mouth, both sides of old street.

Cheng Renchang, founded by Cheng Zhaoye (third son of Cheng Yuebiao), eighth year of Guangxu (1882), three-story courtyard, three-story small three-sided courtyard (1887), two-story street-side building (1920), four-story Western-style building (early 1930s), located at western foot of Lion Mountain, west side of middle old street.

Cheng Yifeng Yaji, founded by Cheng Zhaojun (second son of Cheng Leyong), two-story three-sided courtyard, located at western foot of Centipede Mountain, west side of old street.

Inns opened in early Republican period (before 1925):

Cheng Chengchang, founded by Cheng Zhaofeng (1855-1928, second son of Cheng Lelai), early Republican period (before 1925), 15 rooms, two-story quadrangle, located at Xiahukeng mouth, east side of old street.

Cheng Longchang, founded by Cheng Zhaogong (sixth son of Cheng Lelai), early Republican period (before 1925), 10 rooms, two-story quadrangle, located in Chenglu village, west side of old street.

Cheng Qianchang, founded by Cheng Dexi, early Republican period (before 1925), 4 rooms, two-story quadrangle, located in Chenglu village, west side of old street.

Cheng Baochang, founded by Cheng Yangxun, early Republican period (before 1925), 5 rooms, two-story three-sided courtyard, located south of Songmen bridge, west side of old street.

Cheng Dachang, founded by Cheng Jinfang, early Republican period (before 1925), 10 rooms, three-story quadrangle, located at Sanjiantou, west side of old street.

Cheng Shunxing, early Republican period (before 1925), two-story street-side building, located at Sanjiantou, west side of old street.

Cheng Meiyi, founded by Lantou Shu, early Republican period (before 1925), 6 rooms, two-story street-side irregular courtyard building, located south of Shangkengkou, east side of old street.

Cheng Shunchang Xinji, Cheng Xinchang, Cheng Baoyuan Xiaoji, Cheng Baoyuan Renji, Cheng Yongquan: early Republican period (before 1925), only mentioned in Fangyan Guide, location and other details unknown.

后记
