后记
中文

后记

北京奥运会前的那个夏天，当我正在清华东门外租的板楼里埋头写作的时候，收到了来自美国爱荷华州立大学（Iowa State University）建筑系的邀请信，聘请我去做讲师。兴奋之余，不免有许多忧虑。由于美国大学开学早，况且我还要顾及找房子、买家具等事宜，我需要在七月底离开北京，这比我原定完成书稿的计划至少早了两个月。我向李秋香、陈志华老师解释了情况，并表明我希望能在美国继续完成书稿的意愿，他们表示非常理解和支持。李老师依旧用她一向和善的口气说，"要注意身体，工作也要抓紧"。

陈老师只是在电话里语重心长地嘱咐，"想把工作完成是好，但是不要拖"。就这样，在数以万计的各国运动员、记者以及游客纷纷降落在崭新的T3航站楼的那个星期，我匆匆地离开了。

在美国教书的工作远远要比我想象得辛苦，也极具挑战性。整个秋季学期，我觉得自己每天都如同打仗一般。晚上一到家，赖在沙发上就什么都不想做了。由于周末经常还要给十几个学生改论文，所以很少能拿出时间来继续我在清华乡土组未完成的工作。惭愧地说，我辜负了李老师和陈老师的嘱托，把这件事拖了下来。去年年底之前，李老师来了一封信，客气地问工作进展如何。我无言以对，于是下定决心无论如何要把这件事尽快做完、做好，也好给无数信任我的人一个交代。转眼间，春季学期几近尾声，虽然外面时常还会下雪，但是至少我心中的这块石头逐渐落了下来，可以翘首企盼姗姗来迟的春天了。

回想在清华的那些日日夜夜，包括去方岩测绘调研、回来整理以及日后写作的整个过程，心中依旧会感慨万千。这其中我得到了许许多多来自各个层面的帮助，而这项工作也只有在整个研究团队的共同努力和协作下，才能最终完成。在这个过程中，我不但收获颇丰，而且受益匪浅。

在方岩的日子里，当地村民对我们的工作给予了极大的支持和关照，让我深刻感受到了人与人之间以心换心的历程。当那些朴实的村民看到我和我的同事们不论刮风下雨、一趟一趟地从老街上走过，当我一边专心聆听、一边认真记笔记，当我一次又一次的登门拜访，当我把冲洗好的照片送到他们手里的时候，他们对我的信任也随之增加。于是他们会把压在箱子底的"宝贝"翻出来给我们看，他们会把刚刚出锅的、准备孝敬祖宗的"金团"拿给我们吃，他们会把自家院里结的橘子塞到我们的包里，他们会讲更多的故事给我们听。

写到这里，不免要说一些"题外话"。印度著名建筑师查尔斯·柯里亚（Charles Correa）曾经说，"如果我们留意一下人道主义者和环境主义者当前所关心的主要问题，例如生态系统的平衡，垃圾的回收和再利用，公众的参与，适宜的生活模式，本土技术的应用等，我们发现亚洲人民已经开始这样做了"。我想他这里指的是亚洲人民以及他们所居住的没有建筑师的建筑。

在我从事研究和写作工作的这两年间，世界性的气候变化以及经济危机无疑给世界各国决策者、各种经济实体乃至每一个人前所未有的挑战，同时也迫使世界各地的建筑师们更加关注如何用新的建筑材料及建筑技术来缓解环境与能源的问题。由此，我想借柯里亚的话说下去：乡土建筑留给我们的不仅仅是悠久的历史和灿烂的文化，更应该超越雕梁画栋与户牖之美，乡土建筑中的许多原则和做法足以给二十一世纪的建筑师们解决环境、能源以及资金问题一些启示，甚至是设计的灵感。

虽然终于写到了结尾，但是我想这本书、这件事，乃至这项工作对我来说仅仅是个开始。陈志华老师曾经感慨：

——乡土文化是中华民族文化中还没有充分开发的宝藏，没有乡土文化的中国文化是残缺不全的，不研究乡土文化就不能真正了解我们这个民族。

——乡土建筑是乡土生活的舞台和物质环境，它是乡土文化最普遍存在的、信息含量最大的组成部分……乡土建筑研究是乡土文化系统研究的基础。

——没有乡土建筑的中国建筑史也是残缺不全的。

我没有陈老师那令人钦佩的远见卓识，更没有资深的阅历来讲出这样深刻的话给别人听；我有的，只是我点点滴滴的真实感受。余秋雨先生曾经感慨，"我居留的大城市里有很多贮存古籍的图书馆，讲授古文化的大学，而中国文化的真实步履却落在这山重水复、莽莽苍苍的大地上"。所以，我很庆幸，我能有机会把自己融入乡土文化这"莽莽苍苍的大地上"；能连续一个星期工作在黄河岸边，感受它的伟大；能坐在有几百年历史的塘边屋前，听年过古稀的老人讲他小时候的故事；能爬进大屋顶，给近人高的斗拱掸掸灰，和它合个影；能怀揣手炉，喝着略带甜涩的井水沏的新茶，翻看着手抄的家谱……

我想，方岩我会再去。我要在"重阳初八夜"那晚和成百上千的人一起上方岩祭拜"胡公大帝"；我也要在正月十四那天和老街上的程氏后人一起迎龙灯；我还要争取参与更多类似的研究工作，让更多的人、特别是年轻人重视和参与到乡土研究工作中来；我更要把我的经历和研究成果写成英文，把中国的乡土建筑推向全世界！

曾经给我们提供帮助的人很多，在此感谢永康市博物馆陈景馆长给予的各方面支持与帮助；方岩镇政府提供的大力协助；永康市的胡春鸿与方岩镇的程伯敦两位学者，以及王岳阳摄影家向我提供了一些他们收集的地方资料；广慈寺前任住持僧允明与现任住持释明修给我讲述了许多广慈寺的重要历史。还要感谢那些住在老街上的程氏后人，程崇舜、程镇康、程茂发、程振锋、程高明、程明星、程圣言、程人龙、程水保、倒插门到程家的应业湘、嫁到老街上的白珑珑和骆巧燕，还有许许多多没来得及记下名字的热心人。

陈志华为岩下老街研究课题总策划，李秋香为项目负责人及测绘主持人，赵巍撰写文字，由陈志华、李秋香指导修改。方岩老街的研究是一项集体共同完成的研究项目，研究组于2007年9月和12月先后两次到方岩进行测绘和调研，参与测绘及调研的人有：李秋香、赵巍、毛葛、孙娜，及建筑学院五年级的林永明、陈华。测绘图主要由赵巍、孙娜、毛葛、林永明和陈华完成，陈金花参加了部分测绘图的绘制修改。随后赵巍又先后两次前往方岩老街，进行调研资料的核实及补充收集。书中手绘插图由赵巍绘制，没有另外注明拍摄者的照片均由赵巍拍摄。

赵巍

2009年4月

艾姆斯（Ames），爱荷华（Iowa）

美国

English

Afterword

In the summer before the Beijing Olympics, while I was buried in writing in a rented apartment building outside the east gate of Tsinghua University, I received an invitation letter from the Department of Architecture at Iowa State University in the United States, hiring me as a lecturer. Beyond excitement, there was inevitably much anxiety.

Since American universities start early, and I also had to take care of finding housing, buying furniture, and other matters, I needed to leave Beijing by the end of July, which was at least two months earlier than my original plan to complete the manuscript.

I explained the situation to Professors Li Qiuxiang and Chen Zhihua, and expressed my wish to continue completing the manuscript in the United States; they expressed great understanding and support.

Professor Li, as always in her gentle tone, said, "Take care of your health, and keep up the work." Professor Chen only earnestly instructed over the phone, "It's good to want to finish the work, but don't delay it." Thus, in the week when tens of thousands of athletes, journalists, and tourists from various countries landed at the brand-new Terminal 3, I left in a hurry.

Teaching in the United States was far more arduous and challenging than I had imagined. Throughout the fall semester, I felt like I was fighting a battle every day. Once home in the evening, I would collapse on the sofa and not want to do anything.

Since weekends often still involved grading papers for over a dozen students, I rarely found time to continue the unfinished work of the Tsinghua vernacular architecture Group. Ashamed to say, I failed to live up to the trust of Professors Li and Chen, and let this matter drag on.

Before the end of last year, Professor Li sent a letter, politely asking about the progress of the work. I had nothing to say in reply, so I made up my mind to finish this matter as soon as possible and do it well, to give an account to the countless people who trusted me.

In the blink of an eye, the spring semester is nearly over; although it still snows outside from time to time, at least the stone in my heart has gradually lifted, and I can look forward to the long-awaited spring.

Looking back on those days and nights at Tsinghua, including the entire process of going to Fangyan for surveying and research, returning to organize, and later writing, my heart still fills with countless emotions.

During this process, I received help from many levels, and this work could only be finally completed through the joint efforts and collaboration of the entire research team. In this process, I not only gained much but also benefited greatly.

During my days in Fangyan, local villagers gave tremendous support and care to our work, letting me deeply experience the process of heart-to-heart exchange between people.

When those simple villagers saw my colleagues and me walking through the old street time and again regardless of wind or rain, when I listened attentively while taking careful notes, when I visited door to door again and again, when I delivered developed photos into their hands, their trust in me grew accordingly.

So they would dig out the "treasures" pressed at the bottom of their boxes to show us, they would bring us the freshly cooked "golden dumplings" prepared to honor their ancestors, they would stuff oranges from their own courtyards into our bags, and they would tell us more stories.

Writing to this point, I cannot help but say some "digressions." The famous Indian architect Charles Correa once said, "If we look at all the major concerns of humanists and environmentalists today: balanced eco-systems, recycling of products, people's participation, appropriate life-styles, indigenous technology, etc, we find the people of Asia already have it all." I think what he refers to here is the Asian people and the architect-less architecture they inhabit.

During the two years I engaged in research and writing, global climate change and the economic crisis undoubtedly presented unprecedented challenges to policymakers, economic entities, and every individual around the world, while also forcing architects everywhere to pay more attention to how to use new building materials and technologies to alleviate environmental and energy problems.

Thus, I would like to continue in Correa's words: what vernacular architecture leaves us is not only long history and splendid culture, but should also transcend the beauty of carved beams and painted rafters and doors and windows; many principles and practices in vernacular architecture are sufficient to give twenty-first-century architects some inspiration, even design inspiration, for solving environmental, energy, and funding problems.

Although I have finally reached the end, I think this book, this matter, and even this work are only a beginning for me. Professor Chen Zhihua once lamented:

— vernacular culture is a treasure in Chinese national culture that has not been fully developed; Chinese culture without vernacular culture is incomplete, and without studying vernacular culture one cannot truly understand our nation.

— vernacular architecture is the stage and material environment of vernacular life; it is the most ubiquitous and information-rich component of vernacular culture... Research on vernacular architecture is the foundation of systematic research on vernacular culture.

— The history of Chinese architecture without vernacular architecture is also incomplete.

I do not have Professor Chen's admirable foresight, let alone the senior experience to speak such profound words to others; what I have is only my bit-by-bit true feelings. Mr.

Yu Qiuyu once lamented, "In the big cities where I reside, there are many libraries storing ancient books and universities teaching ancient culture, but the true footsteps of Chinese culture fall on this land of mountains upon mountains and vast, boundless wilderness." So I am very fortunate to have had the opportunity to immerse myself in this "vast, boundless wilderness" of vernacular culture; to work for a week straight on the banks of the Yellow River, feeling its greatness; to sit in front of a centuries-old house by a pond, listening to an over-seventy elder tell stories of his childhood; to climb into a large roof, dust off a nearly human-height dougong bracket set, and take a photo with it; to hold a hand warmer, drinking slightly sweet-bitter new tea brewed with well water, while flipping through hand-copied genealogy...

I think I will go to Fangyan again.

I want to go up Fangyan with hundreds or thousands of people on the "eighth night of Chongyang" to worship the "Hu Gong Great Emperor"; I also want to welcome the dragon lantern with the Cheng descendants on the old street on the fourteenth day of the first lunar month; I will strive to participate in more similar research work, letting more people, especially young people, value and participate in vernacular research; I will even write my experiences and research results in English, pushing Chinese vernacular architecture to the whole world!

Many people have provided us with help.

Here I thank Curator Chen Jing of Yongkang City Museum for all-round support and assistance; the great assistance provided by Fangyan Town Government; scholars Hu Chunhong from Yongkang City and Cheng Bodun from Fangyan Town, as well as photographer Wang Yueyang, for providing some local materials they collected; former abbot Monk Yunming and current abbot Shi Mingxiu of Guangci temple, for recounting much important history of Guangci temple.

I also thank the Cheng descendants living on the old street: Cheng Chongshun, Cheng Zhenkang, Cheng Maofa, Cheng Zhenfeng, Cheng Gaoming, Cheng Mingxing, Cheng Shengyan, Cheng Renlong, Cheng Shuibao, Ying Yexiang who married into the Cheng family, Bai Longlong and Luo qiaoyan who married into the old street, and countless warm-hearted people whose names I did not have time to record.

Chen Zhihua was the chief planner of the Yanxia Old Street research project; Li Qiuxiang was the project leader and surveying host; Zhao Wei wrote the text, guided and revised by Chen Zhihua and Li Qiuxiang. The research on Fangyan Old Street is a collectively completed research project.

The research team went to Fangyan twice for surveying and research in September and December 2007; those participating in surveying and research were: Li Qiuxiang, Zhao Wei, Mao Ge, Sun Na, and fifth-year architecture students Lin Yongming and Chen Hua.

Survey drawings were mainly completed by Zhao Wei, Sun Na, Mao Ge, Lin Yongming, and Chen Hua; Chen Jinhua participated in the drawing and revision of some survey drawings. Subsequently, Zhao Wei went to Fangyan Old Street twice more for verification and supplementary collection of research materials.

Hand-drawn illustrations in the book were drawn by Zhao Wei; photos without separately noted photographers were all taken by Zhao Wei.

Zhao Wei

April 2009

Ames, Iowa

United States
